# Daily Summary: 2025-05-31

### Scalable Complexity Control Facilitates Reasoning Ability of LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23013v1)
- **Authors**: Liangkai Hang, Junjie Yao, Zhiwei Bai, Tianyi Chen, Yang Chen, Rongjie Diao, Hezhou Li, Pengxiao Lin, Zhiwei Wang, Cheng Xu, Zhongwang Zhang, Zhangchen Zhou, Zhiyu Li, Zehao Lin, Kai Chen, Feiyu Xiong, Yaoyu Zhang, Weinan E, Hongkang Yang, Zhi-Qin John Xu
- **Abstract**: The reasoning ability of large language models (LLMs) has been rapidly advancing in recent years, attracting interest in more fundamental approaches that can reliably enhance their generalizability. This work demonstrates that model complexity control, conveniently implementable by adjusting the initialization rate and weight decay coefficient, improves the scaling law of LLMs consistently over varying model sizes and data sizes. This gain is further illustrated by comparing the benchmark performance of 2.4B models pretrained on 1T tokens with different complexity hyperparameters. Instead of fixing the initialization std, we found that a constant initialization rate (the exponent of std) enables the scaling law to descend faster in both model and data sizes. These results indicate that complexity control is a promising direction for the continual advancement of LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the idea of controlling the complexity of Large Language Models (LLMs) to enhance their reasoning abilities. The authors propose that adjusting the parameter initialization rate and weight decay coefficient can be used to regulate model complexity.  They demonstrate that models trained with controlled complexity (specifically, a smaller initialization scale) show improved scaling laws with both model size and data size. They validate their claims through experiments, comparing the performance of 2.4B models trained on 1T tokens with different complexity hyperparameters across a range of benchmarks. The results suggest that lowering the initialization rate allows the models to capture more sophisticated semantic reasoning and improve generalization. Further analysis examines the impact of the proposed techniques on the embedding space and attention mechanisms, suggesting that complexity control enables models to focus on fundamental relationships between tokens, leading to stronger generalization.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** While the idea of using initialization and weight decay as regularization techniques is well-established in deep learning, the *systematic exploration and demonstration of its impact on the reasoning ability and scaling laws of Large Language Models is novel*. Existing work primarily focused on training stability or synthetic tasks, whereas this paper focuses on downstream benchmarks and analyzes underlying mechanisms for LLMs trained on natural language. The paper's emphasis on using the initialization *rate* (the exponent of std as a function of network width) as a key tuning parameter, rather than the standard deviation of initialization itself, is also a potentially valuable insight. However, the theoretical analysis is somewhat heuristic and could be more rigorously developed.

*   **Significance:** The paper makes a significant contribution by linking model complexity control directly to reasoning ability in LLMs. The observed improvements in scaling laws are particularly important as they suggest a more efficient path to developing better LLMs. The insights into the embedding space and attention mechanisms are also valuable for understanding how complexity control affects model behavior. The practical guidance offered for large-scale model pre-training could have a tangible impact on the field.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Strong Empirical Validation:** The paper presents extensive experimental results on various benchmarks, demonstrating the benefits of complexity control. The data is thoroughly presented.
    *   **Clear and Well-Written:** The paper is generally well-written and easy to follow, with clear explanations of the proposed methods and results.
    *   **Insightful Analysis:** The analysis of the embedding space and attention mechanisms provides valuable insights into the impact of complexity control.
    *   **Practical Guidance:** The paper offers practical guidance for pre-training LLMs, including recommendations for initialization strategies and weight decay coefficients.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Heuristic Theoretical Analysis:** The theoretical analysis is somewhat heuristic and could be more rigorously developed. It relies on specific assumptions that may not hold in all cases.
    *   **Limited Exploration of other Complexity Control Methods:** The paper primarily focuses on initialization and weight decay. Other complexity control methods, such as pruning or knowledge distillation, could have been explored.
    *   **Over-Reliance on SlimPajama Dataset for 0.9B models:** The negative results when using the SlimPajama dataset undermine the robustness of the results. Ideally, all models would be trained on the same high-quality dataset to more fairly compare the scaling laws with different complexity controls.
    *   **Training Stability Issues:** Mentioning training instability raises questions. While the authors describe mitigation strategies, a more detailed analysis of the causes of instability would be valuable.
    *   **The ablation study in Appendix E is difficult to interpret due to the large range of variance between tasks.** A heatmap with a higher gradient would more clearly show the relationships across y and lambda.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of future LLMs by highlighting the importance of complexity control and providing practical guidance for implementing it. The improved scaling laws could lead to more efficient and effective training of LLMs.

**Score:** 7

**Justification:**

The paper's novelty lies in systematically demonstrating the importance of complexity control (through initialization *rate* and weight decay) on the *reasoning ability and scaling laws* of LLMs trained on natural language data, and this is backed by strong empirical validation. However, the heuristic theoretical analysis, dependence on a less-than-ideal SlimPajama dataset in parts, and potentially limited scope of complexity control methods explored slightly reduce the overall impact. The practical guidance for pre-training and the insights into model behavior are valuable, but the need for more rigorous theoretical grounding and consistent datasets across models limits the score. Overall, the contribution is more than incrementally better.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Detecting Stealthy Backdoor Samples based on Intra-class Distance for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23015v1)
- **Authors**: Jinwen Chen, Hainan Zhang, Fei Sun, Qinnan Zhang, Sijia Wen, Ziwei Wang, Zhiming Zheng
- **Abstract**: Fine-tuning LLMs with datasets containing stealthy backdoors from publishers poses security risks to downstream applications. Mainstream detection methods either identify poisoned samples by analyzing the prediction probability of poisoned classification models or rely on the rewriting model to eliminate the stealthy triggers. However, the former cannot be applied to generation tasks, while the latter may degrade generation performance and introduce new triggers. Therefore, efficiently eliminating stealthy poisoned samples for LLMs remains an urgent problem. We observe that after applying TF-IDF clustering to the sample response, there are notable differences in the intra-class distances between clean and poisoned samples. Poisoned samples tend to cluster closely because of their specific malicious outputs, whereas clean samples are more scattered due to their more varied responses. Thus, in this paper, we propose a stealthy backdoor sample detection method based on Reference-Filtration and Tfidf-Clustering mechanisms (RFTC). Specifically, we first compare the sample response with the reference model's outputs and consider the sample suspicious if there's a significant discrepancy. And then we perform TF-IDF clustering on these suspicious samples to identify the true poisoned samples based on the intra-class distance. Experiments on two machine translation datasets and one QA dataset demonstrate that RFTC outperforms baselines in backdoor detection and model performance. Further analysis of different reference models also confirms the effectiveness of our Reference-Filtration.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel method called RFTC (Reference-Filtration and Tfidf-Clustering) for detecting stealthy backdoor samples in datasets used to fine-tune Large Language Models (LLMs).  RFTC first filters suspicious samples by comparing their responses to a reference model's output, and then uses TF-IDF clustering to identify true poisoned samples based on intra-class distance.  The method leverages the observation that poisoned samples tend to cluster more tightly due to specific malicious outputs, while clean samples are more scattered.  Experiments on machine translation and QA datasets demonstrate that RFTC achieves superior backdoor detection rates and maintains model performance, with lower attack success rates and computational complexity than baseline methods. Further analysis of different reference models also confirms the effectiveness of the Reference-Filtration component.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper's novelty lies in combining reference model filtration with TF-IDF clustering based on intra-class distance analysis.  While individual components like reference models and TF-IDF clustering are not new, their integration for backdoor *detection* in *generation* tasks, specifically targeting *stealthy* backdoors, offers a distinctive approach. It's an interesting twist to move beyond input-based trigger detection and focus on output characteristics.

*   **Significance:**  The problem of backdoor attacks in LLMs is undeniably significant, posing security risks to downstream applications. Current backdoor detection methods often focus on classification tasks or rely on paraphrasing, which can degrade generation performance or introduce new triggers. Thus, a method that efficiently eliminates stealthy poisoned samples without significant performance degradation is valuable.  The experimental results, demonstrating improved detection rates and preservation of model performance compared to baselines, support the significance of the proposed approach.  The method's ability to handle stealthy triggers (combination and syntactic) further enhances its practical relevance.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The method is effective in generation tasks, addressing a gap in existing research that primarily focuses on classification.
    *   RFTC addresses stealthy triggers, which are more challenging to detect.
    *   The paper provides solid empirical evidence to support its claims, with comparisons to strong baselines across multiple datasets.
    *   The ablation studies clearly demonstrate the contribution of each component (Reference-Filtration and Tfidf-Clustering).
    *   The analysis of different reference models adds valuable insights into the method's robustness.
    *   Lower computational complexity compared to some baselines (like BERTScore) makes it more practical.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The reliance on a reference model, while offering advantages, introduces a dependency. The paper argues finding one isn't hard but obtaining an appropriate reference model (not too weak, not too strong, and not already backdoored), still requires effort. It depends on the availability of task-specific models.
    *   While experiments are conducted on different datasets, they are limited to machine translation and QA.  Generalizability to other generation tasks (e.g., summarization, dialogue) needs further validation.
    *   The method's reliance on TF-IDF clustering, while efficient, might not capture complex semantic relationships in the output text. More advanced clustering techniques could potentially improve detection accuracy, at the cost of efficiency.
    *   The tuning of the hyperparameter *cs* still exists, requiring potentially costly tuning for each dataset.
    *   The current defense is effective if the backdoors in the reference models are not the same. The paper could benefit from a more detailed exploration of the impact of *similar* backdoors in the reference model on the performance.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to influence the field by providing a practical and effective method for mitigating backdoor risks in LLMs. The focus on stealthy triggers and generation tasks makes it particularly relevant to current challenges in the field. Other researchers may build upon this work by exploring different reference models, clustering techniques, or evaluation datasets.

**Overall:**

The paper presents a well-motivated, technically sound, and empirically validated approach to a significant problem. While the method has some limitations related to reference model dependency and generalizability, the overall contribution is substantial. The method is efficient and achieves better performance than existing methods. It specifically addresses stealthy backdoors in the generation setting.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Sensitivity of DC Network Representation for GIC Analysis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23016v1)
- **Authors**: Aniruddh Mishra, Arthur K. Barnes, Jose E. Tabarez, Adam Mate
- **Abstract**: Geomagnetic disturbances are a threat to the reliability and security of our national critical energy infrastructures. These events specifically result in geomagnetically induced currents, which can cause damage to transformers due to magnetic saturation. In order to mitigate these effects, blocker devices must be placed in optimal locations. Finding this placement requires a dc representation of the ac transmission lines, which this paper discusses. Different decisions in this process, including the method of representing the blocking devices, result in significant variations to the power loss calculations. To analyze these effects, we conclude the paper by comparing the losses on a sample network with different modeling implementations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

This paper investigates the sensitivity of DC network representations used for Geomagnetically Induced Current (GIC) analysis. It explores how different modeling choices, particularly regarding the representation of blocking devices (series capacitors, transformer neutral blockers, and substation ground approximations), affect power loss calculations. The study compares power losses on a small test network using various modeling implementations under both uniform and non-uniform electric field conditions, utilizing PowerModelsGMD.jl for analysis. The key finding is that uniform electric field assumptions can mask system vulnerabilities, and substation blocking approximations can overestimate power loss in non-uniform fields.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper addresses a relevant and under-explored aspect of GIC analysis: the sensitivity of results to DC network modeling choices. While previous works have investigated the impact of network parameters like line resistance on GIC flow, this paper focuses on the representational choices made during DC network construction, particularly concerning blockers. This focus offers a novel perspective, although the modeling techniques themselves aren't entirely new.

*   **Significance:** Understanding the impact of different modeling assumptions is crucial for accurate GIC risk assessment and effective mitigation strategies. The paper highlights the potential pitfalls of relying on simplified assumptions (like uniform electric fields) and demonstrates that the specific blocker representation can influence power loss estimates, which is directly related to the effectiveness of placement optimization. This has practical implications for grid operators and researchers developing GIC mitigation techniques.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem definition and focused research question.
    *   Systematic comparison of different modeling approaches.
    *   Use of a publicly available tool (PowerModelsGMD.jl) enhances reproducibility.
    *   Demonstrates the limitations of uniform electric field assumptions and the impact of substation blocker approximations.
    *   Provides a specific test case that can be extended in future works

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Limited scope: The analysis is performed on a relatively small, custom-designed network.  Extending the analysis to larger, more realistic power system models would significantly strengthen the findings.
    *   The case study is designed such that the substation model approximation of a neutral blocker can be shown to give a noticeably different result. However, this is achieved by limiting the test network to having primarily autotransformers. The results might not be generalizable to the entire bulk electric system, which also contains a number of two-winding transformers.
    *   The reliance on PowerWorld Simulator for neutral blocker modeling presents a minor limitation. The complete end-to-end workflow should ideally be reproducible within a single tool, such as an updated version of PowerModelsGMD.jl.
    *   While the paper demonstrates the sensitivity of power loss calculations, it doesn't fully explore the consequences for blocker placement optimization algorithms. How do these modeling choices affect the *optimal* placement of blockers, and what is the cost of using a sub-optimal representation?
    *   The paper only offers general advice towards the selection of the uniform electric field (using NERC TPL-007.1 and TPL-007.2.). There may be more robust options that would lessen the effect of the induced voltage on a system.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence GIC modeling practices by encouraging researchers and practitioners to:

    *   Critically evaluate their modeling assumptions.
    *   Consider the impact of blocker representation on power loss calculations.
    *   Avoid over-reliance on uniform electric field assumptions.
    *   Develop more accurate and efficient methods for modeling GICs in non-uniform electric fields.

*   **Overall Assessment:** The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field by highlighting the sensitivity of GIC analysis to DC network modeling choices. While the scope of the case study is limited, the findings have practical implications for GIC risk assessment and mitigation. The demonstration that the choice of device placement has implications for the electric grid is noteworthy.

**Score: 6.5**

**Rationale:** The paper is novel in its focused examination of DC network modeling assumptions, provides a valuable message for those working in the field, and is technically sound. However, its impact is somewhat limited by the small scale of the case study network and the lack of a direct connection to blocker placement optimization. A higher score would be warranted if the study had included a more comprehensive analysis of a larger system and demonstrated the impact of modeling choices on the *optimal* mitigation strategies. Additionally, a stronger case study that is less dependent on autotransformers would make the results more impactful.

- **Classification**: eess.SY
- **Score**: 6/10

### Stairway to Success: Zero-Shot Floor-Aware Object-Goal Navigation via LLM-Driven Coarse-to-Fine Exploration
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23019v1)
- **Authors**: Zeying Gong, Rong Li, Tianshuai Hu, Ronghe Qiu, Lingdong Kong, Lingfeng Zhang, Yiyi Ding, Leying Zhang, Junwei Liang
- **Abstract**: Object-Goal Navigation (OGN) remains challenging in real-world, multi-floor environments and under open-vocabulary object descriptions. We observe that most episodes in widely used benchmarks such as HM3D and MP3D involve multi-floor buildings, with many requiring explicit floor transitions. However, existing methods are often limited to single-floor settings or predefined object categories. To address these limitations, we tackle two key challenges: (1) efficient cross-level planning and (2) zero-shot object-goal navigation (ZS-OGN), where agents must interpret novel object descriptions without prior exposure. We propose ASCENT, a framework that combines a Multi-Floor Spatial Abstraction module for hierarchical semantic mapping and a Coarse-to-Fine Frontier Reasoning module leveraging Large Language Models (LLMs) for context-aware exploration, without requiring additional training on new object semantics or locomotion data. Our method outperforms state-of-the-art ZS-OGN approaches on HM3D and MP3D benchmarks while enabling efficient multi-floor navigation. We further validate its practicality through real-world deployment on a quadruped robot, achieving successful object exploration across unseen floors.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and a rigorous evaluation of the provided paper.

**Summary**

The paper introduces ASCENT, a framework for zero-shot floor-aware object-goal navigation (ZS-OGN). The framework addresses the challenges of navigating multi-floor environments with open-vocabulary object descriptions without requiring task-specific training.  ASCENT combines a Multi-Floor Spatial Abstraction module for building hierarchical semantic maps with a Coarse-to-Fine Frontier Reasoning module that leverages Large Language Models (LLMs) to prioritize exploration based on context and prior knowledge.  The method improves upon prior art by incorporating knowledge of floor layouts and transitioning adaptively between floors.  The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of ASCENT on HM3D and MP3D benchmarks, showcasing improvements in success rate and path efficiency, and through a sim-to-real deployment using a quadruped robot.

**Rigorous and Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:**

    *   The explicit handling of multi-floor navigation in a zero-shot setting is a valuable contribution. While previous methods have touched on this, the ASCENT framework presents a more unified approach by combining spatial abstraction and LLM-driven reasoning.
    *   The Coarse-to-Fine Frontier Reasoning module using LLMs for context-aware exploration is a good improvement.  The semantic similarity based value map is a good idea that avoids calling the LLM too often.
    *   The Multi-Floor Spatial Abstraction module addresses a clear issue in embodied navigation but vision-based floor detection may be challenging in real world.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The results on HM3D and MP3D show that ASCENT provides a more efficient and interpretable approach to zero-shot object navigation.
    *   The real-world deployment validates the practical applicability of the framework.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper tackles a relevant and challenging problem in embodied AI, bridging the gap between single-floor and multi-floor navigation.
    *   The framework integrates multiple components (spatial abstraction, LLM reasoning, prior knowledge) into a cohesive system.
    *   The sim-to-real validation adds significant value by demonstrating the robustness of the approach in real-world settings.
    *   Ablation studies offer useful insights into the contribution of each component, allowing for future research to build upon the work.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The reliance on prior knowledge, even though it is used in zero-shot settings, might limit its generality across drastically different environments.
    *   Although vision-based floor awareness is a good idea, it is hard to tell if this is robust enough in real world.
    *   While the sim-to-real results are encouraging, only a few trials were presented.
    *   The paper lacks the comparison with RIM, PIRLNav and XGX on MP3D dataset.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The paper sets a higher standard for zero-shot object navigation by addressing multi-floor environments and leveraging LLMs.
    *   The insights from the component ablation studies could inform future research on embodied AI and LLM-based robotics.
    *   The work encourages further investigation of real-world deployment of embodied AI systems.

*   **Rigorous Rationale:** The ASCENT framework provides a solid framework to overcome difficulties in existing methods by carefully combining spatial abstraction, prior knowledge, and LLM. Although there are some difficulties in real world and lack of some experiment settings, the advantages in addressing the multi-floor environment is clearly shown.

Score: 7

### AgentAlign: Navigating Safety Alignment in the Shift from Informative to Agentic Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23020v1)
- **Authors**: Jinchuan Zhang, Lu Yin, Yan Zhou, Songlin Hu
- **Abstract**: The acquisition of agentic capabilities has transformed LLMs from "knowledge providers" to "action executors", a trend that while expanding LLMs' capability boundaries, significantly increases their susceptibility to malicious use. Previous work has shown that current LLM-based agents execute numerous malicious tasks even without being attacked, indicating a deficiency in agentic use safety alignment during the post-training phase. To address this gap, we propose AgentAlign, a novel framework that leverages abstract behavior chains as a medium for safety alignment data synthesis. By instantiating these behavior chains in simulated environments with diverse tool instances, our framework enables the generation of highly authentic and executable instructions while capturing complex multi-step dynamics. The framework further ensures model utility by proportionally synthesizing benign instructions through non-malicious interpretations of behavior chains, precisely calibrating the boundary between helpfulness and harmlessness. Evaluation results on AgentHarm demonstrate that fine-tuning three families of open-source models using our method substantially improves their safety (35.8% to 79.5% improvement) while minimally impacting or even positively enhancing their helpfulness, outperforming various prompting methods. The dataset and code have both been open-sourced.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces AgentAlign, a framework designed to improve the safety alignment of large language model (LLM) agents.  The central argument is that current safety alignment techniques are insufficient to address the risks posed by agentic LLMs, which can perform actions in the real world. AgentAlign addresses this by: 1) creating abstract behavior chains that represent common patterns in harmful activities, 2) instantiating these chains in simulated environments with diverse tool instances to generate realistic instructions, and 3) synthesizing both harmful and benign instructions to balance safety and utility. The authors demonstrate that fine-tuning open-source LLMs with data generated by AgentAlign significantly improves their safety performance on the AgentHarm benchmark while maintaining or improving their helpfulness.  The framework leverages prompt engineering and quality control mechanisms to ensure high-quality alignment data.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its holistic approach to agent safety alignment using abstract behavior chains and simulated environments. While existing works have explored safety alignment for LLMs, this paper focuses specifically on agentic LLMs and the unique challenges they present. The idea of using abstract behavior chains to guide instruction generation, ensuring executability and authenticity is a key contribution. The explicit focus on generating diverse, high-quality safety alignment data tailored for agentic capabilities sets this work apart from previous efforts. Although, the use of simulated environments and prompt engineering are not novel in isolation, their combination and application in creating AgentAlign is.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from the increasing adoption of LLM agents in various applications, and the associated risks of misuse. Improving the safety of these agents is crucial for their responsible deployment. The presented results on AgentHarm, showing substantial safety improvements across multiple LLM families, suggest the potential of AgentAlign as a practical tool for mitigating risks. The release of the dataset and code contributes to the community effort in improving agent safety. The demonstration that safety alignment can be achieved with minimal impact on helpfulness (or even improved helpfulness) is particularly significant.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The methodology is well-defined and the paper clearly explains each component of the AgentAlign framework.
    *   The use of simulated environments addresses the challenge of generating realistic and executable instructions, which is a known issue in safety alignment.
    *   The framework addresses the crucial balance between safety and utility by generating both harmful and benign instructions.
    *   The experimental results are compelling and demonstrate the effectiveness of AgentAlign across multiple LLM families.
    *   The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the contribution of each component of the framework.
    *   The human evaluation and the analysis of the tool implementation contribute to the quality and transparency of the work.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The simulated environment, while realistic, may not fully capture the complexities of real-world interactions. This could limit the generalizability of the results.  While the authors addressed this, it remains a concern.
    *   The specific choice of tools and categories within the simulated environment might influence the generated instructions and the effectiveness of the alignment.
    *   The reliance on LLMs for semantic validation introduces a potential bias, depending on the LLMs selected.
    *   The experiments do not compare against every existing safety training technique, limiting the assessment of relative performance.

*   **Potential Influence:** AgentAlign has the potential to influence the field of LLM safety by:

    *   Providing a practical framework for aligning agentic LLMs.
    *   Inspiring further research into the use of abstract behavior chains and simulated environments for safety alignment.
    *   Highlighting the importance of balancing safety and utility in LLM agents.
    *   Encouraging the development of more realistic and comprehensive simulated environments for agent evaluation and training.
    *   Serving as a baseline for comparing future safety alignment methods.

*   **Justification for Score:**  Considering the paper's novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses, a score of **7.5** is justified. While prompt engineering, RLHF, and other techniques can improve LLMs, they are not targeted at improving agents' safety behavior. The paper offers a novel and practical approach to agent safety alignment that addresses the unique challenges of agentic LLMs. The demonstrated safety improvements are substantial, and the potential influence on the field is significant. However, the limitations of the simulated environment and the reliance on LLMs for semantic validation detract slightly from the overall impact.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Context Robust Knowledge Editing for Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23026v1)
- **Authors**: Haewon Park, Gyubin Choi, Minjun Kim, Yohan Jo
- **Abstract**: Knowledge editing (KE) methods offer an efficient way to modify knowledge in large language models. Current KE evaluations typically assess editing success by considering only the edited knowledge without any preceding contexts. In real-world applications, however, preceding contexts often trigger the retrieval of the original knowledge and undermine the intended edit. To address this issue, we develop CHED -- a benchmark designed to evaluate the context robustness of KE methods. Evaluations on CHED show that they often fail when preceding contexts are present. To mitigate this shortcoming, we introduce CoRE, a KE method designed to strengthen context robustness by minimizing context-sensitive variance in hidden states of the model for edited knowledge. This method not only improves the editing success rate in situations where a preceding context is present but also preserves the overall capabilities of the model. We provide an in-depth analysis of the differing impacts of preceding contexts when introduced as user utterances versus assistant responses, and we dissect attention-score patterns to assess how specific tokens influence editing success.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Context Robust Knowledge Editing for Language Models":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses a critical limitation in current knowledge editing (KE) evaluations, which typically assess editing success in isolation, ignoring the influence of preceding context. The authors argue that in real-world scenarios, preceding context can significantly impact whether a language model (LLM) retrieves the edited knowledge or reverts to the original, unedited knowledge. To address this, the paper introduces:

1.  **CHED (Contextual Hop Editing Dataset):** A novel benchmark designed to evaluate the context robustness of KE methods by prepending distractive prefix contexts to the edit prompt. These contexts are carefully curated using Wikidata to include semantically relevant entities that tend to receive high attention scores, thus challenging the KE methods.

2.  **CoRE (Context Robust Editing):** A new KE method that aims to improve context robustness by minimizing context-sensitive variance in the model's hidden states for the edited knowledge. CoRE builds upon the locate-then-edit approach and effectively regularizes the model parameters to prevent overfitting to specific prefix contexts.

The paper presents extensive evaluations that demonstrate the limitations of existing KE methods in the presence of context and the effectiveness of CoRE in mitigating this issue. The authors also provide an in-depth analysis of the impact of user vs. assistant context and attention score patterns.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper makes several novel contributions:

*   **Problem Formulation:** Identifying and explicitly addressing the issue of context robustness in knowledge editing is a significant contribution. Previous KE research has largely overlooked this aspect, leading to an overestimation of editing success in real-world scenarios.
*   **CHED Dataset:** The creation of CHED fills a crucial gap in KE evaluation. The dataset's design, incorporating semantically relevant prefix contexts derived from Wikidata, provides a more realistic and challenging benchmark for assessing KE methods.
*   **CoRE Method:** The proposed CoRE method offers a practical approach to improve context robustness. Regularizing the model parameters based on context-sensitive variance in hidden states is a novel technique for mitigating the effects of distractive contexts.
*   **Analysis:** The in-depth analysis of user vs. assistant context and attention score patterns provides valuable insights into the factors that influence editing success.

**Significance:** The paper has the potential to significantly impact the field of knowledge editing in several ways:

*   **Realistic Evaluation:** CHED establishes a new standard for evaluating KE methods, encouraging researchers to consider context robustness as a critical performance metric.
*   **Improved KE Methods:** CoRE provides a promising approach to develop more robust KE methods that can effectively handle distractive contexts.
*   **Practical Applications:** By addressing context robustness, the paper contributes to the development of more reliable and practical KE techniques for real-world applications such as chatbots, virtual assistants, and dialogue systems.

**Strengths:**

*   **Well-Motivated:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing KE evaluation approaches and the importance of context robustness.
*   **Rigorous Evaluation:** The paper presents extensive experimental results that validate the effectiveness of CHED and CoRE.
*   **In-Depth Analysis:** The paper provides valuable insights into the underlying mechanisms that influence editing success through the analysis of user/assistant context and attention scores.
*   **Well-Written and Organized:** The paper is clearly written and well-organized, making it easy to understand the problem, proposed solution, and experimental results.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Dataset Limitations:** CHED is constructed using 1-hop relations from Wikidata, which might not capture all relevant contextual information. The construction of distractive contexts still relies on GPT-4 for sentence generation, which introduces potential biases or quality issues.
*   **Method Limitations:** While CoRE improves context robustness, it might not be applicable to all KE methods. It builds upon the locate-then-edit paradigm, and its effectiveness with other paradigms such as meta-learning or weight-preserving approaches is not explored.
*   **Scope Limitations:** The paper primarily focuses on factual knowledge editing. It does not address the challenges of editing other types of knowledge, such as commonsense reasoning or procedural knowledge.
*   **Generalization Concerns**: As mentioned in the limitations section of the paper, results could have limited applications in settings beyond simple factual knowledge.

**Score and Justification:**

Score: 8

**Justification:** The paper presents a novel problem formulation, a valuable benchmark dataset, and a promising method for improving context robustness in knowledge editing. It is well-motivated, rigorously evaluated, and provides valuable insights into the factors that influence editing success. While the paper has some limitations regarding dataset construction, method applicability, and scope, its contributions are significant and have the potential to drive further research and development in the field of knowledge editing. The emphasis on real-world applicability and the attention to practical challenges elevates its significance beyond purely theoretical contributions.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Case-Based Reasoning Enhances the Predictive Power of LLMs in Drug-Drug Interaction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23034v1)
- **Authors**: Guangyi Liu, Yongqi Zhang, Xunyuan Liu, Quanming Yao
- **Abstract**: Drug-drug interaction (DDI) prediction is critical for treatment safety. While large language models (LLMs) show promise in pharmaceutical tasks, their effectiveness in DDI prediction remains challenging. Inspired by the well-established clinical practice where physicians routinely reference similar historical cases to guide their decisions through case-based reasoning (CBR), we propose CBR-DDI, a novel framework that distills pharmacological principles from historical cases to improve LLM reasoning for DDI tasks. CBR-DDI constructs a knowledge repository by leveraging LLMs to extract pharmacological insights and graph neural networks (GNNs) to model drug associations. A hybrid retrieval mechanism and dual-layer knowledge-enhanced prompting allow LLMs to effectively retrieve and reuse relevant cases. We further introduce a representative sampling strategy for dynamic case refinement. Extensive experiments demonstrate that CBR-DDI achieves state-of-the-art performance, with a significant 28.7% accuracy improvement over both popular LLMs and CBR baseline, while maintaining high interpretability and flexibility.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CBR-DDI, a novel framework that enhances the DDI prediction capabilities of large language models (LLMs) by incorporating case-based reasoning (CBR).  CBR-DDI constructs a knowledge repository consisting of historical DDI cases, enriched with pharmacological insights extracted by LLMs and drug associations modeled by Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) from knowledge graphs.  A hybrid retrieval mechanism and dual-layer knowledge-enhanced prompting are used to retrieve and reuse relevant cases.  A representative sampling strategy dynamically refines the case repository. The experiments demonstrate that CBR-DDI achieves state-of-the-art performance, significantly improving accuracy and interpretability compared to standalone LLMs and a naive CBR baseline.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using CBR to improve LLM performance for DDI prediction is novel. It bridges a gap between clinical practice and LLM-based methods. The hybrid retrieval strategy combining semantic and structural similarity is a well-justified innovation, and the dual-layer prompting method for case reuse is a clever way to integrate information from historical cases and knowledge graphs. The representative sampling strategy, while a common technique, is appropriately adapted to the specific context of DDI prediction for scaling and maintaining diversity.

*   **Significance:** DDI prediction is a critical task with real-world implications for patient safety and healthcare costs. The paper addresses a significant challenge: the limitations of current LLM-based approaches in discovering underlying pharmacological mechanisms and generalizing to new drugs. The improvement in accuracy demonstrated by CBR-DDI is substantial, suggesting a practical benefit.  Furthermore, the interpretability afforded by the framework is a key advantage over "black box" LLM solutions, which is essential in a medical context.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive framework:** CBR-DDI provides a well-defined, modular framework, integrating LLMs, GNNs, and CBR effectively.
    *   **Strong empirical results:** The experiments are thorough, with comparisons against strong baselines across multiple datasets and settings. The ablation studies provide insights into the contribution of each component.
    *   **Interpretability:** The emphasis on interpretable interaction mechanisms is a major strength.
    *   **Plug-and-Play Flexibility:** CBR-DDI integrates with off-the-shelf LLMs without fine-tuning which is desirable.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on KG Quality:** The GNN module's performance is directly tied to the quality and completeness of the underlying knowledge graph. The paper acknowledges this but could explore the sensitivity to KG noise or incompleteness in more detail.
    *   **Scalability of Case Retrieval:** While representative sampling helps, the computational cost of retrieving and processing cases from a large repository could become a bottleneck as the repository grows. This aspect is not thoroughly addressed in the performance analysis.
    *   **Lack of molecular structures:** As the author mentioned, this limits the model's ability to perform fine-grained interaction analysis at a molecular level.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper is likely to influence future research in DDI prediction and related areas. It provides a promising direction for enhancing LLMs with structured knowledge and reasoning capabilities.  The framework could be adapted to other biomedical prediction tasks where historical case data is available.
* **Why the high score?** The paper's primary strength lies in its ability to merge structured knowledge, distilled mechanistic explanations, and LLM reasoning into a single framework. While the individual components such as GNNs and RAGs have been employed previously, the author's ability to harness them for explainable DDI prediction is noteworthy.

**Score: 8**

**Justification:**  The paper represents a strong contribution to the field. It demonstrates a clear improvement in DDI prediction accuracy and interpretability by effectively combining LLMs, GNNs, and CBR. While the framework has limitations related to KG quality and scalability, the novelty, significance, and comprehensive evaluation justify a high score. The focus on interpretable mechanisms addresses a critical need in medical applications and increases the potential for real-world impact.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 8/10

### Improving Multilingual Social Media Insights: Aspect-based Comment Analysis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23037v1)
- **Authors**: Longyin Zhang, Bowei Zou, Ai Ti Aw
- **Abstract**: The inherent nature of social media posts, characterized by the freedom of language use with a disjointed array of diverse opinions and topics, poses significant challenges to downstream NLP tasks such as comment clustering, comment summarization, and social media opinion analysis. To address this, we propose a granular level of identifying and generating aspect terms from individual comments to guide model attention. Specifically, we leverage multilingual large language models with supervised fine-tuning for comment aspect term generation (CAT-G), further aligning the model's predictions with human expectations through DPO. We demonstrate the effectiveness of our method in enhancing the comprehension of social media discourse on two NLP tasks. Moreover, this paper contributes the first multilingual CAT-G test set on English, Chinese, Malay, and Bahasa Indonesian. As LLM capabilities vary among languages, this test set allows for a comparative analysis of performance across languages with varying levels of LLM proficiency.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of analyzing social media comments, which are often noisy and multilingual, by proposing a Comment Aspect Term Generation (CATG) approach. The method uses multilingual large language models (LLMs) fine-tuned for identifying and generating aspect terms from individual comments. The models are further aligned with human expectations using Direct Preference Optimization (DPO). The authors create a multilingual CATG test set in English, Chinese, Malay, and Bahasa Indonesian, and demonstrate the effectiveness of their method by integrating it into downstream tasks such as monolingual and cross-lingual comment clustering (ComC), achieving improved performance. The approach aims to extract semantic aspects to filter noise and bridge language gaps within noisy data.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents several novel aspects:

    *   The creation of a multilingual CATG dataset is a significant contribution, as existing resources are largely monolingual. This dataset enables research on CATG in multiple Southeast Asian languages, which have been often overlooked.
    *   The application of DPO for aligning LLM outputs with human preferences in the context of CATG is a valuable addition, particularly as it addresses the knowledge bias in LLMs such as GPT-4, thereby leading to more accurate alignment with manual annotation.
    *   The combination of aspect term generation and comment clustering is not entirely novel in itself, as the integration is relatively straightforward. The key novelty lies in how CATG is used to enhance the performance of existing methods, namely the dynamic comment clustering.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in the following:

    *   **Improved Social Media Analysis:** The results show improvements in comment clustering, demonstrating the practical benefit of CATG for understanding social media discourse. The study also provides insights for dealing with noisy social media data and diverse language uses.
    *   **Resource for Future Research:** The multilingual dataset serves as a benchmark for future research on CATG, especially for low-resource languages.
    *   **Practical implications**: The study's findings suggest that targeted fine-tuning and alignment via DPO can significantly improve the relevance of LLM-generated aspect terms in downstream tasks such as comment clustering.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Multilingual Focus:** Addressing the multilingual aspect of social media analysis is highly relevant and enhances the impact of the research.
    *   **Empirical Evaluation:** The paper provides a thorough empirical evaluation with clear performance metrics. The inclusion of both monolingual and cross-lingual experiments strengthens the validity of the findings.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The authors state that the code and data will be made available, which promotes reproducibility.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Language Coverage:** While the inclusion of multiple languages is a strength, the dataset currently covers only four languages. Expanding this to include more languages would increase the generalizability of the approach.
    *   **Complexity:** While the DPO is beneficial in some cases, the analysis highlights challenges in achieving consistent improvements across different models and languages. More research is needed to understand the factors influencing its effectiveness.
    *   **Incremental nature**: While the method utilizes DPO for human preference alignment, the approach of concatenating vectors for ComC may seem straightforward to some. The benefit is incremental in nature.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in several ways:

    *   The multilingual dataset can stimulate more research on CATG in low-resource languages.
    *   The demonstration of DPO for aligning LLM outputs can inspire similar applications in other NLP tasks.
    *   The improvements in comment clustering can lead to more effective social media analysis tools.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the strengths and weaknesses outlined above, a score of 7 is appropriate. The paper addresses a real-world problem with a practical solution and contributes a valuable multilingual dataset. The use of DPO to enhance LLM outputs is a sound idea, but the overall impact of the individual contributions may not warrant a significantly higher score. While the overall result is an incremental improvement of previous works, the significance lies in the practical demonstration and multilingual angle.

**Score: 7**
- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### EL4NER: Ensemble Learning for Named Entity Recognition via Multiple Small-Parameter Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23038v1)
- **Authors**: Yuzhen Xiao, Jiahe Song, Yongxin Xu, Ruizhe Zhang, Yiqi Xiao, Xin Lu, Runchuan Zhu, Bowen Jiang, Junfeng Zhao
- **Abstract**: In-Context Learning (ICL) technique based on Large Language Models (LLMs) has gained prominence in Named Entity Recognition (NER) tasks for its lower computing resource consumption, less manual labeling overhead, and stronger generalizability. Nevertheless, most ICL-based NER methods depend on large-parameter LLMs: the open-source models demand substantial computational resources for deployment and inference, while the closed-source ones incur high API costs, raise data-privacy concerns, and hinder community collaboration. To address this question, we propose an Ensemble Learning Method for Named Entity Recognition (EL4NER), which aims at aggregating the ICL outputs of multiple open-source, small-parameter LLMs to enhance overall performance in NER tasks at less deployment and inference cost. Specifically, our method comprises three key components. First, we design a task decomposition-based pipeline that facilitates deep, multi-stage ensemble learning. Second, we introduce a novel span-level sentence similarity algorithm to establish an ICL demonstration retrieval mechanism better suited for NER tasks. Third, we incorporate a self-validation mechanism to mitigate the noise introduced during the ensemble process. We evaluated EL4NER on multiple widely adopted NER datasets from diverse domains. Our experimental results indicate that EL4NER surpasses most closed-source, large-parameter LLM-based methods at a lower parameter cost and even attains state-of-the-art (SOTA) performance among ICL-based methods on certain datasets. These results show the parameter efficiency of EL4NER and underscore the feasibility of employing open-source, small-parameter LLMs within the ICL paradigm for NER tasks.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary, a critical evaluation of novelty and significance, and a score with justification for the provided paper, "EL4NER: Ensemble Learning for Named Entity Recognition via Multiple Small-Parameter Large Language Models."

**Summary:**

The paper introduces EL4NER, an ensemble learning method for Named Entity Recognition (NER) that utilizes multiple open-source, small-parameter Large Language Models (LLMs). The method aims to achieve comparable or better performance than larger, more resource-intensive LLMs while reducing deployment and inference costs and mitigating concerns around data privacy. EL4NER features a task decomposition-based pipeline that facilitates deep, multi-stage ensemble learning, a span-level sentence similarity algorithm for improved ICL demonstration retrieval, and a self-validation mechanism to reduce noise during the ensemble process. The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of EL4NER across multiple NER datasets, showing it can surpass many closed-source, large-parameter LLM-based methods and even achieve state-of-the-art results on certain datasets.

**Rigorous and Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper has a good degree of novelty, it addresses the limitations of relying solely on large-parameter LLMs for ICL-based NER. The idea of ensembling small, open-source models for NER is somewhat novel.  While ensemble methods are not new, applying them to NER in the specific context of ICL with small LLMs, combined with the specific techniques like the span-level similarity and self-validation, contributes to a unique approach. The task decomposition strategy is not revolutionary, but the way it's implemented to facilitate multi-stage ensemble learning is a valuable aspect of the work. However, each of these components is not entirely new individually, so a significant part of the novelty lies in the integrated design.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from addressing a practical problem.  Large LLMs are computationally expensive and raise privacy concerns.  A method that can achieve comparable or better results with smaller, open-source models has clear practical value for researchers and practitioners who may not have access to large computational resources or are concerned about data privacy. Achieving strong NER performance with open-source models significantly broadens access and encourages community collaboration. The experimental results, showing EL4NER surpassing some closed-source large LLM methods, are strong evidence of the significance of the approach. The experiments showcase that EL4NER achieves SOTA on certain datasets, further underscoring its importance.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addresses a clear and practical problem:** The high cost and resource requirements of large LLMs.
    *   **Well-designed architecture:** The combination of task decomposition, span-level similarity, and self-validation creates a robust system.
    *   **Strong empirical results:** Demonstrates competitive or superior performance compared to larger LLM-based methods on multiple datasets.
    *   **Reduces costs:** Lower parameter counts mean reduced deployment, inference and API costs.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Component-level novelty:** While the overall system is novel, the individual components have roots in prior research.
    *   **Generalizability across all NER tasks:** The paper could discuss limitations of the approach, such as tasks where the diverse knowledge of larger LLMs is absolutely critical, even if small LLMs may be better suited to some.
    *   **Hyperparameter tuning:** The paper should address hyperparameter sensitivity and the robustness of the parameters.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper introduces a valuable approach to NER that addresses the practical limitations of large LLMs. The system's components (task decomposition, span-level similarity, self-validation) and the experimental results provide convincing evidence of its effectiveness. While the individual components are not entirely novel, the combination and application within the ICL framework is valuable. The potential for wider adoption and community contribution using the method increases significantly due to the use of small open-source LLMs.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### From Theory to Application: Fine-Tuning Large EEG Model with Real-World Stress Data
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23042v1)
- **Authors**: Siwen Wang, Shitou Zhang, Wan-Lin Chen, Dung Truong, Tzyy-Ping Jung
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements in Large Language Models have inspired the development of foundation models across various domains. In this study, we evaluate the efficacy of Large EEG Models (LEMs) by fine-tuning LaBraM, a state-of-the-art foundation EEG model, on a real-world stress classification dataset collected in a graduate classroom. Unlike previous studies that primarily evaluate LEMs using data from controlled clinical settings, our work assesses their applicability to real-world environments. We train a binary classifier that distinguishes between normal and elevated stress states using resting-state EEG data recorded from 18 graduate students during a class session. The best-performing fine-tuned model achieves a balanced accuracy of 90.47% with a 5-second window, significantly outperforming traditional stress classifiers in both accuracy and inference efficiency. We further evaluate the robustness of the fine-tuned LEM under random data shuffling and reduced channel counts. These results demonstrate the capability of LEMs to effectively process real-world EEG data and highlight their potential to revolutionize brain-computer interface applications by shifting the focus from model-centric to data-centric design.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper explores the application of Large EEG Models (LEMs) to real-world stress classification using EEG data collected in a classroom setting. Specifically, the authors fine-tune LaBraM, a state-of-the-art LEM, on a stress dataset and demonstrate its effectiveness in distinguishing between normal and elevated stress states. The fine-tuned model achieves a balanced accuracy of 90.47% with a short (5-second) window, outperforming traditional stress classifiers. The paper also investigates the model's robustness to data shuffling and reduced channel counts, highlighting both the potential and limitations of LEMs in processing real-world EEG data and shifting the focus from model-centric to data-centric BCI designs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Real-World Data Application:** A key strength is the use of EEG data collected in a classroom setting, a more ecologically valid environment compared to controlled lab settings often used in LEM evaluation. This provides a valuable assessment of the model's applicability to real-world scenarios.
    *   **Strong Performance:** The reported 90.47% accuracy, especially with only a 5-second window, is impressive and suggests the potential of LEMs for real-time stress detection. This highlights the ability of LEMs to learn transferable features.
    *   **Comparison with Traditional Methods:** The comparison with traditional stress classifiers provides a benchmark and demonstrates the superior performance of the fine-tuned LEM in this context. This gives added weight to the LEM's advantages.
    *   **Robustness Evaluation:** The investigation of model robustness to data shuffling and reduced channel counts provides valuable insights into the model's limitations and potential for optimization. The fact that accuracy degrades with reduced channels points to future avenues of improvement.
    *   **Data-Centric Approach:** The paper advocates for a data-centric approach in BCI development, which aligns with current trends in machine learning and emphasizes the importance of high-quality datasets.
    *   **Thorough Parameter Tuning and Experimental Rigor:** The authors document their parameter selections and experimental configurations making the methodology sound.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Dataset Size:** While real-world, the dataset is still relatively small (n=18 subjects and a total of 92 recordings). This limits the generalizability of the findings and increases the risk of overfitting, despite the use of data augmentation. The authors acknowledge this limitation, particularly noting the sensitivity of results to out-of-distribution samples in the small testing set. More data points are needed to confirm findings.
    *   **Lack of Demographic Diversity:** The participants are all graduate students from a single university. This limits the applicability of the model to other populations (e.g., different age groups, occupations, or cultural backgrounds).
    *   **Limited Channel Selection Methodology.** The channel reduction process could be performed more rigorously. One channel selection relied on previously identified channels with high value for stress detection, but another relied purely on random selections, which limits the conclusions that can be drawn on the importance of different channels.
    *   **Black Box Nature:** The paper does not address the interpretability of the LEM. While acknowledging that LEMs are "black boxes," it does not offer any analysis of *what* the model is learning from the EEG data. Understanding the features that contribute to stress classification would be valuable.
    *   **Computational Demands:** The paper mentions that fine-tuning LEMs requires significant computational resources. This could limit accessibility for researchers with limited resources. Addressing how this challenge might be overcome could also be helpful.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The application of LEMs to real-world stress classification is somewhat novel. While LEMs have been applied to other EEG tasks, this paper explores their potential in a less controlled, more ecologically valid setting. Showing that LEMs are useful in real-world settings has important implications for BCI design.
    *   The performance of the fine-tuned LEM, especially with a short window, is significant, as it suggests that LEMs can be used for near-real-time stress detection.
    *   The robustness evaluation provides valuable insights into the limitations and potential for optimization of LEMs in this context.

    *The focus on shifting from a model-centric to a data-centric approach is aligned with modern machine learning trends and helps provide a useful direction for future research in this area.*

**Overall:**
The paper makes a useful contribution by demonstrating the use of LEMs in real-world settings. The strong performance in stress classification, coupled with the robustness evaluation, provides a solid foundation for future research. However, the small dataset size and lack of interpretability limit the impact of the findings. Considering the strengths and weaknesses, and how well the strengths contribute to the field, the overall novelty score of this paper lies between 6 and 7.

Score: 6.5

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### DenoiseRotator: Enhance Pruning Robustness for LLMs via Importance Concentration
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23049v1)
- **Authors**: Tianteng Gu, Bei Liu, Bo Xiao, Ke Zeng, Jiacheng Liu, Yanmin Qian
- **Abstract**: Pruning is a widely used technique to compress large language models (LLMs) by removing unimportant weights, but it often suffers from significant performance degradation - especially under semi-structured sparsity constraints. Existing pruning methods primarily focus on estimating the importance of individual weights, which limits their ability to preserve critical capabilities of the model. In this work, we propose a new perspective: rather than merely selecting which weights to prune, we first redistribute parameter importance to make the model inherently more amenable to pruning. By minimizing the information entropy of normalized importance scores, our approach concentrates importance onto a smaller subset of weights, thereby enhancing pruning robustness. We instantiate this idea through DenoiseRotator, which applies learnable orthogonal transformations to the model's weight matrices. Our method is model-agnostic and can be seamlessly integrated with existing pruning techniques such as Magnitude, SparseGPT, and Wanda. Evaluated on LLaMA3, Qwen2.5, and Mistral models under 50% unstructured and 2:4 semi-structured sparsity, DenoiseRotator consistently improves perplexity and zero-shot accuracy. For instance, on LLaMA3-70B pruned with SparseGPT at 2:4 semi-structured sparsity, DenoiseRotator reduces the perplexity gap to the dense model by 58%, narrowing the degradation from 8.1 to 3.4 points. Codes are available at https://github.com/Axel-gu/DenoiseRotator.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "DenoiseRotator: Enhance Pruning Robustness for LLMs via Importance Concentration":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DenoiseRotator, a novel framework designed to improve the robustness of large language model (LLM) pruning.  Instead of solely focusing on identifying which weights to prune, DenoiseRotator aims to reshape the distribution of importance scores within the model *before* pruning. It achieves this through learnable orthogonal transformations applied to weight matrices, concentrating importance onto a smaller subset of parameters. The authors minimize the information entropy of normalized importance scores, a technique termed "entropy-guided importance concentration." DenoiseRotator is model-agnostic and can be integrated with existing pruning methods like Magnitude, SparseGPT, and Wanda.  Extensive experiments on LLaMA3, Qwen2.5, and Mistral models demonstrate improved perplexity and zero-shot accuracy under various sparsity constraints (unstructured and semi-structured).

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of *reshaping* the importance distribution *before* pruning is relatively novel in the context of LLM pruning.  Most existing methods focus on identifying and removing less important weights within the existing parameter space. DenoiseRotator's orthogonal transformations and entropy minimization offer a different perspective. The authors leverage the computational invariance of Transformers, which is not a new concept in itself (see SliceGPT), but its application for *importance concentration* in pruning is a valuable contribution.
*   **Significance:** Improving the robustness of LLM pruning is a crucial problem. Pruning is vital for deploying and scaling LLMs due to their large size and computational cost. The ability to maintain performance under high sparsity levels is essential for practical applications. DenoiseRotator's demonstrated improvements in perplexity and zero-shot accuracy, especially in challenging semi-structured pruning scenarios, are significant. The plug-and-play nature of the framework further enhances its practical value. The gains demonstrated are impactful and could lead to more efficient and deployable LLMs.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Principled Approach:** The method is theoretically grounded in information theory (entropy minimization) and leverages the properties of orthogonal transformations. This provides a more solid foundation than purely heuristic approaches.
    *   **Model-Agnostic and Plug-and-Play:** The framework's compatibility with various LLM architectures and pruning techniques makes it highly versatile and easily adaptable.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The paper presents comprehensive experimental results on diverse models and sparsity patterns, showing consistent improvements. The ablation study provides insights into the effectiveness of entropy reduction.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the method, its motivation, and its advantages.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Computational Overhead:** Training the orthogonal transformations introduces additional computational cost, though the authors note that this is independent of the calibration dataset size and occurs only once. Also, while small, there is some overhead associated with the learned rotations.
    *   **Limited Analysis of Failure Cases:** The paper primarily focuses on positive results. A deeper analysis of cases where DenoiseRotator *doesn't* improve pruning performance would strengthen the work.
    *   **Lack of Direct Comparison with Other Rotation-Based Methods:** While the paper mentions RotPruner and other related works, a more direct comparison (especially in the experimental section) would be beneficial. It would also be valuable to discuss in what ways the method is distinct and provides improvement over rotpruner.
    *   **Semi-structured Sparsity and Alignment:** The paper suggests orthogonal matrices "act as a form of random permutation, increasing the chance that crucial weights align with the semi-structured sparsity pattern". While this is a plausible explanation, it is not fully explored or justified, and may require further investigation.
    *   **Limited fine-tuning details**: No fine-tuning was conducted, but no analysis was offered regarding why they believe this approach is effective without fine-tuning and if finetuning would allow better outcomes.
*   **Potential Influence:** DenoiseRotator has the potential to become a widely adopted technique for LLM pruning, particularly in scenarios where high sparsity and/or semi-structured sparsity are required. Its focus on reshaping the importance landscape offers a new direction for research in model compression.

**Score:** 8

**Rationale:**

DenoiseRotator presents a novel and effective method for improving the robustness of LLM pruning. The core idea of entropy-guided importance concentration is well-motivated and theoretically grounded. The plug-and-play nature of the framework and comprehensive experimental results make it a significant contribution to the field. While there are some weaknesses related to computational overhead, limited failure case analysis, more comparison with existing rotation methods, further justifcation for semi-structured sparsity performance and impact from downstream fine-tuning, its overall impact is high due to its potential for more efficient and deployable LLMs. The score reflects the paper's significant contributions to the important problem of LLM pruning robustness, balanced by areas where further investigation and analysis would strengthen the work.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### Query Routing for Retrieval-Augmented Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23052v1)
- **Authors**: Jiarui Zhang, Xiangyu Liu, Yong Hu, Chaoyue Niu, Fan Wu, Guihai Chen
- **Abstract**: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) significantly improves the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) on knowledge-intensive tasks. However, varying response quality across LLMs under RAG necessitates intelligent routing mechanisms, which select the most suitable model for each query from multiple retrieval-augmented LLMs via a dedicated router model. We observe that external documents dynamically affect LLMs' ability to answer queries, while existing routing methods, which rely on static parametric knowledge representations, exhibit suboptimal performance in RAG scenarios. To address this, we formally define the new retrieval-augmented LLM routing problem, incorporating the influence of retrieved documents into the routing framework. We propose RAGRouter, a RAG-aware routing design, which leverages document embeddings and RAG capability embeddings with contrastive learning to capture knowledge representation shifts and enable informed routing decisions. Extensive experiments on diverse knowledge-intensive tasks and retrieval settings show that RAGRouter outperforms the best individual LLM by 3.61% on average and existing routing methods by 3.29%-9.33%. With an extended score-threshold-based mechanism, it also achieves strong performance-efficiency trade-offs under low-latency constraints.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of effectively routing queries to the most suitable Large Language Model (LLM) within a Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) framework.  It argues that existing LLM routing methods, designed for non-RAG scenarios, fail to account for the dynamic influence of retrieved documents on LLMs' ability to answer queries.  The authors propose RAGRouter, a contrastive learning-based routing mechanism that incorporates document embeddings and "RAG capability embeddings" to capture knowledge representation shifts and enable more informed routing decisions. They demonstrate through experiments on various knowledge-intensive tasks that RAGRouter outperforms individual LLMs and existing routing methods, exhibiting improved performance and performance-efficiency trade-offs, especially under low-latency constraints.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's main novelty lies in explicitly addressing the LLM routing problem in the RAG setting and recognizing the limitations of static-knowledge-based routing approaches.  Introducing document embeddings and RAG capability embeddings within a contrastive learning framework is a novel approach to capture the dynamic interplay between LLM's inherent knowledge and retrieved information.  The formal definition of the routing problem under RAG setting is also a solid contribution.

* **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its ability to improve the performance and efficiency of RAG systems.  By intelligently routing queries to the most capable LLM *after* considering the retrieved documents, it overcomes the inherent heterogeneity of LLMs in their ability to utilize external knowledge.  This is particularly important in scenarios with multiple available LLMs or in latency-sensitive applications where invoking all models is not feasible.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing LLM routing methods in RAG settings and provides a formal definition of the RAG-aware routing problem.
    * **Well-Designed Approach:** RAGRouter is a well-designed system that incorporates relevant factors influencing post-retrieval performance (document semantics, query interactions, and LLM RAG capability). The architecture incorporates appropriate encoders (document encoder, cross encoder) and embedding layers.
    * **Contrastive Learning Framework:** The use of contrastive learning, with both cross-setting and intra-setting contrasts, is a strong technical choice for capturing knowledge shifts and mitigating variations introduced by retrieval.
    * **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The authors perform extensive experiments on diverse knowledge-intensive tasks and retrieval settings, comparing against relevant baselines and demonstrating significant performance gains. The latency-aware extension and ablation studies further validate the effectiveness and importance of different components of RAGRouter.
    * **Case Studies:**  The inclusion of case studies gives a clear picture of the effectiveness of RAGRouter.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Implementation Complexity:** The RAGRouter architecture, with its various encoders and embeddings, adds complexity to the RAG pipeline, which may increase the engineering overhead of deploying such a system in production.  The paper could benefit from more discussion about the practical deployment considerations.
    * **Dependence on a high-quality retriever:** The performance of the system still relies heavily on the quality of retrieved documents. The discussion on how RAGRouter handles scenarios where the retrieved documents are irrelevant or incorrect could be improved.
    * **Limited Generalization Discussion:** While the paper demonstrates strong results on specific datasets, the generalization ability of RAGRouter to significantly different domains or LLM architectures could be further explored.

* **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence future research on LLM routing and RAG systems. It opens up new avenues for exploring how to effectively combine multiple LLMs and leverage external knowledge in dynamic and efficient ways.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field of LLM routing by specifically addressing the challenges of RAG. RAGRouter addresses a specific and current gap in existing research by incorporating document embeddings and "RAG capability embeddings" within a contrastive learning framework. The design and the experiments, along with the case studies, are thorough. However, the added complexity in the RAG pipeline and the reliance on retriever quality (which is common to all RAG-based systems) somewhat limits the impact. Overall, the contribution justifies a score of 7.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Augment or Not? A Comparative Study of Pure and Augmented Large Language Model Recommenders
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23053v1)
- **Authors**: Wei-Hsiang Huang, Chen-Wei Ke, Wei-Ning Chiu, Yu-Xuan Su, Chun-Chun Yang, Chieh-Yuan Cheng, Yun-Nung Chen, Pu-Jen Cheng
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have introduced new paradigms for recommender systems by enabling richer semantic understanding and incorporating implicit world knowledge. In this study, we propose a systematic taxonomy that classifies existing approaches into two categories: (1) Pure LLM Recommenders, which rely solely on LLMs, and (2) Augmented LLM Recommenders, which integrate additional non-LLM techniques to enhance performance. This taxonomy provides a novel lens through which to examine the evolving landscape of LLM-based recommendation. To support fair comparison, we introduce a unified evaluation platform that benchmarks representative models under consistent experimental settings, highlighting key design choices that impact effectiveness. We conclude by discussing open challenges and outlining promising directions for future research. This work offers both a comprehensive overview and practical guidance for advancing next-generation LLM-powered recommender.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a comparative study of Large Language Model (LLM) recommenders.
