- **Score**: 7/10

### ContextQFormer: A New Context Modeling Method for Multi-Turn Multi-Modal Conversations
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23121v1)
- **Authors**: Yiming Lei, Zhizheng Yang, Zeming Liu, Haitao Leng, Shaoguo Liu, Tingting Gao, Qingjie Liu, Yunhong Wang
- **Abstract**: Multi-modal large language models have demonstrated remarkable zero-shot abilities and powerful image-understanding capabilities. However, the existing open-source multi-modal models suffer from the weak capability of multi-turn interaction, especially for long contexts. To address the issue, we first introduce a context modeling module, termed ContextQFormer, which utilizes a memory block to enhance the presentation of contextual information. Furthermore, to facilitate further research, we carefully build a new multi-turn multi-modal dialogue dataset (TMDialog) for pre-training, instruction-tuning, and evaluation, which will be open-sourced lately. Compared with other multi-modal dialogue datasets, TMDialog contains longer conversations, which supports the research of multi-turn multi-modal dialogue. In addition, ContextQFormer is compared with three baselines on TMDialog and experimental results illustrate that ContextQFormer achieves an improvement of 2%-4% in available rate over baselines.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ContextQFormer, a novel method for context modeling in multi-turn multi-modal conversations. Addressing the limitation of existing multi-modal LLMs in handling long-context interactions, ContextQFormer utilizes a memory block to enhance the representation of contextual information.  Furthermore, the authors created TMDialog, a new multi-turn multi-modal dialogue dataset designed for pre-training, instruction tuning, and evaluation. Experiments comparing ContextQFormer with baselines on TMDialog show improvements in available rate, demonstrating the effectiveness of the proposed approach.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper has some novelty in the following aspects:

*   **ContextQFormer Module:** The introduction of a memory block specifically designed for capturing contextual information in long multi-modal dialogues is a novel contribution. The use of learnable queries and cross-attention with historical information is a well-motivated approach.
*   **TMDialog Dataset:** The creation and open-sourcing of TMDialog address a crucial gap in the availability of long-context multi-modal dialogue datasets. The dataset seems to have useful characteristics, like longer conversations and better-defined dialogues than existing ones.
*   **Combining Multiple Approaches:** The paper combines several existing techniques (ViT, Roberta, Q-Former) with a new module (ContextQFormer) within a two-stage training framework. The novelty lies in how these elements are integrated and adapted for the specific task of multi-turn, multi-modal dialogue.

**Significance:** The significance of this work is moderate.

*   **Addressing a Limitation:** The paper tackles a real and relevant problem – the poor performance of current multi-modal LLMs in long, multi-turn conversations.
*   **Dataset Contribution:** The TMDialog dataset could be a valuable resource for the community, enabling further research in this area.
*   **Performance Improvement:** The reported improvements in available rate, even if relatively modest (2-4%), indicate a positive impact of the proposed method.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Incremental Improvement:** The improvements are somewhat incremental, with the core architecture relying heavily on existing components like ViT, Q-Former, and LLaMA. The ContextQFormer module, while novel, may not be a groundbreaking architectural change.
*   **Reliance on GPT-4 for Data Generation:** The use of GPT-4 for generating a significant portion of the TMDialog dataset raises concerns about potential biases, data quality, and the possibility of the model simply memorizing or overfitting the characteristics of GPT-4 itself.  The paper mentions rechecking with GPT-3.5, but it remains a potential limitation.
*   **Limited Comparison:** A more comprehensive comparison against other state-of-the-art long-context models (especially ones specifically designed for long dialogue, even if unimodal) would have strengthened the evaluation.
*   **Modest Results:** The 2-4% improvements are reasonable but not dramatic. It's worth noting the evaluation metrics and potential for human errors/bias due to reliance on another large language model (GPT-4) for the scoring. This should be addressed in future work, where human evaluation could provide more convincing validation.
*   **Lack of Ablation Studies:** It would be helpful to include an ablation study isolating the effect of different components to demonstrate the value of ContextQFormer over baseline approaches. For instance, the benefit of different components, such as the memory block, query mechanism, or individual contributions of the encoders, may not be clearly understood.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper has the potential to influence the field in the following ways:

*   **Inspiring Context Modeling Techniques:** The ContextQFormer module could inspire further research into more effective ways of modeling context in multi-modal dialogue systems.
*   **Providing a Benchmark:** The TMDialog dataset could serve as a valuable benchmark for evaluating and comparing different approaches to long-context multi-modal conversation.
*   **Opening Avenues for Optimization:** The code and dataset released by the authors could provide the basis for future research on the task and new methods to tackle the problem.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **6/10**.

Here's the rationale:

The paper presents a solid, well-structured approach to addressing a relevant problem in multi-modal dialogue. The creation of the TMDialog dataset is a valuable contribution. The novelty, however, is somewhat limited, as the approach relies heavily on existing components and shows only modest performance improvements. The reliance on GPT-4 for data generation and evaluation raises some concerns. Future work should address these limitations with more comprehensive experiments and a thorough analysis of the dataset's characteristics. The potential for the dataset to serve as a community benchmark and the ideas presented to inspire further research warrant the score of 6.

### PBEBench: A Multi-Step Programming by Examples Reasoning Benchmark inspired by Historical Linguistics
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23126v1)
- **Authors**: Atharva Naik, Darsh Agrawal, Manav Kapadnis, Yuwei An, Yash Mathur, Carolyn Rose, David Mortensen
- **Abstract**: Recently, long chain of thought (LCoT), Large Language Models (LLMs), have taken the machine learning world by storm with their breathtaking reasoning capabilities. However, are the abstract reasoning abilities of these models general enough for problems of practical importance? Unlike past work, which has focused mainly on math, coding, and data wrangling, we focus on a historical linguistics-inspired inductive reasoning problem, formulated as Programming by Examples. We develop a fully automated pipeline for dynamically generating a benchmark for this task with controllable difficulty in order to tackle scalability and contamination issues to which many reasoning benchmarks are subject. Using our pipeline, we generate a test set with nearly 1k instances that is challenging for all state-of-the-art reasoning LLMs, with the best model (Claude-3.7-Sonnet) achieving a mere 54% pass rate, demonstrating that LCoT LLMs still struggle with a class or reasoning that is ubiquitous in historical linguistics as well as many other domains.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PBEBench, a new benchmark for evaluating the reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs).  This benchmark is inspired by problems in historical linguistics, specifically the task of forward reconstruction, where the goal is to infer a sequence of string rewrite rules (a program) that transforms a set of input strings into corresponding output strings.  The benchmark generation process is fully automated, allowing for controlled difficulty and scalability, addressing potential contamination issues.  The paper demonstrates that even state-of-the-art LLMs struggle on this task, achieving only a 54% pass rate with the best model (Claude-3.7-Sonnet). The authors perform analyses to understand the complexity features that make instances more difficult and evaluate the models based on their ability to generate programs consistent with linguistic principles such as feeding and bleeding relations between sound changes.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel benchmark task within the context of LLM reasoning. The use of historical linguistics as inspiration is unique and interesting.  The focus on *forward reconstruction* as a PBE problem, combining inductive reasoning and planning, distinguishes this work from existing PBE benchmarks which often focus on coding, math, or data wrangling.  The fully automated benchmark generation and the focus on relationships between rules (feeding, bleeding) are significant contributions. The paper also introduces a provably correct algorithm for detecting feeding and bleeding relations, which seems to be unique.
*   **Significance:** The paper highlights a class of reasoning problems where even state-of-the-art LLMs still struggle.  This is significant because it shows that LLMs' abstract reasoning abilities are not as general as one might assume and that there is a continued need for improvement in their ability to perform multi-step reasoning tasks with complex interdependencies between steps.  The benchmark provides a valuable tool for evaluating and improving LLMs' reasoning capabilities in a domain that is both practically relevant (though perhaps niche) and scientifically interesting.  The ability to control the difficulty of the benchmark is a significant strength, enabling targeted evaluation and development of models. The focus on addressing data leakage concerns is also crucial for creating a reliable benchmark.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear problem definition:** The forward reconstruction problem is well-defined and explained.
    *   **Automated benchmark generation:**  The automated pipeline addresses scalability and contamination issues that plague many benchmarks.
    *   **Controlled difficulty:**  The ability to control complexity features allows for a more nuanced evaluation of LLMs.
    *   **Interesting domain:** The use of historical linguistics as inspiration makes the benchmark engaging and relevant.
    *   **Provably correct algorithms:** The work introduces novel algorithms for detecting feeding and bleeding relations between rules.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Low instance complexity:** The data generation strategy, after removing “degenerate” programs, leads to a skewed data distribution with a low percentage of instances having BFCC relations, reducing the practical challenge. The limitations reported in the discussion section about sampling efficiency and “degenerate” programs highlights this limitation. This raises concern for the diversity of the benchmark, as it seems that removing these "degenerate" programs effectively limits its ability to generate challenging relations between the rules, which seem to be the core complexity sought.
    *   **Limited analysis:** The analysis of why LLMs struggle on this task could be more in-depth. While the paper discusses cascade length, relations, and input-output distance, it doesn't fully explore the specific failure modes of LLMs. More detailed analysis of the errors produced by the LLMs could shed light on the specific reasoning challenges posed by the task. For example, how frequently did the models produce replacement sequences that preserved the relationships between rules? How frequently did models violate instruction constraints on program composition?
    *   **Limited scope of the benchmark:**  While novel, the specific problem of forward reconstruction in historical linguistics might be considered niche. The applicability of the benchmark's findings to more general reasoning tasks could be made more explicit. The reliance on string rewriting rules makes it difficult to generalize into abstract reasoning.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more robust reasoning LLMs and inspire the creation of new benchmarks in other domains that require a similar combination of inductive reasoning and planning. The focus on "opaque" rule ordering could lead to new research directions in improving LLMs' ability to handle complex dependencies.

**Score:** 7

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel and potentially significant contribution. The use of historical linguistics to inspire a reasoning benchmark is clever, and the controlled generation process is a valuable feature. The paper clearly identifies a challenging area for current LLMs. The limited complexity due to the data generation procedure is a significant drawback. A deeper investigation into the failure modes of models and a more explicit connection to general reasoning problems would strengthen the work. While significant, the specific historical linguistics domain is somewhat niche, limiting its general impact compared to work with a broader scope.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### VERINA: Benchmarking Verifiable Code Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23135v1)
- **Authors**: Zhe Ye, Zhengxu Yan, Jingxuan He, Timothe Kasriel, Kaiyu Yang, Dawn Song
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly integrated in software development, but ensuring correctness in LLM-generated code remains challenging and often requires costly manual review. Verifiable code generation -- jointly generating code, specifications, and proofs of code-specification alignment -- offers a promising path to address this limitation and further unleash LLMs' benefits in coding. Yet, there exists a significant gap in evaluation: current benchmarks often lack support for end-to-end verifiable code generation. In this paper, we introduce Verina (Verifiable Code Generation Arena), a high-quality benchmark enabling a comprehensive and modular evaluation of code, specification, and proof generation as well as their compositions. Verina consists of 189 manually curated coding tasks in Lean, with detailed problem descriptions, reference implementations, formal specifications, and extensive test suites. Our extensive evaluation of state-of-the-art LLMs reveals significant challenges in verifiable code generation, especially in proof generation, underscoring the need for improving LLM-based theorem provers in verification domains. The best model, OpenAI o4-mini, generates only 61.4% correct code, 51.0% sound and complete specifications, and 3.6% successful proofs, with one trial per task. We hope Verina will catalyze progress in verifiable code generation by providing a rigorous and comprehensive benchmark. We release our dataset on https://huggingface.co/datasets/sunblaze-ucb/verina and our evaluation code on https://github.com/sunblaze-ucb/verina.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the VERINA paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces VERINA (Verifiable Code Generation Arena), a new benchmark for evaluating verifiable code generation capabilities of large language models (LLMs). VERINA consists of 189 manually curated coding tasks in Lean, complete with problem descriptions, reference implementations, formal specifications, and extensive test suites. The benchmark allows for modular evaluation of code, specification, and proof generation, as well as combinations thereof. The authors evaluate several state-of-the-art LLMs using VERINA, revealing challenges in verifiable code generation, particularly in proof generation. The paper releases the dataset and evaluation code to catalyze progress in the field.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper makes a significant contribution by addressing a gap in the evaluation of verifiable code generation. Existing benchmarks often focus on individual tasks (code generation alone) or lack comprehensive support for specifications and proofs. VERINA provides a holistic benchmark. The use of Lean for verification, while not entirely new, is less common than Dafny in LLM benchmarks, offering a different perspective on verifiable code generation.

*   **Significance:** Verifiable code generation is a crucial step towards trustworthy AI-driven software development. By providing a benchmark for this task, VERINA contributes to:
    *   **Rigorous evaluation:** Enabling standardized and repeatable comparisons between different LLMs' abilities in generating verifiable code.
    *   **Identifying limitations:** Highlighting the specific challenges LLMs face in generating correct specifications and proofs. The finding that even the best LLMs struggle with proof generation (pass@1 of only 3.6% for OpenAI 04-mini) underscores the need for improvements in theorem proving capabilities for LLMs.
    *   **Driving research:** Providing a well-defined target for future research aimed at improving verifiable code generation techniques. The modular design facilitates targeted research on specific weaknesses.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **High-quality dataset:** The manual curation and verification of the benchmark instances ensure accuracy and clarity, making it a reliable resource for evaluation. The diversity in difficulty between VERINA-BASIC and VERINA-ADV is a major strength.
    *   **Comprehensive evaluation:** The modular design allows for a wide range of evaluation scenarios, capturing real-world use cases.
    *   **Robust metrics:** The paper introduces a practical, testing-based approach for evaluating specification soundness and completeness, addressing the challenges of formal verification in complex domains. The metrics are well-defined and allow for comparisons between different approaches.
    *   **Thorough experimentation:** The paper conducts a comprehensive evaluation of nine state-of-the-art LLMs, providing valuable insights into their strengths and weaknesses in verifiable code generation. The study of iterative refinement is also interesting.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited scale:** While high-quality, the dataset size (189 examples) is modest compared to some other code generation benchmarks. This may limit the ability to fine-tune models directly on VERINA.
    *   **Task complexity:** The focus is on relatively simple, standalone tasks. Complex real-world verification projects are not fully captured.
    *   **Evaluation metric for SpecGen:** Although clever, the test-based approach for specification evaluation is inherently limited; it can identify incorrect specifications through counterexamples but cannot *prove* a specification is correct.
    *   **Data contamination risk:** Although the programs are newly written, the underlying tasks are drawn from widely available sources. This introduces a risk (albeit mitigated) of data contamination in models pre-trained on large corpora.

*   **Potential Influence:** VERINA has the potential to become a standard benchmark in the field of verifiable code generation. It can guide the development of new techniques for improving the reliability and trustworthiness of AI-driven software development. The clear articulation of remaining challenges (particularly in proof generation) should stimulate focused research in this area. The code/data release is an essential element for the field's progress.

*Given the significance of the problem being addressed, the novelty in the approach to benchmarking, the comprehensiveness of the evaluation, and the release of the dataset to the research community, VERINA represents a significant contribution, although some limitations need to be taken into account.*

**Score: 8.5**
- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### Enhancing Large Language Models'Machine Translation via Dynamic Focus Anchoring
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23140v1)
- **Authors**: Qiuyu Ding, Zhiqiang Cao, Hailong Cao, Tiejun Zhao
- **Abstract**: Large language models have demonstrated exceptional performance across multiple crosslingual NLP tasks, including machine translation (MT). However, persistent challenges remain in addressing context-sensitive units (CSUs), such as polysemous words. These CSUs not only affect the local translation accuracy of LLMs, but also affect LLMs' understanding capability for sentences and tasks, and even lead to translation failure. To address this problem, we propose a simple but effective method to enhance LLMs' MT capabilities by acquiring CSUs and applying semantic focus. Specifically, we dynamically analyze and identify translation challenges, then incorporate them into LLMs in a structured manner to mitigate mistranslations or misunderstandings of CSUs caused by information flattening. Efficiently activate LLMs to identify and apply relevant knowledge from its vast data pool in this way, ensuring more accurate translations for translating difficult terms. On a benchmark dataset of MT, our proposed method achieved competitive performance compared to multiple existing open-sourced MT baseline models. It demonstrates effectiveness and robustness across multiple language pairs, including both similar language pairs and distant language pairs. Notably, the proposed method requires no additional model training and enhances LLMs' performance across multiple NLP tasks with minimal resource consumption.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel method called "Dynamic Focus Anchoring" (DFA) to enhance the machine translation (MT) capabilities of large language models (LLMs). DFA addresses the "semantic ambiguity" problem arising from context-sensitive units (CSUs) like polysemous words, which often leads to translation inaccuracies or failures in LLMs. DFA involves two main steps: (1) identifying and classifying CSUs within the input sentence using both external linguistic resources and LLM-intrinsic knowledge, and (2) injecting semantic focus into the prompt by explicitly guiding the LLM to focus on these CSUs during translation. The method doesn't require model fine-tuning or parallel data. The authors conduct experiments on WMT datasets for English-German (EN-DE) and English-Chinese (EN-ZH) language pairs, showing performance improvements compared to existing LLM-based MT baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addresses a real problem:** The paper tackles a significant issue in LLM-based MT – the handling of context-sensitive units and semantic ambiguity, a challenge that is not well addressed by simply scaling models or using standard prompt engineering.
    *   **Resource-efficient:**  A key strength is that the proposed method is resource-efficient, requiring no additional model training or parallel data, which makes it practical for real-world deployment, and could allow to reduce the cost associated with training LLMs.
    *   **Novelty:** The approach of dynamically identifying and anchoring focus on CSUs within the prompt is a relatively novel way to guide LLMs during translation. The dual-path approach for CSU identification (external resources + internal knowledge activation) is also a good design.
    *   **Empirical validation:** The method is evaluated on standard datasets with multiple metrics, and shows consistent improvements. The ablation studies offer insights into the contribution of different components of the method.
    *   **Clear explanation and analysis:**  The paper provides a clear explanation of the method, its rationale, and an analysis of the experimental results, including addressing limitations and providing case studies.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited scope of CSUs:**  While the paper focuses on polysemous, domain-specific, and culturally-specific CSUs, there might be other types of context-sensitive units that could be considered. This is explicitly acknowledged as a limitation.
    *   **Hyperparameter sensitivity:** The method involves a hyperparameter *k* (maximum number of challenging words), and while analyzed, its optimal value may vary depending on the dataset and language pair. The impact and method to estimate k automatically could be further explored.
    *   **Modest performance improvements on Llama3-8B:** Improvements on stronger base models like Llama3-8B were more modest.  This suggests that the gains from DFA might diminish as LLMs become more inherently capable of handling semantic ambiguity, and that DFA might not scale well.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The work demonstrates a simple yet effective way to improve LLM-based MT without extensive training. The approach highlights the importance of guiding LLMs' attention to specific challenges within the input.
    *   The method can be easily integrated into existing LLM-based MT pipelines, making it a practical solution.
    *   The analysis of different types of CSUs provides valuable insights for future research on improving LLM's understanding of context.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of LLM-based machine translation. The novelty lies in the dynamic focus anchoring approach, which is both resource-efficient and empirically effective. The method addresses a real problem and offers insights into improving LLMs' understanding of context.  While the performance gains on stronger models are modest, the simplicity and efficiency of the approach make it a valuable addition to the toolkit for LLM-based MT. The analysis is thorough and the limitations are acknowledged.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Implicit Inversion turns CLIP into a Decoder
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23161v1)
- **Authors**: Antonio D'Orazio, Maria Rosaria Briglia, Donato Crisostomi, Dario Loi, Emanuele Rodolà, Iacopo Masi
- **Abstract**: CLIP is a discriminative model trained to align images and text in a shared embedding space. Due to its multimodal structure, it serves as the backbone of many generative pipelines, where a decoder is trained to map from the shared space back to images. In this work, we show that image synthesis is nevertheless possible using CLIP alone -- without any decoder, training, or fine-tuning. Our approach optimizes a frequency-aware implicit neural representation that encourages coarse-to-fine generation by stratifying frequencies across network layers. To stabilize this inverse mapping, we introduce adversarially robust initialization, a lightweight Orthogonal Procrustes projection to align local text and image embeddings, and a blending loss that anchors outputs to natural image statistics. Without altering CLIP's weights, this framework unlocks capabilities such as text-to-image generation, style transfer, and image reconstruction. These findings suggest that discriminative models may hold untapped generative potential, hidden in plain sight.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CLIP-1, a novel approach to text-to-image generation that repurposes the CLIP (Contrastive Language-Image Pre-training) model *without* requiring a separate decoder, fine-tuning, or additional training. CLIP is typically used as a discriminative model. CLIP-1 leverages Implicit Neural Representations (INRs) optimized via a frequency-aware strategy to achieve coarse-to-fine image generation. It addresses the "modality gap" between text and image embeddings through Orthogonal Procrustes alignment and enforces natural image statistics using a blending loss.  The paper demonstrates the approach's effectiveness on text-to-image generation, style transfer, image reconstruction, and controlled image editing.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The central claim of performing text-to-image generation *without* a dedicated decoder or training is indeed novel. Existing methods often rely on GANs or diffusion models as decoders, or require some form of fine-tuning of CLIP itself. The use of INRs for this purpose is also a distinct methodological contribution. A key distinction from prior work lies in the optimization of INRs rather than direct pixel-space optimization, reducing artifacts. The modality gap alignment via Procrustes and frequency-aware optimization are valuable additions.

*   **Significance:** The significance hinges on the performance and versatility achieved despite the simplicity of the approach (no decoder, no training). Demonstrating competitive results in text-to-image generation, while also showcasing zero-shot transfer to image reconstruction, style transfer, and controlled editing, is compelling. The discovery of a significant generative capability inside CLIP itself, untapped until this work, is interesting. This may open up new avenues of research for leveraging discriminative models for generative tasks. The exploration of INR frequency-specific optimization is a practical technique that addresses INR's traditional shortfalls.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Decoder-free and training-free approach simplifies the text-to-image pipeline.
    *   INRs offer a compact and efficient representation for optimization.
    *   Orthogonal Procrustes alignment bridges the modality gap effectively.
    *   The framework transfers well to various downstream tasks.
    * The proposed methods produce detailed and semantically aligned images in comparison to prior works

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the results are promising, the visual quality may not consistently reach the state-of-the-art achieved by more complex, decoder-based methods like diffusion models (although this is stated and acknowledged). There may be some limitations in the intricacy of the scenes produced.
    *   The paper relies on a good initialization for the INR, which is obtained by searching over a pre-trained dataset of image-caption pairs. This reliance on a pre-trained dataset could limit the generalizability of the approach to novel domains.

*   **Justification for Score:** The paper's novelty and significance are substantial, particularly given the minimalist approach. The experimental results showcase a surprising and useful generative capacity hidden within CLIP.  However, the dependence on CLIP's representations is a limitation that must be considered when comparing against other generative models. While the visuals appear competitive compared to related methods such as DAS and CLIPAG, the authors' visual comparisons could be stronger to fully assess the approach. Also, the approach would greatly benefit from a more thorough comparative quantitative analysis and thorough ablations. Despite the clear potential, further research would be needed to see if this approach can be applied to the open world.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Infinite-Instruct: Synthesizing Scaling Code instruction Data with Bidirectional Synthesis and Static Verification
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23177v1)
- **Authors**: Wenjing Xing, Wenke Lu, Yeheng Duan, Bing Zhao, Zhenghui kang, Yaolong Wang, Kai Gao, Lei Qiao
- **Abstract**: Traditional code instruction data synthesis methods suffer from limited diversity and poor logic. We introduce Infinite-Instruct, an automated framework for synthesizing high-quality question-answer pairs, designed to enhance the code generation capabilities of large language models (LLMs). The framework focuses on improving the internal logic of synthesized problems and the quality of synthesized code. First, "Reverse Construction" transforms code snippets into diverse programming problems. Then, through "Backfeeding Construction," keywords in programming problems are structured into a knowledge graph to reconstruct them into programming problems with stronger internal logic. Finally, a cross-lingual static code analysis pipeline filters invalid samples to ensure data quality. Experiments show that on mainstream code generation benchmarks, our fine-tuned models achieve an average performance improvement of 21.70% on 7B-parameter models and 36.95% on 32B-parameter models. Using less than one-tenth of the instruction fine-tuning data, we achieved performance comparable to the Qwen-2.5-Coder-Instruct. Infinite-Instruct provides a scalable solution for LLM training in programming. We open-source the datasets used in the experiments, including both unfiltered versions and filtered versions via static analysis. The data are available at https://github.com/xingwenjing417/Infinite-Instruct-dataset
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Infinite-Instruct: Synthesizing Scaling Code instruction Data with Bidirectional Synthesis and Static Verification."

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Infinite-Instruct, a new framework for automatically generating high-quality code instruction datasets. Addressing the limitations of existing methods (limited diversity, poor logic, and inadequate quality assurance), Infinite-Instruct utilizes two complementary mechanisms: Reverse Construction (transforming code snippets into programming problems) and Backfeeding Construction (building a knowledge graph to structure keywords into programming tasks).  The framework incorporates a cross-lingual static code analysis pipeline to filter invalid samples. Experiments show that fine-tuning models with data generated by Infinite-Instruct improves performance on code generation benchmarks, achieving significant gains with a fraction of the data compared to other methods. The authors also release their generated datasets, both filtered and unfiltered.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's core novelty lies in its bidirectional approach, combining Reverse Construction (code to problem) with Backfeeding Construction (keywords to problem).  While OSS-Instruct (from which it extends) already leveraged code snippets, Infinite-Instruct adds a more structured approach to problem generation and a more robust quality control mechanism. The knowledge-graph-enhanced backfeeding is a valuable addition not seen before, and the application of sophisticated static code analysis tools is a practical and important contribution. While neither of these elements is groundbreaking individually, their combined effect and targeted application to code instruction data synthesis is novel.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper stems from its ability to generate high-quality instruction data at scale, a crucial bottleneck for training large language models for code. The experimental results are compelling, showing substantial performance improvements compared to fine-tuning with existing datasets. The ability to achieve comparable results with a fraction of the data is particularly significant, highlighting the efficiency of the generated data. It provides a potentially scalable solution for LLM training in programming. The release of the datasets makes the work immediately accessible and usable by other researchers.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Bidirectional approach:** Combining code and keyword based synthesis offers a more diverse and potentially higher quality data generation process.
    *   **Knowledge Graph:** The addition of knowledge graphs leads to a more structured and logical dataset.
    *   **Static analysis filtering:** Rigorous code quality filtering is essential, and the paper demonstrates the effectiveness of its pipeline.
    *   **Empirical validation:** Experiments on standard benchmarks clearly demonstrate the efficacy of the approach.
    *   **Data release:** The authors make their data publicly available which is of great benefit to the community.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Difficulty adjustment mechanism:** The paper acknowledges the simplistic difficulty adjustment, which relies primarily on keyword count. This is a limitation and could lead to less nuanced and potentially less effective datasets.
    *   **Static analysis limitations:** As noted by the authors, static analysis cannot catch all errors, especially logical or algorithmic issues.  This means there could still be problems in the generated code that are not caught by the filter.
    *   **Imbalanced multi-language support:** The different levels of support for different languages are a valid concern. Future work may need to address this imbalance.
    *   **Reliance on GPT-4 for quality and difficulty:** The process relies on GPT-4 as the engine for evaluation. The inherent biases of such foundation models are still present despite their ability in language understanding.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by providing a practical and effective method for generating code instruction data. The bidirectional approach and static analysis pipeline could serve as a blueprint for future research in this area. The open-source datasets are also a valuable resource that can accelerate progress in LLM training for code.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The paper presents a valuable, novel, and practical contribution to the field of code instruction data synthesis. It addresses a critical bottleneck in LLM training and demonstrates impressive empirical results. The bidirectional approach, the knowledge-graph integration and the static analysis pipeline are important strengths. While there are limitations regarding difficulty adjustment, dependence on foundational model and the ability of static analysis to detect all errors as well as limitations with multi-language support, the overall contribution is substantial and well-validated, therefore a score of 7.5 is justified.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### DIP-R1: Deep Inspection and Perception with RL Looking Through and Understanding Complex Scenes
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23179v1)
- **Authors**: Sungjune Park, Hyunjun Kim, Junho Kim, Seongho Kim, Yong Man Ro
- **Abstract**: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have demonstrated significant visual understanding capabilities, yet their fine-grained visual perception in complex real-world scenarios, such as densely crowded public areas, remains limited. Inspired by the recent success of reinforcement learning (RL) in both LLMs and MLLMs, in this paper, we explore how RL can enhance visual perception ability of MLLMs. Then we develop a novel RL-based framework, Deep Inspection and Perception with RL (DIP-R1) designed to enhance the visual perception capabilities of MLLMs, by comprehending complex scenes and looking through visual instances closely. DIP-R1 guides MLLMs through detailed inspection of visual scene via three simply designed rule-based reward modelings. First, we adopt a standard reasoning reward encouraging the model to include three step-by-step processes: 1) reasoning for understanding visual scenes, 2) observing for looking through interested but ambiguous regions, and 3) decision-making for predicting answer. Second, a variance-guided looking reward is designed to examine uncertain regions for the second observing process. It explicitly enables the model to inspect ambiguous areas, improving its ability to mitigate perceptual uncertainties. Third, we model a weighted precision-recall accuracy reward enhancing accurate decision-making. We explore its effectiveness across diverse fine-grained object detection data consisting of challenging real-world environments, such as densely crowded scenes. Built upon existing MLLMs, DIP-R1 achieves consistent and significant improvement across various in-domain and out-of-domain scenarios. It also outperforms various existing baseline models and supervised fine-tuning methods. Our findings highlight the substantial potential of integrating RL into MLLMs for enhancing capabilities in complex real-world perception tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "DIP-R1: Deep Inspection and Perception with RL Looking Through and Understanding Complex Scenes" introduces a novel Reinforcement Learning (RL)-based framework designed to enhance the visual perception capabilities of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs), particularly in densely crowded real-world environments. The framework, called DIP-R1, guides MLLMs through a detailed inspection process using three rule-based reward modelings: a reasoning reward, a variance-guided looking reward, and a weighted precision-recall accuracy reward.  The reasoning reward encourages step-by-step problem-solving, while the variance-guided reward directs the model's attention to ambiguous regions, and the weighted precision-recall reward promotes accurate decision-making.  The authors demonstrate DIP-R1's effectiveness across challenging real-world object detection datasets, showing improvements in both in-domain and out-of-domain scenarios compared to existing MLLMs and supervised fine-tuning approaches.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the specific combination of RL with three relatively simple, yet effective, reward structures to improve MLLM's fine-grained visual perception.  While RL has been used previously to improve MLLM reasoning and vision tasks, DIP-R1's emphasis on guided inspection with variance-aware attention and a precision-recall reward for object detection in crowded scenes represents a distinct approach. The idea of using the inherent uncertainty (variance) within MLLM's predictions as a signal for guiding attention during the "looking" phase is a key innovative element.

*   **Significance:**  The work addresses a critical limitation of current MLLMs: their difficulty in perceiving and distinguishing individual objects in complex, crowded environments. By improving the ability to understand and inspect such scenes, DIP-R1 has the potential to enable MLLMs in various real-world applications, such as public safety, crowd management, and autonomous driving.  The experimental results demonstrate that DIP-R1 achieves significant improvements over established baselines. The study provides a convincing demonstration of the potential benefits of integrating RL into MLLMs for enhanced visual perception, opening avenues for future research.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-designed and easily interpretable reward functions.
    *   Thorough experimental evaluation on diverse datasets.
    *   Significant performance improvements over baselines.
    *   The approach is relatively simple and practical, which facilitates implementation.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The reward functions are rule-based, which might limit the adaptability and scalability of the framework to more complex tasks. Other reward functions could be designed and compared in the future.
    *   Limited ablation studies. While the authors present ablation studies, they could be more comprehensive.
    *   The computational cost of DIP-R1 is not explicitly discussed, given that it relies on iteratively sampling group responses.
    *   The MLLMs is trained on one dataset only.
* **Potential Influence:** The paper could inspire further research in:
   * Exploring more sophisticated RL techniques for visual perception in MLLMs.
   * Developing adaptive and learnable reward functions for guided inspection.
   * Applying DIP-R1 or similar frameworks to other multimodal tasks beyond object detection.
   * Investigating the interplay between visual perception and reasoning in MLLMs.

**Justification for Score:**
While the individual components of DIP-R1 (RL, attention mechanisms, and precision-recall metrics) are not entirely novel in isolation, their specific combination and application to the problem of fine-grained object detection in crowded scenes, guided by a variance-aware looking process, constitute a notable contribution. The experimental results are solid, demonstrating that DIP-R1 outperforms existing MLLMs. The clear presentation and well-defined problem statement further contribute to the paper's value.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Unsupervised Word-level Quality Estimation for Machine Translation Through the Lens of Annotators (Dis)agreement
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23183v1)
- **Authors**: Gabriele Sarti, Vilém Zouhar, Malvina Nissim, Arianna Bisazza
- **Abstract**: Word-level quality estimation (WQE) aims to automatically identify fine-grained error spans in machine-translated outputs and has found many uses, including assisting translators during post-editing. Modern WQE techniques are often expensive, involving prompting of large language models or ad-hoc training on large amounts of human-labeled data. In this work, we investigate efficient alternatives exploiting recent advances in language model interpretability and uncertainty quantification to identify translation errors from the inner workings of translation models. In our evaluation spanning 14 metrics across 12 translation directions, we quantify the impact of human label variation on metric performance by using multiple sets of human labels. Our results highlight the untapped potential of unsupervised metrics, the shortcomings of supervised methods when faced with label uncertainty, and the brittleness of single-annotator evaluation practices.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates unsupervised word-level quality estimation (WQE) for machine translation. It explores the use of language model interpretability and uncertainty quantification methods to identify translation errors, avoiding the cost of large language model (LLM) prompting or ad-hoc training on human-labeled data. The study evaluates 10 unsupervised metrics across 12 translation directions, examining the impact of human label variation on metric performance. It concludes that unsupervised metrics have untapped potential, highlights shortcomings of supervised methods due to label uncertainty, and critiques single-annotator evaluation practices. The authors also offer recommendations for improving WQE systems, particularly focusing on using multiple human annotations and employing in-distribution calibration.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its systematic evaluation of unsupervised WQE metrics across a diverse set of languages, models, and *multiple* annotation sets. While individual unsupervised metrics themselves aren't necessarily new, the extensive comparison and, crucially, the focus on the *impact of annotator disagreement* on WQE metric performance is a significant contribution. This highlights the often-overlooked issue of human label variation in WQE.  The analysis demonstrating the inadequacy of default, commonly used supervised methods (XCOMET) and providing a fix through confidence weighting is also a notable finding.

* **Significance:** The significance of this work is multi-faceted:
    * **Efficiency:** It demonstrates the potential of efficient, unsupervised methods for WQE, addressing the resource limitations that often hinder MT quality assessment, especially in low-resource settings.
    * **Robustness:** It raises important concerns about the reliability of WQE evaluations that rely on single-annotator data. By quantifying the impact of label uncertainty, it encourages a more nuanced and robust approach to WQE evaluation using multiple annotators.
    * **Calibration:** It highlights that widely used supervised methods, if not correctly calibrated, are outperformed by simpler approaches.
    * **Actionable Recommendations:** It provides actionable recommendations for the community, including the importance of using annotator agreement as a control, emphasizing calibration, and employing visualizations to convey confidence levels.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The breadth of the evaluation, spanning multiple languages, models, metrics, and annotation sets, strengthens the validity and generalizability of the findings.
    * **Focus on Human Factors:**  The explicit focus on the impact of human annotator disagreement is a key strength and offers a more realistic assessment of WQE performance.
    * **Clear Research Questions:**  The research questions are well-defined and addressed thoroughly throughout the paper.
    * **Actionable Recommendations:** The paper provides practical recommendations based on the findings.
    * **Attention to Methodology:** The methodology clearly describes extraction of the metrics and the use of force decoding which addresses potential confounders in the evaluation.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Theoretical Innovation:** The paper focuses more on empirical evaluation than on introducing entirely new theoretical frameworks or algorithms for WQE. While the practical implications are significant, the theoretical contribution is less substantial.
    * **Dependency on Existing Metrics:**  The study evaluates existing unsupervised metrics rather than developing fundamentally novel metrics tailored for WQE. The derived metrics are still based on information available in the model, and are limited by the model's accuracy.
    * **Specific Data Sources:** The evaluation is limited to open-source MT models and publicly available datasets.  While this ensures reproducibility, expanding the evaluation to proprietary systems and more diverse data sources would further strengthen the conclusions.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in WQE by:
    * **Shifting Focus:** Encouraging a greater emphasis on unsupervised methods and the consideration of human label variation in WQE evaluations.
    * **Improving Evaluation Practices:**  Promoting more rigorous and robust evaluation methodologies for WQE, incorporating multiple annotations and in-distribution calibration.
    * **Guiding Metric Selection:** Providing insights into the relative performance of different unsupervised metrics and highlighting the importance of confidence weighting in supervised models.

Overall, this is a well-executed empirical study that addresses an important problem in machine translation quality assessment. While the theoretical contribution is modest, the systematic evaluation, insightful analysis of human factors, and practical recommendations make this a valuable contribution to the field.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**
A score of 7.5 reflects the paper's valuable empirical contribution, particularly its comprehensive evaluation of unsupervised WQE metrics and its highlighting of the importance of human annotation variability. It's penalized slightly (kept from being higher than 8) due to its relatively lower theoretical innovation, the dependency on existing metrics and models, and the reliance on a specific set of data sources.  The actionable recommendations based on the results justify the 7.5. The use of multiple annotators and the attention to calibration of existing methods is a positive addition.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Two Is Better Than One: Rotations Scale LoRAs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23184v1)
- **Authors**: Hongcan Guo, Guoshun Nan, Yuan Yang, Diyang Zhang, Haotian Li, Zhican Chen, Qinchuan Zhou, Yuhan Ran, Xinye Cao, Sicong Leng, Xiaofeng Tao, Xudong Jiang
- **Abstract**: Scaling Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA)-based Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) facilitates large language models (LLMs) to efficiently adapt to diverse tasks. However, traditional gating mechanisms that route inputs to the best experts may fundamentally hinder LLMs' scalability, leading to poor generalization and underfitting issues. We identify that the root cause lies in the restricted expressiveness of existing weighted-sum mechanisms, both within and outside the convex cone of LoRA representations. This motivates us to propose RadarGate, a novel geometrically inspired gating method that introduces rotational operations of LoRAs representations to boost the expressiveness and facilitate richer feature interactions among multiple LoRAs for scalable LLMs. Specifically, we first fuse each LoRA representation to other LoRAs using a learnable component and then feed the output to a rotation matrix. This matrix involves learnable parameters that define the relative angular relationship between LoRA representations. Such a simple yet effective mechanism provides an extra degree of freedom, facilitating the learning of cross-LoRA synergies and properly tracking the challenging poor generalization and underfitting issues as the number of LoRA grows. Extensive experiments on 6 public benchmarks across 21 tasks show the effectiveness of our RadarGate for scaling LoRAs. We also provide valuable insights, revealing that the rotations to each pair of representations are contrastive, encouraging closer alignment of semantically similar representations during geometrical transformation while pushing distance ones further apart. We will release our code to the community.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenges of scaling Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA)-based Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) for large language models (LLMs).  It identifies that traditional gating mechanisms in LoRA-MoE architectures, which rely on weighted sums of expert outputs, restrict the expressiveness of the model and can lead to poor generalization and underfitting as the number of LoRA experts increases. To overcome this limitation, the authors propose RadarGate, a novel gating method that introduces rotational operations on LoRA representations before the weighted summation. RadarGate uses a RotationGate to learn angular relationships between LoRA representations, followed by a StretchGate to scale their magnitudes. The rotation allows for feature interactions beyond the convex cone of the original LoRA representations, enhancing expressiveness and enabling better adaptation to diverse tasks. The paper presents extensive experimental results across several benchmarks demonstrating the effectiveness of RadarGate in scaling LoRA-MoE models, showing improvements in fitting, generalization, and stability.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of incorporating rotational operations into the gating mechanism of LoRA-MoE is relatively novel. Existing methods largely rely on weighted combinations of LoRA outputs or more complex adaptive routing schemes without explicit geometric transformations like rotations. This geometric perspective on gating and the use of rotations to enhance feature interaction are a significant contribution. The key innovation is the idea of allowing LoRA representations to interact in a way that isn't simply a weighted sum of their individual outputs, but a learned *combination* of their features through rotation. While concepts like MOLE and adaptive routing exist, this method tackles the convex cone limitation directly using geometry.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial problem in scaling LLMs, namely, adapting to diverse tasks in a parameter-efficient manner. LoRA and MoE are promising approaches, but their combination faces limitations. Overcoming these scalability barriers is significant for deploying LLMs in real-world scenarios. The experimental results demonstrating performance improvements in fitting and generalization, coupled with the analysis of the rotational operations, contribute meaningfully to the understanding and advancement of LoRA-MoE architectures. The analysis of contrastive rotations for closer alignment and pushing apart semantically closer representations adds a nuanced value.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the problem of limited expressiveness and its consequences on generalization and underfitting in scaled LoRA-MoE models.
    *   **Novel Method:** The proposed RadarGate is a novel and well-motivated approach to address the identified limitations.
    *   **Theoretical Justification:** The paper provides theoretical insights using lemmas to explain how RadarGate improves fitting and generalization, lending credibility to the approach.
    *   **Extensive Experiments:**  The experimental evaluation is comprehensive, covering multiple benchmarks, architectures, and ablation studies, making the results convincing.
    *   **Insightful Analysis:**  The analysis of rotation operations and their impact on feature alignment provides valuable insights.
    *   **Code Availability:** The authors intend to release the code, which will facilitate adoption and further research.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Complexity:** The rotation calculation, though presented with low-rank approximations, adds to the overall complexity of the model. This might raise practical implementation barriers.
    *   **Limited Ablation:** While the authors do an ablation study, they could have done more to separate the effects of different rotation matrices or angle initialization.
    *   **Real-world Scenarios:** While the datasets are common, it would be valuable to test on very large and diverse datasets to confirm the robustness of RadarGate in a production setting, or real-world multi-task scenarios.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the design of future LoRA-MoE architectures. The concept of using geometric transformations to enhance feature interaction could inspire new approaches to gating and expert selection. If RadarGate proves robust in diverse real-world settings, it could become a standard component in scalable LLM adaptation pipelines.

**Overall:** The paper makes a significant contribution to the field of parameter-efficient LLM adaptation. It introduces a novel and well-justified method for scaling LoRA-MoE models, supported by solid theoretical arguments and extensive experimental results. While there are some minor weaknesses, the strengths outweigh them, making this a valuable contribution to the community.

### HiGarment: Cross-modal Harmony Based Diffusion Model for Flat Sketch to Realistic Garment Image
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23186v1)
- **Authors**: Junyi Guo, Jingxuan Zhang, Fangyu Wu, Huanda Lu, Qiufeng Wang, Wenmian Yang, Eng Gee Lim, Dongming Lu
- **Abstract**: Diffusion-based garment synthesis tasks primarily focus on the design phase in the fashion domain, while the garment production process remains largely underexplored. To bridge this gap, we introduce a new task: Flat Sketch to Realistic Garment Image (FS2RG), which generates realistic garment images by integrating flat sketches and textual guidance. FS2RG presents two key challenges: 1) fabric characteristics are solely guided by textual prompts, providing insufficient visual supervision for diffusion-based models, which limits their ability to capture fine-grained fabric details; 2) flat sketches and textual guidance may provide conflicting information, requiring the model to selectively preserve or modify garment attributes while maintaining structural coherence. To tackle this task, we propose HiGarment, a novel framework that comprises two core components: i) a multi-modal semantic enhancement mechanism that enhances fabric representation across textual and visual modalities, and ii) a harmonized cross-attention mechanism that dynamically balances information from flat sketches and text prompts, allowing controllable synthesis by generating either sketch-aligned (image-biased) or text-guided (text-biased) outputs. Furthermore, we collect Multi-modal Detailed Garment, the largest open-source dataset for garment generation. Experimental results and user studies demonstrate the effectiveness of HiGarment in garment synthesis. The code and dataset will be released.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "HiGarment: Cross-modal Harmony Based Diffusion Model for Flat Sketch to Realistic Garment Image":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a new task, Flat Sketch to Realistic Garment Image (FS2RG), where the goal is to generate realistic garment images from flat sketches and text prompts.  This task aims to bridge the gap between garment design and production, offering a way to generate production-ready prototypes. To address the challenges of fabric detail representation and potential conflicts between sketch and text, the authors propose HiGarment. This framework comprises a multi-modal semantic enhancement mechanism (MSE) to improve fabric representation and a harmonized cross-attention mechanism (HCA) to balance information from sketches and text. The authors also present a new dataset, MMDGarment, which includes garment images, text annotations, flat sketches, and a fabric vector database. Experimental results and user studies demonstrate the effectiveness of HiGarment.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper offers novelty in several aspects:

    *   **New Task (FS2RG):** The FS2RG task is a significant contribution as it directly addresses the needs of garment production, a less explored area in garment synthesis research. It shifts the focus from pure design to production feasibility.
    *   **HiGarment Architecture:** The proposed HiGarment architecture, with its MSE and HCA components, is novel.  MSE uses a fabric database and Q-Formers to enhance fabric representation, which is clever. HCA offers a way to balance visual and textual inputs.
    *   **MMDGarment Dataset:**  The dataset, MMDGarment, fills a void in the research community, providing a well-annotated multi-modal dataset for garment synthesis.
*   **Significance:**

    *   **Practical Application:** The FS2RG task has a clear practical application in garment production, potentially streamlining prototyping and reducing waste.
    *   **Technical Contribution:** The HiGarment architecture contributes to the broader field of multi-modal image generation by addressing the challenges of modality harmony and fine-grained attribute control.
    *   **Dataset Impact:** The MMDGarment dataset can serve as a benchmark for future research in garment synthesis and multi-modal learning.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly defines the FS2RG task and articulates its importance.
    *   **Well-Designed Architecture:** The HiGarment architecture is well-motivated and addresses the specific challenges of the task. The components (MSE and HCA) are explained clearly.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper includes both qualitative and quantitative evaluations, along with ablation studies and a user study.
    *   **Dataset Contribution:** The release of the MMDGarment dataset is a valuable contribution to the research community.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Improvement:**  While the components are novel, the improvement over existing methods like IP-Adapter and Uni-ControlNet, even if consistent, might be considered somewhat incremental rather than groundbreaking, and it would be good to address this in the paper.
    *   **Limited Generalizability:** The focus is very specific to garment synthesis. It would be valuable to discuss the potential generalizability of the MSE and HCA components to other multi-modal image generation tasks.
    *   **Tiny Logos:** The paper mentions the difficulty in preserving tiny logos, pointing to an inherent limitation. More analysis of *why* this limitation exists and potential solutions would be helpful.
    *   **Resource Limitations:** The authors mention limited evaluation comparisons with certain baselines due to resource constraints, this weakens the claims, to a certain extent.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to influence research in multi-modal image generation and garment synthesis. The MMDGarment dataset is particularly useful and it also provides insights into how to balance visual and textual information during synthesis.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper makes a significant contribution by introducing a novel task and providing a well-designed solution. The release of the MMDGarment dataset is valuable. While the improvements over existing methods might be considered incremental, the overall impact of the paper is considerable, particularly its focus on practical application and filling in dataset gaps.
