    *   **Lack of Comparison to More Recent Methods:** The field of sparse attention and efficient LLMs is rapidly evolving. Comparing AnchorAttention to very recent methods would strengthen the paper's claims of superiority.
    *   **Ablation on different data/tasks:** The evaluation of ablations is conducted on one context length/dataset (128k/RULER), which does not present a complete picture. It is unclear whether the same observation can be made for other contexts.
    *   **Potential Overfitting:** The reported hyperparameter tuning may lead to overfitting on the tested datasets. Results may degrade for unseen datasets or tasks.
    *   **Scalability Challenges:** While speedup is evident, the extent of performance degradation at higher throughput when compared to Full Attention is absent.
    *   **Computational Cost of Hyperparameter Tuning:** There is no discussion of the overhead associated with finding optimal hyperparameter values, specifically the choice of *θ* and the `step` size. In practice, hyperparameter tuning can be quite costly, thus limiting adoption.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   AnchorAttention could influence future research on sparse attention mechanisms by introducing a new approach to identifying critical attention regions.
    *   The stripe granularity and discrete KV loading could be adopted and adapted by other researchers to further improve efficiency.
    *   The paper encourages a move away from purely local information and towards more global contextual awareness in sparsity strategies.

**Justification for the Score:**

The paper presents a novel and promising sparse attention mechanism with demonstrated efficiency gains. However, the limited model generalization, the lack of comparison to very recent methods, and the potential for overfitting detract from its significance. The method appears useful, but the evaluation section has room for improvement. Overall, the approach is promising and could lead to future improvements. A middle-ground score seems appropriate.

**Score: 6.5**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### OmniEarth-Bench: Towards Holistic Evaluation of Earth's Six Spheres and Cross-Spheres Interactions with Multimodal Observational Earth Data
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23522v1)
- **Authors**: Fengxiang Wang, Mingshuo Chen, Xuming He, YiFan Zhang, Feng Liu, Zijie Guo, Zhenghao Hu, Jiong Wang, Jingyi Xu, Zhangrui Li, Fenghua Ling, Ben Fei, Weijia Li, Long Lan, Wenjing Yang, Wenlong Zhang, Lei Bai
- **Abstract**: Existing benchmarks for Earth science multimodal learning exhibit critical limitations in systematic coverage of geosystem components and cross-sphere interactions, often constrained to isolated subsystems (only in Human-activities sphere or atmosphere) with limited evaluation dimensions (less than 16 tasks). To address these gaps, we introduce OmniEarth-Bench, the first comprehensive multimodal benchmark spanning all six Earth science spheres (atmosphere, lithosphere, Oceansphere, cryosphere, biosphere and Human-activities sphere) and cross-spheres with one hundred expert-curated evaluation dimensions. Leveraging observational data from satellite sensors and in-situ measurements, OmniEarth-Bench integrates 29,779 annotations across four tiers: perception, general reasoning, scientific knowledge reasoning and chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning. This involves the efforts of 2-5 experts per sphere to establish authoritative evaluation dimensions and curate relevant observational datasets, 40 crowd-sourcing annotators to assist experts for annotations, and finally, OmniEarth-Bench is validated via hybrid expert-crowd workflows to reduce label ambiguity. Experiments on 9 state-of-the-art MLLMs reveal that even the most advanced models struggle with our benchmarks, where none of them reach 35\% accuracy. Especially, in some cross-spheres tasks, the performance of leading models like GPT-4o drops to 0.0\%. OmniEarth-Bench sets a new standard for geosystem-aware AI, advancing both scientific discovery and practical applications in environmental monitoring and disaster prediction. The dataset, source code, and trained models were released.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the "OmniEarth-Bench" paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces OmniEarth-Bench, a new multimodal benchmark designed to holistically evaluate the performance of multimodal learning models (MLLMs) in Earth science. Unlike existing benchmarks that primarily focus on specific domains (e.g., human activities, atmosphere) and have limited evaluation dimensions, OmniEarth-Bench covers all six Earth science spheres (atmosphere, lithosphere, oceansphere, cryosphere, biosphere, and human activities) and cross-sphere interactions. The benchmark includes 100 expert-curated evaluation dimensions, leveraging observational data from satellite sensors and in-situ measurements. The evaluation is structured across four tiers: perception, general reasoning, expert-knowledge deductive reasoning, and chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning. Experiments using state-of-the-art MLLMs demonstrate that even the most advanced models struggle with the benchmark, highlighting limitations in their understanding of geosystems.  The authors aim to advance both scientific discovery and practical applications in environmental monitoring and disaster prediction through this benchmark.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the comprehensive coverage of all six Earth science spheres and cross-sphere interactions within a single multimodal benchmark. Existing benchmarks are often limited in scope, focusing on specific subsystems. The inclusion of CoT reasoning for Earth science tasks is also a notable contribution.

*   **Significance:**  The significance of OmniEarth-Bench stems from its potential to drive research and development in geosystem-aware AI.  The benchmark highlights the limitations of existing MLLMs in understanding complex Earth science data and reasoning about intricate inter-sphere interactions. By providing a challenging and diverse evaluation platform, OmniEarth-Bench could stimulate the development of more specialized and effective models for environmental monitoring, disaster prediction, and other crucial applications. The meticulous expert curation and cross-validation also add to the credibility and potential long-term impact of the benchmark. The inclusion of data sourced directly from satellites is valuable.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Coverage:** Extends beyond existing benchmarks by encompassing all six Earth science spheres.
    *   **Expert Curation:** Expert involvement in defining evaluation dimensions, curating datasets, and annotating samples ensures the quality and relevance of the benchmark.
    *   **Challenging Evaluation Dimensions:** Includes a diverse range of tasks, from basic perception to complex reasoning and CoT.
    *   **Real-world Relevance:** Leverages observational data from satellite sensors and in-situ measurements.
    *   **Public Availability:** The dataset, source code, and trained models are publicly available, facilitating widespread adoption and research.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Data Acquisition Costs:**  The high cost and difficulty of data acquisition are acknowledged, which limit the number of dimensions covered per Earth sphere (e.g., currently only eight dimensions for the Cryosphere). This restricts the detail and the thoroughness of a specific sphere evaluation.
    *   **Performance Gap:** The low performance of existing MLLMs on the benchmark might discourage some researchers initially, but the potential for improvement is a powerful incentive.
    *   **Potential Reliance on Specialized Models**: If specialized models perform far better than generic MLLMs, the usefulness of a single comprehensive benchmark diminishes, but is mitigated by the paper's potential to facilitate future research in geo-specific AI.
    *   **Subjectivity in Expert Annotations**: Expert annotations, while generally more accurate, are influenced by the individual expert's biases in reasoning and assessment. More details on the mitigation of this aspect of annotation would add credibility to the benchmark.
    *  **Dataset Balance & Diversity**: The long tail phenomenon in rare events or anomalies are difficult to capture without large datasets, and that MLLMs struggle is highlighted as a key takeaway in the study. A future version of OmniEarth-Bench may need to be augmented with synthetically generated data of rare events to stress test the MLLMs further.

*   **Potential Influence:**  OmniEarth-Bench has the potential to become a standard benchmark for evaluating MLLMs in Earth science, driving progress in areas such as environmental monitoring, disaster prediction, and climate modeling. Its comprehensive nature could also encourage the development of more holistic and interdisciplinary AI models for Earth system science. The benchmark may also facilitate more effective transfer learning from larger models with generic knowledge of Earth Systems.

**Justification for Score:**

OmniEarth-Bench represents a significant contribution to the field, addressing a critical gap in the evaluation of MLLMs for Earth science. Its comprehensiveness, expert curation, and challenging evaluation dimensions make it a valuable resource for researchers. While the limited coverage per sphere and relatively low performance of existing models represent some weaknesses, the benchmark's potential influence and the authors' commitment to public availability justify a high score.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Normalizing Flows are Capable Models for RL
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23527v1)
- **Authors**: Raj Ghugare, Benjamin Eysenbach
- **Abstract**: Modern reinforcement learning (RL) algorithms have found success by using powerful probabilistic models, such as transformers, energy-based models, and diffusion/flow-based models. To this end, RL researchers often choose to pay the price of accommodating these models into their algorithms -- diffusion models are expressive, but are computationally intensive due to their reliance on solving differential equations, while autoregressive transformer models are scalable but typically require learning discrete representations. Normalizing flows (NFs), by contrast, seem to provide an appealing alternative, as they enable likelihoods and sampling without solving differential equations or autoregressive architectures. However, their potential in RL has received limited attention, partly due to the prevailing belief that normalizing flows lack sufficient expressivity. We show that this is not the case. Building on recent work in NFs, we propose a single NF architecture which integrates seamlessly into RL algorithms, serving as a policy, Q-function, and occupancy measure. Our approach leads to much simpler algorithms, and achieves higher performance in imitation learning, offline, goal conditioned RL and unsupervised RL.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Normalizing Flows are Capable Models for RL":

**Summary:**

The paper argues that Normalizing Flows (NFs) are underexplored yet highly capable probabilistic models for reinforcement learning (RL). It challenges the prevailing belief that NFs lack sufficient expressiveness compared to models like transformers and diffusion models. The authors propose a simple NF architecture based on existing techniques (coupling layers and linear flows) and demonstrate its effectiveness across a range of RL settings, including imitation learning, offline RL, goal-conditioned RL, and unsupervised RL. Their experiments show that NFs can achieve competitive or even superior performance to more complex models while being computationally simpler and easier to integrate into existing RL algorithms. The paper highlights NFs' ability to efficiently compute likelihoods, sample complex distributions, and be trained with both maximum likelihood estimation (MLE) and variational inference (VI) as key advantages.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**  The paper doesn't present radically new NF *architectures*. The core NF building blocks are based on existing work (RealNVP, GLOW elements). The novelty lies primarily in its application and evaluation across a diverse set of RL problems, demonstrating NFs as a universally applicable and efficient model within the RL context. It's a systematic exploration and application of existing NFs to RL. Showing that a relatively simple NF architecture can rival or even surpass state-of-the-art methods, particularly when considering computational efficiency, contributes a fresh perspective.

**Significance:**  The significance stems from its potential to simplify RL algorithm design. Current trends often favor increasingly complex probabilistic models, which can be computationally expensive and challenging to train. This paper demonstrates that a simpler, more efficient alternative (NF) can achieve comparable or better performance in many RL tasks. If NFs become more widely adopted, it could lead to faster experimentation, more resource-efficient RL applications, and easier integration of probabilistic modeling into various RL pipelines.

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper presents a thorough empirical evaluation across a wide range of RL settings and tasks. This makes a strong case for the general applicability of NFs.
*   **Clarity and Simplicity:** The proposed NF architecture is relatively simple and easy to understand, which makes it more accessible to the RL community. The paper is also well-written and clearly explains the motivation, methodology, and results.
*   **Performance and Efficiency:** The experiments show that NFs can achieve competitive or superior performance to more complex models while being computationally more efficient (fewer parameters, easier training).
*   **Emphasis on Practicality:** The paper stresses the practical benefits of NFs, such as simpler algorithm design and easier integration into existing RL frameworks.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Incremental NF Architecture:**  The NF architecture itself isn't a major breakthrough. It combines existing techniques. The innovation is primarily in demonstrating its effectiveness in RL.
*   **Limited Ablation Studies:**  While the experiments are extensive, more in-depth ablation studies on the NF architecture (e.g., the effect of different coupling layers or the linear flow component) could strengthen the claims about the importance of specific architectural choices.
*   **Lack of Theoretical Analysis:** The paper lacks a theoretical analysis of the expressiveness or limitations of the proposed NF architecture in RL. This would help to better understand when NFs are likely to be successful and when they might fail.
*   **Comparison could be more detailed** A lot of the tasks are reported to have been taken from existing datasets and benchmark works. A more granular view on how the method compares against these benchmarks would strengthen the impact.

**Potential Influence:**

This paper has the potential to significantly influence the field by encouraging researchers to revisit simpler probabilistic models like NFs and to prioritize computational efficiency in RL algorithm design. It could also lead to new research on developing more specialized NF architectures for specific RL tasks. It challenges the "bigger is better" mentality, pushing for more elegant and resource-conscious solutions.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a well-supported argument for the use of NFs in RL, demonstrating their capabilities across a diverse range of settings and tasks. While the NF architecture itself is not entirely novel, the systematic evaluation and the focus on practical benefits make a valuable contribution. The paper's clarity, simplicity, and potential to simplify RL algorithm design contribute to its significance. However, the lack of in-depth ablation studies, theoretical analysis, and a more detailed comparison to existing benchmarks limit its impact.

Considering these factors, a score of 7 is warranted. This is a strong score, reflecting the paper's solid empirical results, clear presentation, and potential to influence the field. It's not a groundbreaking theoretical result (hence, not a 9 or 10), but it is a valuable contribution to the practical application and understanding of probabilistic models in RL.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Domain-Aware Tensor Network Structure Search
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23537v1)
- **Authors**: Giorgos Iacovides, Wuyang Zhou, Chao Li, Qibin Zhao, Danilo Mandic
- **Abstract**: Tensor networks (TNs) provide efficient representations of high-dimensional data, yet identification of the optimal TN structures, the so called tensor network structure search (TN-SS) problem, remains a challenge. Current state-of-the-art (SOTA) algorithms are computationally expensive as they require extensive function evaluations, which is prohibitive for real-world applications. In addition, existing methods ignore valuable domain information inherent in real-world tensor data and lack transparency in their identified TN structures. To this end, we propose a novel TN-SS framework, termed the tnLLM, which incorporates domain information about the data and harnesses the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs) to directly predict suitable TN structures. The proposed framework involves a domain-aware prompting pipeline which instructs the LLM to infer suitable TN structures based on the real-world relationships between tensor modes. In this way, our approach is capable of not only iteratively optimizing the objective function, but also generating domain-aware explanations for the identified structures. Experimental results demonstrate that tnLLM achieves comparable TN-SS objective function values with much fewer function evaluations compared to SOTA algorithms. Furthermore, we demonstrate that the LLM-enabled domain information can be used to find good initializations in the search space for sampling-based SOTA methods to accelerate their convergence while preserving theoretical performance guarantees.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Domain-Aware Tensor Network Structure Search":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the tensor network structure search (TN-SS) problem, which is the challenge of finding the optimal structure (e.g., ranks, topology) for a tensor network representation of high-dimensional data. Current state-of-the-art (SOTA) TN-SS algorithms are computationally expensive due to requiring numerous function evaluations.  The paper proposes a novel framework, tnLLM, which leverages large language models (LLMs) and domain-specific information about the tensor data to predict suitable TN structures. The framework uses a domain-aware prompting pipeline to instruct the LLM to infer TN structures based on relationships between tensor modes.  The key advantages highlighted are: (1) reduced number of function evaluations compared to SOTA algorithms, and (2) generation of domain-aware explanations for the identified TN structures. Experiments on real-world tensor datasets demonstrate that tnLLM achieves comparable performance to SOTA methods with significantly fewer evaluations and provides meaningful explanations. The paper also shows tnLLM can be used to initialize SOTA search methods leading to faster convergence.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

*   **Strengths:** The core novelty lies in the *integration* of LLMs *with* domain knowledge to tackle the TN-SS problem.  This is a departure from purely numerical or algorithmic approaches.  Existing works like tnGPS use LLMs, but to generate *algorithms*, not to directly infer or guide the structure search, *making this method significantly different*. The ability to generate explanations for the discovered structures is a key benefit missing from most TN-SS methods, enhancing transparency. The integration of LLMs to initialize search methods with theoretical guarantees is also very novel.
*   **Weaknesses:** While innovative, the approach is fundamentally *heuristic*. It relies on the LLM's ability to reason and generalize, which, while powerful, is not always reliable or predictable. The paper needs to better highlight and validate the reliability of the domain knowledge.

**Significance:**

*   **Strengths:** Reducing the computational cost of TN-SS is practically significant, making tensor network methods more accessible for real-world applications. The domain-awareness and explainability aspects address a major limitation of many existing methods, potentially broadening the adoption of tensor networks in fields where interpretability is crucial. The paper's methodology can serve as a new paradigm in searching algorithms.
*   **Weaknesses:** The dependence on LLMs also introduces a potential scalability bottleneck.  While the paper reports relatively low LLM inference time, this could become significant for extremely large-scale problems or if more complex LLMs are required. The empirical results, while promising, are limited to a few datasets. A broader and more diverse set of evaluations would strengthen the claims. A key question is also the dependence on a very capable LLM (GPT-40) versus potentially more cost effective methods. While an ablation study is presented, it might be more helpful if more explicit cost tradeoffs between accuracy, compute, and cost are better evaluated.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and practically relevant approach to tensor network structure search. The use of domain information guided LLMs to accelerate the TN-SS process and generate explanations is a clear advance over existing purely numerical and algorithmic methods. The demonstrated reduction in function evaluations and initial SOTA approach using LLMs is very significant. However, the reliance on LLMs introduces certain limitations, and a broader set of experiments would be beneficial. The work would be even more significant if theoretical guarantees can be provided, or the costs for this can be thoroughly characterized. The LLM prompting method itself and LLM method robustness study, however, are not thoroughly validated. For these reasons, a high, but non-exceptional score seems appropriate.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Probability-Consistent Preference Optimization for Enhanced LLM Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23540v1)
- **Authors**: Yunqiao Yang, Houxing Ren, Zimu Lu, Ke Wang, Weikang Shi, Aojun Zhou, Junting Pan, Mingjie Zhan, Hongsheng Li
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in preference optimization have demonstrated significant potential for improving mathematical reasoning capabilities in large language models (LLMs). While current approaches leverage high-quality pairwise preference data through outcome-based criteria like answer correctness or consistency, they fundamentally neglect the internal logical coherence of responses. To overcome this, we propose Probability-Consistent Preference Optimization (PCPO), a novel framework that establishes dual quantitative metrics for preference selection: (1) surface-level answer correctness and (2) intrinsic token-level probability consistency across responses. Extensive experiments show that our PCPO consistently outperforms existing outcome-only criterion approaches across a diverse range of LLMs and benchmarks. Our code is publicly available at https://github.com/YunqiaoYang/PCPO.
- **Summary**: The paper "Probability-Consistent Preference Optimization for Enhanced LLM Reasoning" introduces Probability-Consistent Preference Optimization (PCPO), a novel method for improving the mathematical reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs). Unlike existing methods like IRPO and ScPO that focus solely on the correctness or self-consistency of the final answer when selecting preference training pairs, PCPO leverages both the final answer and the internal logical connections within responses. It does this by calculating a weighted score based on the token-level probability consistency between preferred and dispreferred answers, ensuring a more robust and principled selection of preference pairs. The authors demonstrate through extensive experiments across various LLMs (Llama3, Mathstral, Qwen) and benchmarks (GSM8K, MATH, Olympiadbench, AMC23) that PCPO consistently outperforms outcome-only criterion approaches, leading to improved mathematical reasoning performance. The paper also includes ablation studies and case studies to further analyze the effectiveness of PCPO.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The primary novelty of the paper lies in its incorporation of token-level probability consistency as a criterion for selecting preference pairs. While existing methods focus on outcome-based metrics (answer correctness, self-consistency), PCPO attempts to capture the nuances of the reasoning process by considering the internal coherence of the generated text. This is a reasonable and potentially valuable addition to the preference optimization framework. However, the approach is not entirely groundbreaking. Token-level information and consistency have been explored in other contexts (TDPO, SparsePO). The core idea of weighting based on probabilities is not entirely new either. The authors build on existing work and combine various concepts into a new framework.

**Significance:** The significance of the paper is in demonstrating improved mathematical reasoning abilities across several LLMs and datasets. The consistent outperformance of PCPO over IRPO and SCPO suggests that considering internal logical connections can lead to better preference training data. While the absolute improvements are not always massive, the consistency across different architectures and benchmarks strengthens the argument for the effectiveness of PCPO. The analysis provides useful insights into the important factors influencing reasoning capabilities, although the computational cost is noted to be a significant barrier to widespread adoption. A noteworthy weakness is that the results seem more impressive for particular LLMs and tasks than others; for instance the improvement seems smaller in the Qwen models which already seem heavily fine-tuned.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of existing preference optimization methods in capturing the nuances of the reasoning process.
*   **Well-Defined Methodology:** PCPO is presented as a well-defined, principled framework with clear mathematical formulations.
*   **Extensive Experiments:** The experiments are comprehensive, covering various LLMs and benchmarks.
*   **Ablation Studies and Case Studies:** The additional analysis provides further insights into the effectiveness of PCPO.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Novelty:** While the combination of techniques is novel, the core concepts have been explored in other contexts. Token-level consideration in reward shaping is not new.
*   **Computational Cost:** The increased computational cost due to token probability calculations could hinder widespread adoption.
*   **Incremental Improvements:** While consistent, the absolute improvements are sometimes marginal (especially for Qwen models), and the paper could benefit from more in-depth explanations for these differences.
*   **Dependency on Supervised Data:** The method relies on the availability of ground-truth final answers for constructing preference pairs.

**Overall Impact:** The paper makes a valuable contribution by demonstrating the importance of considering internal logical connections in preference optimization. The PCPO framework offers a practical approach for improving LLM reasoning capabilities. However, the incremental nature of the improvements, the reliance on labeled data, and increased computational cost limit the overall impact.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**

I assigned a score of 7 because the paper introduces a novel method with demonstrable improvements in performance. While PCPO builds on existing ideas and has limitations, its consistent outperformance on various LLMs and benchmarks justifies this rating. The paper contributes to the understanding of preference optimization and offers a new perspective for selecting preference training pairs that considers the nuances of reasoning processes beyond final answer correctness. The authors showcase improvements that can be achieved by considering aspects of token consistency and provide data which show an incremental but important boost to LLM reasoning performance.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Position Paper: Metadata Enrichment Model: Integrating Neural Networks and Semantic Knowledge Graphs for Cultural Heritage Applications
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23543v1)
- **Authors**: Jan Ignatowicz, Krzysztof Kutt, Grzegorz J. Nalepa
- **Abstract**: The digitization of cultural heritage collections has opened new directions for research, yet the lack of enriched metadata poses a substantial challenge to accessibility, interoperability, and cross-institutional collaboration. In several past years neural networks models such as YOLOv11 and Detectron2 have revolutionized visual data analysis, but their application to domain-specific cultural artifacts - such as manuscripts and incunabula - remains limited by the absence of methodologies that address structural feature extraction and semantic interoperability. In this position paper, we argue, that the integration of neural networks with semantic technologies represents a paradigm shift in cultural heritage digitization processes. We present the Metadata Enrichment Model (MEM), a conceptual framework designed to enrich metadata for digitized collections by combining fine-tuned computer vision models, large language models (LLMs) and structured knowledge graphs. The Multilayer Vision Mechanism (MVM) appears as the key innovation of MEM. This iterative process improves visual analysis by dynamically detecting nested features, such as text within seals or images within stamps. To expose MEM's potential, we apply it to a dataset of digitized incunabula from the Jagiellonian Digital Library and release a manually annotated dataset of 105 manuscript pages. We examine the practical challenges of MEM's usage in real-world GLAM institutions, including the need for domain-specific fine-tuning, the adjustment of enriched metadata with Linked Data standards and computational costs. We present MEM as a flexible and extensible methodology. This paper contributes to the discussion on how artificial intelligence and semantic web technologies can advance cultural heritage research, and also use these technologies in practice.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary of the paper and a critical evaluation of its novelty and significance, including a score and justification.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces the Metadata Enrichment Model (MEM), a framework for enhancing metadata in digitized cultural heritage collections. MEM integrates computer vision models (specifically, retrained neural networks), large language models (LLMs), and semantic knowledge graphs.  A key innovation is the Multilayer Vision Mechanism (MVM), an iterative process designed to dynamically detect nested features within images, such as text within seals or images within stamps.  The paper presents a proof-of-concept study using a dataset of digitized incunabula from the Jagiellonian Digital Library, including the release of a manually annotated dataset.  The authors discuss challenges related to domain-specific fine-tuning, adapting enriched metadata to Linked Data standards, and computational costs. The paper argues for MEM as a flexible and extensible methodology that can advance cultural heritage research by combining AI and semantic web technologies.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper tackles a significant problem: the lack of enriched, semantically structured metadata in digitized cultural heritage collections. While the digitization of these collections has increased accessibility, the limited metadata hinders true interoperability, cross-institutional collaboration, and advanced analysis.  The idea of combining computer vision, LLMs, and knowledge graphs is not entirely new, but the specific architecture of MEM, particularly the Multilayer Vision Mechanism (MVM), represents a valuable contribution.

**Strengths:**

*   **Novelty of MVM:** The MVM is a strong point. The iterative approach to detecting nested features is crucial for handling the complex structure of many historical artifacts. It explicitly addresses a gap in existing AI-driven metadata enrichment approaches, which often focus on isolated tasks or struggle with structured data.
*   **Holistic Approach:**  The paper integrates different AI components with semantic web technologies, moving beyond isolated task-specific applications. The framework not only extracts information but also structures it according to Linked Data principles, facilitating interoperability.
*   **Practical Validation:**  The proof-of-concept study, including the release of a manually annotated dataset, adds to the paper's value. It provides concrete evidence of the framework's potential and identifies practical challenges that need to be addressed.
*   **Discussion of Ethical Considerations:** The authors acknowledge the ethical implications of automated metadata enrichment, particularly the risk of algorithmic bias and the need for human-in-the-loop validation.
*   **Clear Problem Definition and Approach:** The paper clearly articulates the problem being addressed and presents a well-structured framework as a solution.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Depth of Empirical Evaluation:** While the proof-of-concept is valuable, the paper lacks a more rigorous quantitative evaluation.  For instance, how much does MEM improve the quality of metadata compared to baseline methods? What is the precision and recall of the object detection and feature extraction modules? More detailed performance metrics would significantly strengthen the paper.
*   **Lack of Specificity in Model Selection and Fine-Tuning:** The paper mentions the use of YOLOv11 and Detectron2, but it does not provide specific details about the fine-tuning process. What data augmentation techniques were used? What hyperparameters were optimized? Providing more details would allow other researchers to replicate the results.
*   **Ontology Development Details:** The paper refers to the creation of a draft RDF ontology but does not fully detail the methodology, extent, or challenges faced during this process. A more concrete discussion of the ontology design and its alignment with existing standards (e.g., CIDOC CRM) would improve the paper's credibility.
*   **Scalability Concerns not fully addressed:** While the authors claim MEM is scalable, the paper doesn't present concrete evidence to support this. The computational demands and processing time per image might become prohibitive for large-scale deployments.
*   **Limited Comparison to Existing Frameworks:** While the related work section covers some relevant research, the paper could benefit from a more detailed comparison to other existing metadata enrichment frameworks, highlighting the unique advantages of MEM.

**Significance:**

The significance of the paper lies in its potential to transform how cultural heritage institutions manage and enrich their digital collections. The MEM framework addresses critical limitations in existing approaches by integrating AI-driven analysis with semantic web technologies. The MVM approach, in particular, offers a promising solution for handling the complex structure of many historical artifacts. If fully realized, MEM could significantly improve the accessibility, interoperability, and analytical potential of digitized cultural heritage resources.

**Overall:**

The paper presents a promising framework with a clear potential to address an important challenge in the field of digital humanities. The MVM is a novel approach to metadata enrichment with practical applications. However, the paper lacks a more robust empirical evaluation, detailed specifications, and a more in-depth comparison to other frameworks, all of which would strengthen its contribution.

Score: 7

**Justification:**

I assigned a score of 7 because the paper demonstrates a valuable conceptual framework and presents a novel component (MVM) with potential benefits. However, the lack of empirical validation, detailed implementation specifications, and a more rigorous comparative analysis hold back the paper from reaching a higher score. The proof-of-concept is a good starting point, but more extensive and detailed experiments are needed to fully demonstrate the framework's effectiveness and scalability. The paper has the potential to influence future research in AI-driven metadata enrichment, but it requires further development and evaluation to achieve its full potential.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Translation in the Wild
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23548v1)
- **Authors**: Yuri Balashov
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) excel in translation among other things, demonstrating competitive performance for many language pairs in zero- and few-shot settings. But unlike dedicated neural machine translation models, LLMs are not trained on any translation-related objective. What explains their remarkable translation abilities? Are these abilities grounded in "incidental bilingualism" (Briakou et al. 2023) in training data? Does instruction tuning contribute to it? Are LLMs capable of aligning and leveraging semantically identical or similar monolingual contents from different corners of the internet that are unlikely to fit in a single context window? I offer some reflections on this topic, informed by recent studies and growing user experience. My working hypothesis is that LLMs' translation abilities originate in two different types of pre-training data that may be internalized by the models in different ways. I discuss the prospects for testing the "duality" hypothesis empirically and its implications for reconceptualizing translation, human and machine, in the age of deep learning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the provided paper abstract and introduction:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the remarkable translation abilities of Large Language Models (LLMs), questioning how they achieve competitive performance in zero- and few-shot translation settings despite not being explicitly trained on translation objectives. It contrasts LLMs with dedicated Neural Machine Translation (NMT) models and proposes a "duality" hypothesis: LLMs' translation skills stem from two distinct learning sources within their pre-training data: "Local learning" (bilingual signals within a context window) and "Global learning" (leveraging semantically related monolingual content spread across the vast pre-training dataset). The paper discusses potential empirical tests of this hypothesis and its broader implications for reconceptualizing translation, both human and machine, in the age of deep learning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper's novelty lies in its proposed "duality" hypothesis, suggesting two distinct origins for LLM translation abilities.  While the existence of "incidental bilingualism" in training data has been previously acknowledged, the specific breakdown into local and global learning mechanisms, and the emphasis on leveraging monolingual content, represents a fresh perspective. The paper also pushes back a bit on the "emergence" narrative, suggesting the skills were latent and not purely emergent, which is a valuable nuance.
*   **Significance:** If validated, the duality hypothesis could significantly impact our understanding of LLMs and translation.  It provides a framework for designing more effective training strategies (emphasizing both local and global learning aspects) and for improving prompt engineering techniques. Furthermore, it prompts us to rethink the very nature of translation in an era where machines can achieve high levels of performance through mechanisms different from traditional, linguistically-grounded approaches. The paper also raises important questions about the universality and limitations of LLMs' translation capabilities, particularly regarding low-resource languages and specialized domains. The focus on mechanistic interpretability is also a valuable avenue. The paper engages with both practical (training strategies) and theoretical (reconceptualizing translation) aspects, increasing its potential impact.
*   **Weaknesses:** The primary weakness is that the current submission is a conceptual paper, primarily offering a hypothesis. The empirical testing outlined is only prospective and needs to be implemented to assess the validity of the claims. The concepts of "Local" and "Global" learning, while intuitively appealing, could benefit from a more rigorous formalization.  The introduction could also better acknowledge the existing literature on cross-lingual representation learning in LLMs, clarifying the specific ways the proposed framework builds upon or diverges from prior work. Also, the historical argument for the "neglected" role of translation as driving force behind AI advancements can be debated.
*   **Impact:**  The paper's impact is largely dependent on the success of future empirical validation.  If the duality hypothesis holds, it will influence research in LLM training, prompt engineering, and mechanistic interpretability. Even if the hypothesis is not fully supported, the paper's conceptual framework will stimulate discussion and further investigation into the origins and limitations of LLM translation abilities. It highlights the importance of data transparency and careful scrutiny of training corpora. Furthermore, its engagement with Translation Studies opens up potential cross-disciplinary dialogue and new research directions.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the novelty of the duality hypothesis and the potential for significant impact in guiding future research, but considering the current lack of empirical validation and the somewhat limited engagement with existing literature, a score of 7 is warranted.

**Score: 7**
- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### LLM-based Property-based Test Generation for Guardrailing Cyber-Physical Systems
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23549v1)
- **Authors**: Khashayar Etemadi, Marjan Sirjani, Mahshid Helali Moghadam, Per Strandberg, Paul Pettersson
- **Abstract**: Cyber-physical systems (CPSs) are complex systems that integrate physical, computational, and communication subsystems. The heterogeneous nature of these systems makes their safety assurance challenging. In this paper, we propose a novel automated approach for guardrailing cyber-physical systems using property-based tests (PBTs) generated by Large Language Models (LLMs). Our approach employs an LLM to extract properties from the code and documentation of CPSs. Next, we use the LLM to generate PBTs that verify the extracted properties on the CPS. The generated PBTs have two uses. First, they are used to test the CPS before it is deployed, i.e., at design time. Secondly, these PBTs can be used after deployment, i.e., at run time, to monitor the behavior of the system and guardrail it against unsafe states. We implement our approach in ChekProp and conduct preliminary experiments to evaluate the generated PBTs in terms of their relevance (how well they match manually crafted properties), executability (how many run with minimal manual modification), and effectiveness (coverage of the input space partitions). The results of our experiments and evaluation demonstrate a promising path forward for creating guardrails for CPSs using LLM-generated property-based tests.
- **Summary**: The paper proposes CHEKPROP, a novel approach that leverages Large Language Models (LLMs) to generate property-based tests (PBTs) for guardrailing cyber-physical systems (CPSs). The approach uses the source code, documentation, and unit tests of a CPS as input to an LLM, which extracts properties and generates PBTs to verify those properties. These PBTs can be used both at design time for testing and at runtime for monitoring. The paper presents a prototype implementation of CHEKPROP and evaluates its performance on a dataset of nine Python CPS programs, assessing the relevance of extracted properties and the quality (executability and effectiveness) of generated PBTs. The results indicate promising potential for LLMs in automating PBT generation for CPSs.

**Critical Evaluation of Novelty and Significance:**

While the paper demonstrates a practical application of LLMs in the domain of CPS testing, the overall novelty is moderate. The core idea of using LLMs for code analysis and test generation isn't entirely new, particularly in the broader software engineering context.  The adaptation to the specific challenges of CPS, incorporating environmental context and physical element interactions, is a notable aspect of novelty, but it is also somewhat limited.  While prior related work focuses on scenario generation (e.g., for autonomous driving) or traditional API test generation, this paper specifically addresses property-based test generation, and more importantly, tackles the complexities inherent in Cyber-Physical Systems.  The introduction of a two-phased approach including both PBT generation and property-based monitoring is also somewhat novel. The paper acknowledges this body of work, which further weakens its claims to complete originality.

The significance of the work lies primarily in its practical demonstration. The experimental results, though preliminary, showcase that LLMs can effectively extract relevant properties and generate reasonably useful PBTs for real-world CPS programs. The paper identifies specific challenges related to mocking CPS components and incorporating relevant documentation, which provides valuable insights for future research in this area. The implementation of CHEKPROP and its public availability are also beneficial contributions, facilitating further exploration and refinement of the approach.

However, several weaknesses limit the significance. The experimental evaluation is based on a relatively small dataset of Python CPS programs, which may not be representative of all CPS domains. The lack of a thorough comparison with existing CPS testing techniques makes it difficult to assess the true advantages of the proposed approach. The analysis of PBT quality relies heavily on manual inspection, which is subjective and may not be scalable to larger datasets. Also, the study acknowledges that its prototype doesn't include an improvement loop. Without that improvement loop, one cannot assess the usefulness of prompts and failure messages for iteratively refining the generated PBTs, which significantly limits its usefulness as a testing approach. Also, for some scenarios, the method requires modifications to the target CPS's code which raises some red flags.

Ultimately, the paper offers a valuable initial investigation into a promising research direction, but further work is needed to address the identified limitations and demonstrate the broader applicability and impact of LLM-based PBT generation for CPSs. The insights provided into the prompting design (e.g. how mocking should be handled) are relevant, but their value would be significantly higher if they were incorporated into a refined, automated PBT generation loop.

Score: 6

**Justification:**

A score of 6 reflects the paper's moderate novelty and significance.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Practical application of LLMs to a challenging domain (CPS testing).
    *   Identification of key challenges and insights for future research.
    *   Open-source implementation for further exploration.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Moderate technical novelty.
    *   Limited experimental evaluation in dataset and manual inspection.
    *   Lack of comparison with existing techniques.
    *   Limited usefulness without inclusion of the improvement loop, and some method requires modification of target CPS's code.

The paper takes a step forward in an important direction, showcasing the potential of LLMs for CPS testing. The evaluation provides actionable insights into practical challenges. It doesn't demonstrate dramatic advances or a transformative paradigm shift, but rather represents a solid, iterative improvement within the existing landscape of LLM-based software engineering techniques, adapted for the specific intricacies of CPS. Hence the assigned score.

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 6/10

### Sustainable Carbon-Aware and Water-Efficient LLM Scheduling in Geo-Distributed Cloud Datacenters
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23554v1)
- **Authors**: Hayden Moore, Sirui Qi, Ninad Hogade, Dejan Milojicic, Cullen Bash, Sudeep Pasricha
- **Abstract**: In recent years, Large Language Models (LLM) such as ChatGPT, CoPilot, and Gemini have been widely adopted in different areas. As the use of LLMs continues to grow, many efforts have focused on reducing the massive training overheads of these models. But it is the environmental impact of handling user requests to LLMs that is increasingly becoming a concern. Recent studies estimate that the costs of operating LLMs in their inference phase can exceed training costs by 25x per year. As LLMs are queried incessantly, the cumulative carbon footprint for the operational phase has been shown to far exceed the footprint during the training phase. Further, estimates indicate that 500 ml of fresh water is expended for every 20-50 requests to LLMs during inference. To address these important sustainability issues with LLMs, we propose a novel framework called SLIT to co-optimize LLM quality of service (time-to-first token), carbon emissions, water usage, and energy costs. The framework utilizes a machine learning (ML) based metaheuristic to enhance the sustainability of LLM hosting across geo-distributed cloud datacenters. Such a framework will become increasingly vital as LLMs proliferate.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper "Sustainable Carbon-Aware and Water-Efficient LLM Scheduling in Geo-Distributed Cloud Datacenters":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the growing environmental impact of large language model (LLM) inference, specifically focusing on carbon emissions, water usage, and energy costs in geo-distributed cloud datacenters. The authors propose SLIT, a novel framework that co-optimizes these factors alongside the time-to-first-token (TTFT) performance metric. SLIT employs a metaheuristic approach combining machine learning (ML) and evolutionary algorithms (EA) to generate a Pareto front of scheduling solutions.  The paper models the different components of LLM inference (workload, datacenter resources, energy usage, water use, carbon emissions) and compares SLIT against existing scheduling approaches (Helix and Splitwise), demonstrating improvements in sustainability metrics while maintaining competitive performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the integrated approach to co-optimize multiple sustainability metrics (carbon, water, energy cost) *together with* performance (TTFT) specifically for LLM inference.  Existing works often focus on a subset of these metrics or consider other types of cloud workloads.  The use of a hybrid ML/EA metaheuristic is not entirely novel in resource scheduling, but its application to LLM inference with a strong focus on sustainability and the specific problem formulation is a valuable contribution. The modeling of energy, carbon, and water at the datacenter level, especially considering the water intensity from diverse energy sources, adds to the significance.

*   **Significance:**  The increasing adoption of LLMs makes their environmental impact a crucial concern. The paper provides concrete estimations for water consumption during LLM inference, an often-overlooked aspect.  By demonstrating that it is possible to simultaneously reduce carbon emissions, water usage, and energy costs without significantly sacrificing performance, the paper motivates the adoption of more sustainable LLM deployment strategies. The Pareto front approach offers datacenter operators flexibility in choosing solutions that best align with their specific priorities.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Modeling:** The models for LLM workload, datacenters, energy, carbon, and water are well-defined and grounded in existing literature.  The models account for several factors influencing energy/water usage and carbon emissions at various levels (node, datacenter, grid).
    *   **Practical Applicability:** The framework is designed for real-time decision-making with a 15-minute time budget, making it applicable to practical LLM deployment scenarios.
    *   **Performance Improvement:** SLIT improves TTFT, carbon emissions, energy cost, and water usage relative to state-of-the-art approaches.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Workload Generation:** The creation of synthetic workload from the analysis of real traces helps to scale experimental scope. However, there is no discussion or analysis surrounding the implications of synthetic data generation. How the synthetic data matches reality impacts the real-world performance of LLM.
    *   **Limited Scope:** Although the scope is limited in this paper, additional factors that can be introduced in the future include location of users, and LLM model size.
    *   **Scalability/Complexity:** The computational complexity of the ML/EA metaheuristic as the number of datacenters, LLM models, or workload increases is not fully addressed. This could be a bottleneck in very large-scale deployments. A discussion on how the method would scale to many more datacenters and users could provide additional insight.

*   **Overall:** The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of sustainable cloud computing, specifically in the context of LLM inference. The integrated approach, comprehensive modeling, and experimental results demonstrate the potential for significant reductions in environmental impact without compromising performance. While some aspects, such as the computational complexity and the limitations of workload characterization, could be explored further, the paper makes a strong case for adopting sustainability-aware scheduling frameworks for LLMs.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The score reflects the paper's solid contributions to an important and growing area of research. The novelty lies in the integration of sustainability metrics and the application of a metaheuristic to LLM inference scheduling, and the significance stems from the increasing environmental concerns associated with LLMs. The weaknesses, while relevant, do not detract significantly from the overall value of the paper. It provides a strong foundation for future research and offers practical insights for datacenter operators.

- **Classification**: cs.DC
- **Score**: 7/10

### Adaptive Federated LoRA in Heterogeneous Wireless Networks with Independent Sampling
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23555v1)
- **Authors**: Yanzhao Hou, Jiaxiang Geng, Boyu Li, Xiaofeng Tao, Juncheng Wang, Xiaodong Xu, Bing Luo
- **Abstract**: Federated LoRA has emerged as a promising technique for efficiently fine-tuning large language models (LLMs) on distributed devices by reducing the number of trainable parameters. However, existing approaches often inadequately overlook the theoretical and practical implications of system and data heterogeneity, thereby failing to optimize the overall training efficiency, particularly in terms of wall-clock time. In this paper, we propose an adaptive federated LoRA strategy with independent client sampling to minimize the convergence wall-clock time of federated fine-tuning under both computation and communication heterogeneity. We first derive a new convergence bound for federated LoRA with arbitrary and independent client sampling, notably without requiring the stringent bounded gradient assumption. Then, we introduce an adaptive bandwidth allocation scheme that accounts for heterogeneous client resources and system bandwidth constraints. Based on the derived theory, we formulate and solve a non-convex optimization problem to jointly determine the LoRA sketching ratios and sampling probabilities, aiming to minimize wall-clock convergence time. An efficient and low-complexity algorithm is developed to approximate the solution. Finally, extensive experiments demonstrate that our approach significantly reduces wall-clock training time compared to state-of-the-art methods across various models and datasets.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of efficiently fine-tuning Large Language Models (LLMs) in a federated learning (FL) setting, specifically focusing on Federated LoRA (Low-Rank Adaptation). It acknowledges that existing Federated LoRA approaches often overlook the complexities of system and data heterogeneity, impacting training efficiency. The core contribution is an adaptive Federated LoRA strategy with independent client sampling designed to minimize the wall-clock convergence time under both computational and communication heterogeneity. The paper presents a new convergence bound for Federated LoRA with independent client sampling, an adaptive bandwidth allocation scheme, and a non-convex optimization problem to jointly determine LoRA sketching ratios and sampling probabilities. It also offers an efficient algorithm for approximating the solution and demonstrates the approach's benefits through experiments.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper presents several novel aspects:
    *   **Convergence Bound:** Deriving a new convergence bound for Federated LoRA under independent client sampling, particularly without the stringent bounded gradient assumption, is a significant theoretical contribution. This distinguishes it from prior art, and offers a more general understanding of Federated Lora convergence behavior.
    *   **Adaptive Bandwidth Allocation:** The proposed adaptive bandwidth allocation is designed specifically for Federated LoRA, jointly considering communication, computation heterogeneity, and the system bandwidth. While others have considered such heterogeneities, the joint allocation strategy is novel.
    *   **Joint Optimization:** The optimization problem to jointly determine LoRA sketching ratios and sampling probabilities is also a novel contribution, allowing the algorithm to optimize wall-clock time.
    *   **Independent Client Sampling:** Relaxing the assumption that a fixed number of clients will take part in a federated learning task and instead formulating the optimization with independent client sampling is a significant and novel approach.

* **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in its ability to address the practical challenges of deploying Federated LoRA in realistic wireless edge networks where system and data heterogeneity are prevalent. By minimizing wall-clock time, the approach can accelerate the deployment of LLMs on resource-constrained edge devices, which opens up new possibilities in applications like personalized AI, edge-based natural language processing, and federated knowledge discovery. The empirical results, showcasing substantial reductions in training time compared to state-of-the-art methods, further solidify its practical relevance.

* **Strengths:**
    *   **Strong Theoretical Foundation:** The paper provides a solid theoretical analysis with a new convergence bound and a well-formulated optimization problem.
    *   **Practical Applicability:** The proposed solution is designed to address real-world challenges in heterogeneous wireless networks.
    *   **Efficient Algorithm:** The development of an efficient and low-complexity algorithm to approximate the solution of the non-convex optimization problem is a key strength, enhancing the practicality of the method.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The extensive experiments across various models and datasets provide strong evidence of the effectiveness of the proposed approach.
    *   **Unbiased Aggregation with Independent Sampling:** The result that the aggregation method with independent sampling is unbiased, and the analysis of the parameters and equations used.

* **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Complexity:** The algorithm is complex and will likely be hard to re-implement. The estimation phase will likely have significant overhead if chosen poorly, and will impact the performance of the algorithm.
    *   **Algorithm Explanation:** The paper only runs Algorithm 1 (for baseline tests), while providing Algorithm 2 as the end result of the effort in solving P1. It would be useful to see Algorithm 2's runtime, memory impact, convergence, etc., as the efficiency claim can only be claimed if Algorithm 2 is also performant. The empirical results in the paper did not describe the full picture of this tradeoff.
    *   **Constant Bandwidth:** The algorithm assumes the bandwidth is constant between clients, which may not be true due to environmental conditions and real-world signal characteristics.
    *   **Implementation Details:** While the paper includes extensive experiments, the paper would benefit from more detail on how the algorithm was implemented.

* **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of Federated Learning, particularly in the context of LLMs and edge computing. The theoretical insights and practical solutions presented can serve as a foundation for future research in resource-aware and heterogeneity-aware Federated LoRA. The work can also inspire new designs for Federated Learning algorithms that are tailored to the specific characteristics of different application domains and hardware platforms.

**Rigorous Rationale for Score:**

While the paper offers significant advancements in the domain of Federated LoRA, it also exhibits certain weaknesses that temper its overall impact. The complexity of the algorithm and the limited evaluation of Algorithm 2, a constant bandwidth assumption, and implementation details prevent it from achieving a higher score. The paper addresses the major challenge of LLM deployment in federated learning and demonstrates a promising approach with a solid theoretical foundation and practical validation, but the concerns above, as well as the highly theoretical nature of the convergence calculation, limit its immediate application. This leads to a lower score.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Merge Hijacking: Backdoor Attacks to Model Merging of Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23561v1)
- **Authors**: Zenghui Yuan, Yangming Xu, Jiawen Shi, Pan Zhou, Lichao Sun
- **Abstract**: Model merging for Large Language Models (LLMs) directly fuses the parameters of different models finetuned on various tasks, creating a unified model for multi-domain tasks. However, due to potential vulnerabilities in models available on open-source platforms, model merging is susceptible to backdoor attacks. In this paper, we propose Merge Hijacking, the first backdoor attack targeting model merging in LLMs. The attacker constructs a malicious upload model and releases it. Once a victim user merges it with any other models, the resulting merged model inherits the backdoor while maintaining utility across tasks. Merge Hijacking defines two main objectives-effectiveness and utility-and achieves them through four steps. Extensive experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of our attack across different models, merging algorithms, and tasks. Additionally, we show that the attack remains effective even when merging real-world models. Moreover, our attack demonstrates robustness against two inference-time defenses (Paraphrasing and CLEANGEN) and one training-time defense (Fine-pruning).
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Merge Hijacking," a novel backdoor attack specifically designed to target model merging in large language models (LLMs).  The core idea is that attackers can create a malicious upload model that, when merged with other models, introduces a backdoor into the resulting merged model. The attack is designed to maintain both effectiveness (the merged model performs the backdoor task) and utility (the merged model still performs well on its intended tasks, and the malicious model itself has good utility).  The paper outlines a four-step process for creating the malicious model, including deriving a backdoor vector, magnitude-based sparsification, rescaling, and fine-tuning. The attack is evaluated across several LLMs, merging algorithms, and tasks, and is shown to be robust against some basic defenses.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a genuinely novel problem and corresponding attack.  While backdoor attacks are well-studied in machine learning, targeting model merging in LLMs is a relatively unexplored area. Previous backdoor attack studies in model merging processes primarily focus on encoder architectures in computer vision. The idea of hijacking the model merging process itself is a creative and significant contribution.

*   **Significance:** The threat posed by Merge Hijacking is potentially significant. Model merging is becoming more common as a cost-effective way to create multi-task LLMs.  Highlighting the potential for injecting backdoors through this process raises a critical security concern. The work points to the need for developing more robust security measures in the model merging workflow.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-defined problem:**  The problem is clearly articulated with well-defined goals (effectiveness and utility).
    *   **Comprehensive methodology:** The four-step attack process is well-explained and technically sound.
    *   **Extensive evaluation:**  The paper includes a wide range of experiments covering different LLMs, merging algorithms, tasks, and even explores real-world models. The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the attack's components.
    *   **Defense analysis:** Testing the attack against common defense mechanisms (Paraphrasing, CLEANGEN, Fine-pruning) is an important aspect of the evaluation, although the lack of success of these defenses highlights the need for more sophisticated protection methods.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Defense Evaluation:** While the paper explores three defense mechanisms, they are all relatively basic. A more thorough evaluation against a wider range of defenses would significantly strengthen the work. More importantly, the lack of successful defenses points to the need for more sophisticated protection mechanisms tailored to model merging scenarios.
    *   **Trigger Selection:** While the paper performs ablation studies using different trigger types, the trigger design seems relatively simple. More sophisticated and stealthy triggers could potentially increase the attack's effectiveness and evade detection.
    *   **Limited Real-World Validation:** Even though testing on real-world models is great, more complete integration of real-world scenarios would add a lot of strength. For example, attempting to find a real model and actually merge the malicious model with it.
    *   **Limited Discussion on Ethical Implications:** The paper could benefit from a deeper discussion of the ethical implications of this type of attack and the responsibility of researchers in disclosing such vulnerabilities.

*   **Potential Impact:** This work has the potential to stimulate further research in the security of model merging. It also raises awareness among practitioners about the potential risks associated with using models from untrusted sources.

**Justification for the Score:**

The paper presents a novel attack with significant implications for the security of LLMs and model merging.  The methodology is well-developed, and the evaluation is comprehensive. While the defense analysis is somewhat limited, the work successfully demonstrates the vulnerability and raises important questions for future research.
Score: 7.

**Rationale:** The paper would be a much stronger 8 or 9 if more sophisticated defense mechanisms could be tested and overcome or even more complete analysis on why defenses struggle in this attack.

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Segment Policy Optimization: Effective Segment-Level Credit Assignment in RL for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23564v1)
- **Authors**: Yiran Guo, Lijie Xu, Jie Liu, Dan Ye, Shuang Qiu
- **Abstract**: Enhancing the reasoning capabilities of large language models effectively using reinforcement learning (RL) remains a crucial challenge. Existing approaches primarily adopt two contrasting advantage estimation granularities: Token-level methods (e.g., PPO) aim to provide the fine-grained advantage signals but suffer from inaccurate estimation due to difficulties in training an accurate critic model. On the other extreme, trajectory-level methods (e.g., GRPO) solely rely on a coarse-grained advantage signal from the final reward, leading to imprecise credit assignment. To address these limitations, we propose Segment Policy Optimization (SPO), a novel RL framework that leverages segment-level advantage estimation at an intermediate granularity, achieving a better balance by offering more precise credit assignment than trajectory-level methods and requiring fewer estimation points than token-level methods, enabling accurate advantage estimation based on Monte Carlo (MC) without a critic model. SPO features three components with novel strategies: (1) flexible segment partition; (2) accurate segment advantage estimation; and (3) policy optimization using segment advantages, including a novel probability-mask strategy. We further instantiate SPO for two specific scenarios: (1) SPO-chain for short chain-of-thought (CoT), featuring novel cutpoint-based partition and chain-based advantage estimation, achieving $6$-$12$ percentage point improvements in accuracy over PPO and GRPO on GSM8K. (2) SPO-tree for long CoT, featuring novel tree-based advantage estimation, which significantly reduces the cost of MC estimation, achieving $7$-$11$ percentage point improvements over GRPO on MATH500 under 2K and 4K context evaluation. We make our code publicly available at https://github.com/AIFrameResearch/SPO.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Segment Policy Optimization (SPO), a novel reinforcement learning (RL) framework for training large language models (LLMs) to improve their reasoning capabilities. SPO addresses limitations in existing RL approaches by estimating advantages at a segment level, an intermediate granularity between token-level and trajectory-level methods. This approach aims to balance more precise credit assignment than trajectory-level methods with fewer estimation points than token-level methods, thus enabling Monte Carlo (MC) estimation without relying on a critic model. The framework consists of flexible segment partition, MC-based advantage estimation, and policy optimization using segment advantages. The authors instantiate SPO into two specific scenarios: SPO-chain for short chain-of-thought (CoT) and SPO-tree for long CoT, each with unique strategies for segment partitioning and advantage estimation. Experiments on GSM8K and MATH500 demonstrate SPO's effectiveness, showing significant accuracy improvements over PPO and GRPO.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the segment-level advantage estimation approach. This provides a middle ground between token-level methods like PPO (which suffer from critic inaccuracies) and trajectory-level methods like GRPO (which are too coarse-grained). The specific instantiation into SPO-chain and SPO-tree, with their respective cutpoint-based and tree-based advantage estimation methods, further contributes to the novelty. The probability-mask optimization strategy, although seemingly minor, appears to provide performance benefits. While step-level methods exist, this is a more generalized partitioning scheme.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial challenge in RL for LLMs: effective credit assignment. By demonstrating improvements in mathematical reasoning tasks like GSM8K and MATH500, the paper suggests that SPO could be a valuable tool for enhancing LLM reasoning. The modular design of SPO allows for adaptable implementations to different tasks and scenarios, increasing its potential impact. The tree-based estimation is notable, because it tries to tackle a major inefficiency of Monte Carlo estimation: the cost. The code release is also an important factor in accelerating adoption in the community.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-defined Problem:** The paper clearly identifies the credit assignment problem in RL for LLMs and provides a targeted solution.
    *   **Comprehensive Framework:** SPO offers a modular and flexible framework adaptable to different reasoning scenarios.
    *   **Strong Experimental Results:** The experimental results on standard benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of SPO and its instantiations.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** The ablation studies provide insights into the importance of different components of SPO.
    *   **Well written**: The exposition is clear and logical.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope of Tasks:** The experiments primarily focus on mathematical reasoning tasks. Testing on broader sets of reasoning tasks would strengthen the claim of general applicability.
    *   **Reliance on MC Estimation:** While avoiding a critic is a strength, relying solely on MC estimation can still be computationally expensive, especially for extremely long sequences. While the SPO-tree strategy mitigates this somewhat, further optimizations might be necessary for very resource-constrained scenarios.
    *   **Baselines:** While the baselines are reasonable, the landscape of RL for LLMs is evolving rapidly. Comparison with more recent approaches would further contextualize SPO's performance.

*   **Potential Influence:** SPO has the potential to influence the field by providing a more effective and efficient way to train LLMs for reasoning tasks. The segment-level advantage estimation approach could inspire further research into alternative granularities for credit assignment. The tree-based strategy may inspire adoption in other MC-based algorithms.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:** The paper presents a novel and well-executed approach (SPO) to address the credit assignment problem in RL for LLMs. The performance improvements shown in the experiments, along with the modular framework design, make it a potentially significant contribution. However, the limited scope of tasks and the computational cost of MC estimation prevent a higher score. While the strengths are clear and the method is implemented well, future work must be performed to further investigate the usefulness of this approach in a broader variety of settings. The approach strikes a good balance between incremental improvement and conceptual innovation. The SPO-tree is a particularly innovative strategy for addressing MC sample efficiency.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Uni-MuMER: Unified Multi-Task Fine-Tuning of Vision-Language Model for Handwritten Mathematical Expression Recognition
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23566v1)
- **Authors**: Yu Li, Jin Jiang, Jianhua Zhu, Shuai Peng, Baole Wei, Yuxuan Zhou, Liangcai Gao
- **Abstract**: Handwritten Mathematical Expression Recognition (HMER) remains a persistent challenge in Optical Character Recognition (OCR) due to the inherent freedom of symbol layout and variability in handwriting styles. Prior methods have faced performance bottlenecks, proposing isolated architectural modifications that are difficult to integrate coherently into a unified framework. Meanwhile, recent advances in pretrained vision-language models (VLMs) have demonstrated strong cross-task generalization, offering a promising foundation for developing unified solutions. In this paper, we introduce Uni-MuMER, which fully fine-tunes a VLM for the HMER task without modifying its architecture, effectively injecting domain-specific knowledge into a generalist framework. Our method integrates three data-driven tasks: Tree-Aware Chain-of-Thought (Tree-CoT) for structured spatial reasoning, Error-Driven Learning (EDL) for reducing confusion among visually similar characters, and Symbol Counting (SC) for improving recognition consistency in long expressions. Experiments on the CROHME and HME100K datasets show that Uni-MuMER achieves new state-of-the-art performance, surpassing the best lightweight specialized model SSAN by 16.31% and the top-performing VLM Gemini2.5-flash by 24.42% in the zero-shot setting. Our datasets, models, and code are open-sourced at: https://github.com/BFlameSwift/Uni-MuMER
- **Summary**: Here is a concise summary and a critical evaluation of the paper "Uni-MuMER: Unified Multi-Task Fine-Tuning of Vision-Language Model for Handwritten Mathematical Expression Recognition":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Uni-MuMER, a unified multi-task fine-tuning framework designed to improve handwritten mathematical expression recognition (HMER) using vision-language models (VLMs).  Uni-MuMER enhances an open-source VLM (Qwen2.5-VL) through three data-driven tasks: Tree-Aware Chain-of-Thought (Tree-CoT) for structured spatial reasoning, Error-Driven Learning (EDL) to reduce confusion among visually similar characters, and Symbol Counting (SC) to improve recognition consistency in long expressions. The authors demonstrate state-of-the-art performance on CROHME and HME100K datasets, surpassing previous methods and zero-shot VLMs significantly.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The key novelty of this paper lies in its unified, data-driven approach to HMER using a VLM. While other papers have integrated auxiliary tasks or attempted to refine model architectures for HMER, Uni-MuMER distinguishes itself by focusing on fine-tuning a general-purpose VLM with specifically designed data-driven tasks, without modifying the original architecture. The individual components (Tree-CoT, EDL, and SC) are conceptually simple, but their combination within a unified training framework on a VLM represents a valuable contribution.
