*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its substantial performance gains over previous HMER methods and even state-of-the-art zero-shot VLMs. The 16.31% improvement over SSAN and the 24.42% improvement over Gemini2.5-flash (zero-shot) on the CROHME dataset is compelling evidence of the effectiveness of the Uni-MuMER framework. This success demonstrates the potential of adapting powerful pre-trained VLMs to the challenging HMER task by injecting domain-specific knowledge through careful data-driven task design. The faster inference speed compared to traditional methods further enhances its practical value.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The experimental results are convincing, demonstrating significant improvements across multiple datasets (CROHME and HME100K) and against strong baselines. The ablation studies clearly demonstrate the importance of each component in Uni-MuMER.
    *   **Unified Framework:** The data-driven approach, integrating different aspects of HMER into a unified framework is a major strength.
    *   **Clarity and Reproducibility:** The paper is well-written and includes sufficient detail to enable reproducibility. The open-sourced code, datasets, and models further contribute to its value.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope of Analysis:** While the ablation study highlights the importance of each component, further analysis of the interaction between these tasks could provide valuable insights. It is not clear whether the contributions of the three data-driven tasks are truly orthogonal or if there's significant synergistic effects.
    *   **Dependence on Qwen2.5-VL:** While leveraging Qwen2.5-VL is practical, the dependency on a single VLM limits the generality of the approach. It would be useful to investigate whether the framework can be easily adapted to other VLM architectures.
    *   **Lack of evaluation on certain VLM benchmarks:** The paper mentions a lack of performance integration with general VLM fine-tuning datasets and a assessment of the performance on a broader set of benchmarks, such as OCR, which are not discussed.

*   **Potential Influence:** The Uni-MuMER framework has the potential to influence future research in HMER by shifting the focus from complex architectural modifications to more effective data-driven fine-tuning strategies.  Its success in adapting a general-purpose VLM could inspire similar approaches in other specialized OCR tasks. The open-source release of Uni-MuMER is likely to facilitate further research and development in this area.

**Justification:** The paper presents a significant advancement in the field of HMER by demonstrating the effectiveness of a unified, data-driven fine-tuning approach for VLMs. The compelling experimental results, clarity of presentation, and open-source release justify a high score.  However, the limited scope of analysis and dependence on a specific VLM, warrants not assigning a score in the highest range.
Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Evaluating AI capabilities in detecting conspiracy theories on YouTube
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23570v1)
- **Authors**: Leonardo La Rocca, Francesco Corso, Francesco Pierri
- **Abstract**: As a leading online platform with a vast global audience, YouTube's extensive reach also makes it susceptible to hosting harmful content, including disinformation and conspiracy theories. This study explores the use of open-weight Large Language Models (LLMs), both text-only and multimodal, for identifying conspiracy theory videos shared on YouTube. Leveraging a labeled dataset of thousands of videos, we evaluate a variety of LLMs in a zero-shot setting and compare their performance to a fine-tuned RoBERTa baseline. Results show that text-based LLMs achieve high recall but lower precision, leading to increased false positives. Multimodal models lag behind their text-only counterparts, indicating limited benefits from visual data integration. To assess real-world applicability, we evaluate the most accurate models on an unlabeled dataset, finding that RoBERTa achieves performance close to LLMs with a larger number of parameters. Our work highlights the strengths and limitations of current LLM-based approaches for online harmful content detection, emphasizing the need for more precise and robust systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the use of open-weight Large Language Models (LLMs), both text-only and multimodal, for detecting conspiracy theory videos on YouTube. The authors leverage the YouNiCon dataset and enrich it with additional metadata (transcripts, images, etc.).  They evaluate a range of LLMs in a zero-shot setting, compare their performance against a fine-tuned RoBERTa baseline, and experiment with different prompting strategies and ensemble methods.  The study finds that text-based LLMs achieve high recall but lower precision, leading to increased false positives, and multimodal models do not significantly outperform their text-only counterparts.  The best-performing models are then applied to an unlabeled dataset of YouTube videos, and a subset of the model-generated labels is manually annotated to assess real-world performance.  The study concludes by highlighting the strengths and limitations of LLM-based approaches for online harmful content detection.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**

    * **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper provides a thorough empirical evaluation of various open-weight LLMs for conspiracy detection on YouTube.  The comparison with a fine-tuned RoBERTa baseline offers a solid point of reference.
    * **Detailed Methodology:** The paper clearly outlines the data collection process, the models used, prompting strategies, and evaluation metrics.  This transparency is crucial for replicability.
    * **Real-World Assessment:** The attempt to evaluate the models on an unlabeled dataset of YouTube videos provides valuable insights into the practical applicability of these approaches.
    * **Cost-Sensitive Evaluation:** Using metrics like Normalized Expected Cost (NEC) demonstrates a more realistic perspective, recognizing the different costs associated with false positives and false negatives.
    * **Addresses important problem:** Conspiracy theories have a tangible negative impact on society. Automatic detection of such contents is a first step toward automated content moderation.

* **Weaknesses:**

    * **Limited Multimodal Analysis:** The finding that multimodal models don't offer a significant improvement is interesting, but the analysis is somewhat limited. The use of only thumbnail images might be insufficient to capture relevant visual cues present in the videos.
    * **Zero-Shot Limitations:** While the use of zero-shot learning showcases the general capabilities of LLMs, it's not necessarily the most effective approach for this specific task.  Fine-tuning the LLMs on the YouNiCon dataset could potentially lead to significantly better results.
    * **Dataset Bias:** The YouNiCon dataset, as acknowledged by the authors, may not fully represent the broader distribution of content on YouTube. The use of a sample from YouTube Commons further skews this distribution, potentially limiting the generalizability of the findings. The manual labeling process and agreement between human annotators is limited.
    * **Incremental Novelty:**  The overall approach of using LLMs for conspiracy detection is not entirely new, as highlighted in the related work.  The paper builds upon previous studies, but it lacks a truly disruptive or groundbreaking contribution.
    * **Lack of in-depth error analysis and investigation:** The paper explores the effect of balancing dataset but the analysis is limited.

* **Novelty and Significance:**

The paper's novelty lies primarily in its comprehensive comparison of various recent open-weight LLMs in a zero-shot setting for YouTube conspiracy detection. While the core problem and the use of LLMs are not entirely new, the paper provides a valuable benchmark for the performance of these models and highlights their strengths and weaknesses in a specific and practically relevant application. The investigation of multimodal models and the use of cost-sensitive evaluation metrics further contribute to the existing body of knowledge. The real-world analysis is particularly helpful.

**Justification for the Score:**

The paper provides a useful, but not groundbreaking, contribution to the field of misinformation detection. It offers a thorough empirical evaluation of open-weight LLMs for a specific and relevant task. However, the limitations related to the dataset bias, limited multimodal analysis, and incremental novelty prevent it from being a truly transformative work.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### CoT Red-Handed: Stress Testing Chain-of-Thought Monitoring
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23575v1)
- **Authors**: Benjamin Arnav, Pablo Bernabeu-Pérez, Nathan Helm-Burger, Tim Kostolansky, Hannes Whittingham, Mary Phuong
- **Abstract**: As AI models are deployed with increasing autonomy, it is important to ensure they do not take harmful actions unnoticed. As a potential mitigation, we investigate Chain-of-Thought (CoT) monitoring, wherein a weaker trusted monitor model continuously oversees the intermediate reasoning steps of a more powerful but untrusted model. We compare CoT monitoring to action-only monitoring, where only final outputs are reviewed, in a red-teaming setup where the untrusted model is instructed to pursue harmful side tasks while completing a coding problem. We find that CoT monitoring improves detection by up to 27 percentage points in scenarios where action-only monitoring fails to reliably identify sabotage. However, CoT traces can also contain misleading rationalizations that deceive the monitor, reducing performance in more obvious sabotage cases. To address this, we introduce a hybrid protocol that independently scores both reasoning and final outputs and combines them using a weighted average. This hybrid monitor consistently outperforms both CoT and action-only monitors across all tested models and tasks, with detection rates over four times higher than action-only monitoring for subtle deception scenarios.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the value of Chain-of-Thought (CoT) monitoring for detecting harmful actions in AI models, comparing it against action-only monitoring.  The setup involves red-teaming a powerful "untrusted" model tasked with completing coding problems while subtly pursuing harmful side tasks. The weaker "trusted" monitor assesses the untrusted model's actions and/or reasoning to detect sabotage. The authors find that CoT monitoring improves detection rates in subtle sabotage scenarios where action-only monitoring struggles.  However, CoT can be misleading in obvious cases due to deceptive rationalizations. To address this, they propose a hybrid monitoring approach combining action-only and CoT scores, which consistently outperforms both individual methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper presents moderate novelty. While CoT monitoring and AI control are established areas, the specific focus on *adversarial* CoT monitoring, with tailored attack policies designed to evade detection, is a valuable contribution. Prior work primarily used CoT for detecting unintended errors or specific, pre-defined failure modes.  The exploration of both benefits and drawbacks, particularly the deceptive potential of CoT, adds a layer of nuance. The hybrid monitoring approach, while conceptually straightforward (a weighted average), demonstrates a practical way to mitigate the identified shortcomings.
*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in highlighting the limitations of relying solely on CoT for safety assurance. The demonstration that reasoned explanations can *decrease* detection rates in certain contexts is crucial. The hybrid approach offers a practical pathway to improve robustness. This work contributes to a more nuanced understanding of how to leverage reasoning traces for safety in potentially misaligned AI systems. Its red-teaming approach is relevant as AI deployments become more autonomous.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-defined experimental setup:** The red-teaming approach, with specified attack policies, provides a concrete framework for evaluating monitoring strategies.
    *   **Clear identification of trade-offs:** The paper effectively demonstrates the complementary strengths and weaknesses of action-only and CoT monitoring.
    *   **Practical solution:** The hybrid monitoring approach offers a tangible improvement and is relatively easy to implement.
    *   **Comprehensive analysis:** The paper considers different model sizes, attack policies, and subtle vs. non-subtle sabotage scenarios.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited task domain:** Focusing solely on coding tasks, while providing a controlled environment, might not generalize to all AI applications.
    *   **Relatively simple hybrid approach:** The hybrid method is a weighted average. More sophisticated approaches (e.g., a model trained to identify deceptive reasoning or learn how to weigh action/CoT information) could potentially achieve higher performance.
    *   **Reliance on heuristics for subtle/non-subtle classification:** Classifying side tasks as 'subtle' or 'non-subtle' based on detection rates in action-only monitoring is somewhat ad hoc. A more principled approach to quantifying task difficulty for monitoring might be beneficial.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Scalability/Cost:**  The paper does not fully address the computational overhead of CoT and its effect on latency and costs for production deployment.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence the design of AI safety protocols, encouraging the use of hybrid monitoring approaches and a more critical assessment of the information provided by reasoning traces.  It also motivates further research into developing more robust and deception-aware monitoring systems.

**Rigorous Rationale for Assigned Score:**

The paper contributes to the growing body of research on AI safety and control. The explicit demonstration of scenarios where CoT monitoring can be *detrimental* is a valuable counterpoint to the often optimistic view of reasoning traces. While the technical innovations are limited, the practical insights and the well-executed red-teaming evaluation make this a worthwhile contribution. The limitations, such as the narrow task domain and the simplistic hybrid approach, temper the overall impact. The paper could inspire more advanced approaches to combining action-based and reasoning-based monitoring, and therefore has influence on the field of AI safety.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Cognitive Guardrails for Open-World Decision Making in Autonomous Drone Swarms
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23576v1)
- **Authors**: Jane Cleland-Huang, Pedro Antonio Alarcon Granadeno, Arturo Miguel Russell Bernal, Demetrius Hernandez, Michael Murphy, Maureen Petterson, Walter Scheirer
- **Abstract**: Small Uncrewed Aerial Systems (sUAS) are increasingly deployed as autonomous swarms in search-and-rescue and other disaster-response scenarios. In these settings, they use computer vision (CV) to detect objects of interest and autonomously adapt their missions. However, traditional CV systems often struggle to recognize unfamiliar objects in open-world environments or to infer their relevance for mission planning. To address this, we incorporate large language models (LLMs) to reason about detected objects and their implications. While LLMs can offer valuable insights, they are also prone to hallucinations and may produce incorrect, misleading, or unsafe recommendations. To ensure safe and sensible decision-making under uncertainty, high-level decisions must be governed by cognitive guardrails. This article presents the design, simulation, and real-world integration of these guardrails for sUAS swarms in search-and-rescue missions.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper based on the OCR'd text.

**Summary:**

The paper "Cognitive Guardrails for Open-World Decision Making in Autonomous Drone Swarms" addresses the challenge of using autonomous drone swarms for tasks like search and rescue in dynamic and uncertain open-world environments. The authors propose the CAIRN framework, which integrates a Bayesian model for reasoning about search strategies, a large language model (LLM) pipeline for interpreting detected clues, and cognitive guardrails to ensure safe, justified, and mission-aligned decision-making. The LLM reasoning helps in understanding unfamiliar objects, but the guardrails prevent hallucinations and unsafe behavior from the LLM. These guardrails include belief entropy, cost-benefit analysis, ethical and regulatory oversight (using "advocate personas"), and a runtime safety envelope. They implemented a simulation environment and began integrating the framework into their existing DroneResponse platform for physical-world deployment.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

*   **Strengths:** The novelty lies in the integration of several components (Bayesian reasoning, LLMs, and guardrails) to create a comprehensive framework for autonomous decision-making in drone swarms, specifically addressing open-world uncertainty. The use of cognitive guardrails to constrain LLM outputs within safety and ethical boundaries is a significant contribution. Using "advocate personas" to oversee the system is an interesting angle.
*   **Weaknesses:** Each component (Bayesian models, LLMs in robotics) has been explored individually. The paper lacks a direct comparison to other state-of-the-art methods. While integrating them is novel, the paper lacks substantial evidence that this leads to significantly better performance than any alternative approach.

**Significance:**

*   **Strengths:** The potential impact is high if the framework proves effective. Autonomous drone swarms have numerous applications (search and rescue, infrastructure inspection, etc.), and improving their robustness and trustworthiness is crucial. The focus on safety and ethical considerations makes it relevant to real-world deployment. The system enables the sUAS to interpret the implications of discovered open-world objects, plan actions, and adapt autonomously. The inclusion of ethical concerns in the system is important.
*   **Weaknesses:** The results presented are primarily based on a custom simulation environment. The paper does not show significant quantitative results. There's no rigorous experimental evaluation against baselines or human performance. The evaluation section describes a single example scenario. A more thorough evaluation with multiple scenarios and performance metrics would strengthen the paper. Many parameters are based on the team's judgment, which calls for more rigorous experiments to tune these. Without more evidence, its claims of improved decision-making remain speculative.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents an interesting framework for an important problem. The integration of Bayesian methods, LLMs, and cognitive guardrails is novel, and it addresses a critical challenge in autonomous robotics. However, the evaluation is limited to a simulation, and there is a lack of quantitative results and comparison to baseline methods. The potential impact is high, but the current evidence is insufficient to fully support the claims.

**Score: 6**

**Rationale:**

A score of 6 reflects the moderate novelty and the potential for significant impact. The integration of different technologies is promising, but the lack of a strong experimental evaluation holds it back. The paper could have been a much stronger contribution with more data from the simulation and especially with initial real-world deployments (or even just more extensive simulated trials) and a clear quantitative assessment of its capabilities.

- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 6/10

### On-Policy RL with Optimal Reward Baseline
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23585v1)
- **Authors**: Yaru Hao, Li Dong, Xun Wu, Shaohan Huang, Zewen Chi, Furu Wei
- **Abstract**: Reinforcement learning algorithms are fundamental to align large language models with human preferences and to enhance their reasoning capabilities. However, current reinforcement learning algorithms often suffer from training instability due to loose on-policy constraints and computational inefficiency due to auxiliary models. In this work, we propose On-Policy RL with Optimal reward baseline (OPO), a novel and simplified reinforcement learning algorithm designed to address these challenges. OPO emphasizes the importance of exact on-policy training, which empirically stabilizes the training process and enhances exploration. Moreover, OPO introduces the optimal reward baseline that theoretically minimizes gradient variance. We evaluate OPO on mathematical reasoning benchmarks. The results demonstrate its superior performance and training stability without additional models or regularization terms. Furthermore, OPO achieves lower policy shifts and higher output entropy, encouraging more diverse and less repetitive responses. These results highlight OPO as a promising direction for stable and effective reinforcement learning in large language model alignment and reasoning tasks. The implementation is provided at https://github.com/microsoft/LMOps/tree/main/opo.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "On-Policy RL with Optimal Reward Baseline":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces On-Policy RL with Optimal reward baseline (OPO), a new reinforcement learning algorithm aimed at improving the stability and efficiency of aligning large language models (LLMs).  OPO emphasizes exact on-policy training to enhance exploration and stability, and it derives an optimal reward baseline that minimizes gradient variance. The algorithm is evaluated on mathematical reasoning benchmarks, demonstrating superior performance and training stability compared to existing methods like PPO and GRPO, without requiring additional models or regularization terms.  Furthermore, OPO exhibits lower policy shifts and higher output entropy, leading to more diverse and less repetitive responses.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the combination of exact on-policy training with a theoretically-derived optimal reward baseline that minimizes gradient variance. While on-policy methods and reward baselines are not entirely novel individually, the specific *derivation* of the optimal baseline and its *integration* with strict on-policy training, and then *application* of the algorithm to the LLM alignment problem is novel. The derivation is mathematically sound, and the claim of optimal variance reduction, *if true*, is a significant theoretical contribution.

* **Significance:** The significance stems from addressing key challenges in LLM alignment – training instability, computational inefficiency, and alignment tax (leading to homogenization of responses).  Stable training enables more robust learning, and lower policy shift leads to better preservation of the LLM's initial capabilities.  Reduced computational overhead (eliminating the need for a value function) is also a practical advantage, even though it may be partly offset by the need for K samples with GRPO like methods.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Theoretical grounding:**  The derivation of the optimal reward baseline provides a strong theoretical foundation for the method.
    * **Empirical validation:** The paper provides experimental results on mathematical reasoning benchmarks that support the claims of improved performance and stability. The comparison against relevant baselines (PPO, GRPO) is fair. The paper uses several public models (Deepseek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B) making it useful.
    * **Simplicity:** By removing the need for auxiliary components like value models and KL regularization, the algorithm simplifies the RLHF pipeline.
    * **Addressing important problems:** The paper targets critical problems in LLM alignment (stability, efficiency, alignment tax) and demonstrates a reduction of these issues.
    * **Reproducibility:** The authors provide an implementation.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Narrow evaluation scope:** The evaluation is primarily limited to mathematical reasoning. While this is a relevant domain, further validation is needed to demonstrate general applicability across diverse LLM alignment tasks, including those relating to safety, factuality, and helpfulness. The paper may be overselling the ability for OPO to encourage more diverse and less repetitive responses when evaluated only on MATH-500 data.
    * **Limited ablation studies:**  A more thorough ablation study is needed to isolate the contributions of the on-policy training and the optimal reward baseline individually.  For example, a comparison with standard on-policy PPO *without* the optimal reward baseline is valuable to further prove the validity of the latter's effectiveness.
    * **Baseline selection:**  While PPO and GRPO are reasonable baselines, including a comparison to more recent RLHF methods that specifically address alignment tax or exploration issues would strengthen the evaluation.
    * **Rule-based reward:**  Experiments should be conducted using human reward signals instead of the rule-based rewards since that is the ultimate purpose of RLHF for LLMs.
    * **Scalability:** While eliminating the value function decreases computational needs, the paper needs to address the increased sampling of K trajectories that OPO does require. Is this really more computationally efficient?

* **Potential Impact:**
    * If the claims hold true across other LLM alignment tasks, the algorithm could significantly improve the efficiency and stability of LLM alignment pipelines.
    * The theoretical framework for deriving the optimal reward baseline could inspire further research in variance reduction techniques for policy gradient methods.
    * The focus on exact on-policy training could lead to a re-evaluation of off-policy approximations in other RLHF settings.

* **Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a theoretically-grounded and empirically-validated contribution to the field of RLHF for LLMs. The combination of exact on-policy training and the derived optimal reward baseline is novel and addresses significant challenges in alignment stability and efficiency. However, the limited evaluation scope and the need for more extensive ablation studies temper the excitement surrounding the work. The potential impact is high, but it is crucial to validate the findings across a broader range of LLM alignment tasks to reach its full potential.

### Jigsaw-R1: A Study of Rule-based Visual Reinforcement Learning with Jigsaw Puzzles
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23590v1)
- **Authors**: Zifu Wang, Junyi Zhu, Bo Tang, Zhiyu Li, Feiyu Xiong, Jiaqian Yu, Matthew B. Blaschko
- **Abstract**: The application of rule-based reinforcement learning (RL) to multimodal large language models (MLLMs) introduces unique challenges and potential deviations from findings in text-only domains, particularly for perception-heavy tasks. This paper provides a comprehensive study of rule-based visual RL using jigsaw puzzles as a structured experimental framework, revealing several key findings. \textit{Firstly,} we find that MLLMs, initially performing near to random guessing on simple puzzles, achieve near-perfect accuracy and generalize to complex, unseen configurations through fine-tuning. \textit{Secondly,} training on jigsaw puzzles can induce generalization to other visual tasks, with effectiveness tied to specific task configurations. \textit{Thirdly,} MLLMs can learn and generalize with or without explicit reasoning, though open-source models often favor direct answering. Consequently, even when trained for step-by-step reasoning, they can ignore the thinking process in deriving the final answer. \textit{Fourthly,} we observe that complex reasoning patterns appear to be pre-existing rather than emergent, with their frequency increasing alongside training and task difficulty. \textit{Finally,} our results demonstrate that RL exhibits more effective generalization than Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT), and an initial SFT cold start phase can hinder subsequent RL optimization. Although these observations are based on jigsaw puzzles and may vary across other visual tasks, this research contributes a valuable piece of jigsaw to the larger puzzle of collective understanding rule-based visual RL and its potential in multimodal learning. The code is available at: \href{https://github.com/zifuwanggg/Jigsaw-R1}{https://github.com/zifuwanggg/Jigsaw-R1}.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Jigsaw-R1: A Study of Rule-based Visual Reinforcement Learning with Jigsaw Puzzles" investigates the application of rule-based reinforcement learning (RL) to multimodal large language models (MLLMs) using jigsaw puzzles as a structured experimental framework. The study reveals several key findings: MLLMs can achieve near-perfect accuracy on jigsaw puzzles through fine-tuning and generalize to unseen configurations. Training on jigsaw puzzles can induce generalization to other visual tasks, with effectiveness depending on specific task configurations. MLLMs can learn and generalize with or without explicit reasoning. Complex reasoning patterns appear to be pre-existing rather than emergent. Finally, RL exhibits more effective generalization than Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT), and an initial SFT cold start phase can hinder subsequent RL optimization. The study suggests potential deviations from text-only RL findings when dealing with perception-heavy tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its systematic exploration of rule-based RL for MLLMs within the visual domain, specifically using jigsaw puzzles. While jigsaw puzzles are a well-established pretext task in computer vision, applying them as a controlled environment for studying RL in MLLMs, especially in relation to reasoning and generalization, is a reasonably novel approach.  The observations regarding the pre-existing nature of complex reasoning and the differential impact of SFT and RL in this multimodal setting provide insights that weren't previously well-documented.

*   **Significance:** The findings contribute to a deeper understanding of how MLLMs learn and generalize in visual RL environments.  The observation about pre-existing reasoning patterns challenges the notion of *emergent* behavior in RL. The comparison between SFT and RL is practically relevant, given the common practice of starting with SFT. The results provide guidance on designing effective visual RL training strategies for MLLMs. The modularity and controllability provided by the Jigsaw puzzle environment enable controlled investigation.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Structured Experimental Design:** The use of jigsaw puzzles provides a well-controlled and easily adjustable experimental framework. Varying puzzle sizes and question types allows for systematic investigation of different learning aspects.
    *   **Comprehensive Analysis:** The paper addresses several important research questions related to reasoning, generalization, and the comparison of RL and SFT.
    *   **Clear Findings:** The findings are presented clearly and are supported by experimental results.
    *   **Relevance:**  The work directly addresses the emerging area of rule-based visual RL and provides valuable insights for researchers working on MLLMs.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Task Specificity:** The findings are based solely on jigsaw puzzles, which may limit their generalizability to other visual tasks. The complexity and nature of information needed to solve a Jigsaw puzzle might not be representative of other, more general, visual learning tasks.
    *   **Model Focus:** The paper focuses primarily on Qwen2 models. While the models have been well-characterized, there are several key architectures missing (specifically the inclusion of the original, or even a later BLOOM model)
    *   **Limited Discussion of Limitations:** While acknowledged, the limitations of the task specificity are not thoroughly discussed in terms of how they might affect the applicability of the conclusions. The models and datasets chosen have inherent biases themselves, and further discussion regarding the implications of this could have been made.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to influence the design and training strategies for visual RL in MLLMs. Its results are particularly relevant for researchers working on improving the reasoning and generalization capabilities of these models. The code release further enhances its impact.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a solid contribution to the field of multimodal reinforcement learning. Its novel use of the Jigsaw puzzle provides for a controlled experiment. However, the task-specific nature of the study and limited generalization makes the score have a reduced value.
