### MAPLE: A Mobile Assistant with Persistent Finite State Machines for Recovery Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23596v1)
- **Authors**: Linqiang Guo, Wei Liu, Yi Wen Heng, Tse-Hsun, Chen, Yang Wang
- **Abstract**: Mobile GUI agents aim to autonomously complete user-instructed tasks across mobile apps. Recent advances in Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) enable these agents to interpret UI screens, identify actionable elements, and perform interactions such as tapping or typing. However, existing agents remain reactive: they reason only over the current screen and lack a structured model of app navigation flow, limiting their ability to understand context, detect unexpected outcomes, and recover from errors. We present MAPLE, a state-aware multi-agent framework that abstracts app interactions as a Finite State Machine (FSM). We computationally model each UI screen as a discrete state and user actions as transitions, allowing the FSM to provide a structured representation of the app execution. MAPLE consists of specialized agents responsible for four phases of task execution: planning, execution, verification, error recovery, and knowledge retention. These agents collaborate to dynamically construct FSMs in real time based on perception data extracted from the UI screen, allowing the GUI agents to track navigation progress and flow, validate action outcomes through pre- and post-conditions of the states, and recover from errors by rolling back to previously stable states. Our evaluation results on two challenging cross-app benchmarks, Mobile-Eval-E and SPA-Bench, show that MAPLE outperforms the state-of-the-art baseline, improving task success rate by up to 12%, recovery success by 13.8%, and action accuracy by 6.5%. Our results highlight the importance of structured state modeling in guiding mobile GUI agents during task execution. Moreover, our FSM representation can be integrated into future GUI agent architectures as a lightweight, model-agnostic memory layer to support structured planning, execution verification, and error recovery.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "MAPLE: A Mobile Assistant with Persistent Finite State Machines for Recovery Reasoning":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MAPLE, a novel mobile GUI agent framework designed to improve task completion, error recovery, and action accuracy in cross-app scenarios. Unlike existing reactive GUI agents that primarily reason based on the current UI screen, MAPLE leverages finite state machines (FSMs) to create a structured, state-aware representation of app interactions. It models each UI screen as a discrete state, user actions as transitions, and incorporates specialized agents for planning, execution, verification, error recovery, and knowledge retention. The system dynamically constructs FSMs in real-time, validating action outcomes and enabling recovery by rolling back to previously stable states when errors occur.  Experiments on Mobile-Eval-E and SPA-Bench demonstrate MAPLE's superior performance compared to a state-of-the-art baseline, Mobile-Agent-E + Evo, showing improvements in task success rate, recovery success, and action accuracy. The paper claims the FSM representation can be integrated into other GUI agent architectures as a lightweight memory layer.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Novel Approach:** The core novelty lies in the integration of FSMs into mobile GUI agent architecture. This approach provides a structured, state-aware reasoning framework that addresses the limitations of reactive agents, particularly in handling errors and maintaining context across multiple app interactions. Using FSMs for representing UI flows is not entirely new, but MAPLE's online construction and integration with planning, execution, and recovery processes is a significant contribution.
*   **Clear Problem Definition:**  The paper clearly articulates the problem with current reactive GUI agents and justifies the need for a more structured approach. The motivating example of a user interacting with the Walmart app effectively illustrates the limitations of existing methods.
*   **Well-Defined Architecture:** The architecture of MAPLE is well-defined, with clearly delineated responsibilities for each specialized agent. This modularity enhances the system's understandability and maintainability.
*   **Empirical Validation:**  The experimental results on two established benchmarks (Mobile-Eval-E and SPA-Bench) provide solid evidence for the effectiveness of MAPLE. The improvements in success rate, recovery success, and action accuracy are statistically significant and demonstrate the practical benefits of the approach.
*   **Potential for Generalization:** The paper highlights the potential of integrating MAPLE's FSM representation into other GUI agent architectures. This modularity and model-agnostic design are valuable and increases practical utility.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Incremental Novelty:** While the integration of FSMs is novel, each individual component (planning, execution, verification, knowledge retention) builds on existing techniques. The novelty lies primarily in the specific way these components are combined and how they interact with the FSM representation.
*   **Dependency on MLLMs:** The system heavily relies on the capabilities of MLLMs for perception, planning, and reasoning. The performance of MAPLE is therefore directly tied to the accuracy and reliability of the underlying MLLMs. While the paper mentions using various MLLMs, it doesn't deeply investigate the impact of MLLM choice on the overall system performance.
*   **Limited Ablation Study:** The ablation study shows the contribution of each component. However, a more granular analysis, particularly on the pre- and post-conditions, could have strengthened the claims. Additionally, the ablation could benefit from an analysis of the performance gain (if any) by using multiple candidate plans as opposed to single plans selected *without* an LLM-based judge.
*   **Scalability Concerns:**  While the paper discusses the architecture, there is no in-depth analysis of the computational complexity or scalability of the FSM construction and maintenance process, especially for more complex apps with a larger number of UI screens. Also, the practicality of the Knowledge Retention portion should be discussed with scalability considerations. How many FSMs can be stored? How does the system perform when it needs to search a large number of FSMs to improve planning, execution, etc.?
*   **Lack of Analysis on Failure Cases:** The paper focuses on demonstrating MAPLE's success, but a more detailed analysis of the failure cases would provide valuable insights into the limitations of the approach and areas for future improvement.
*   **Reproducibility Challenges:**  Due to the involvement of complex MLLMs, the exact reproducibility of the results might be challenging. Details on specific prompt engineering strategies used in generating plans could improve replicability.

**Significance:**

The paper addresses a critical limitation of current mobile GUI agents and proposes a viable solution. The integration of FSMs provides a structured framework for reasoning, error recovery, and context maintenance, which are essential for building more robust and reliable mobile assistants. The experimental results demonstrate the practical benefits of the approach and its potential for improving the user experience. It is a significant improvement to previous approaches such as Mobile-Agent-E.

**Justification of Score:**

I assign a score of **7** to this paper.

**Rationale:** While the integration of FSMs is a key strength that offers significant advantages over reactive systems, the architecture relies on existing technologies, primarily MLLMs, which somewhat reduces its novelty. The empirical validation provides strong evidence for the effectiveness of the approach, but some weaknesses in the experimental design and a limited discussion on failure cases and scalability prevent a higher score.

The contribution is significant as it addresses a core problem in the field of mobile GUI agents and provides a workable solution and could lead to more robust and reliable mobile assistants. The FSM framework is valuable. The practical value, however, is tied strongly to the state-of-the-art of the MLLMs used, and the overall approach still faces scalability challenges. The provided evaluation metrics may have benefited from experiments over more complex cross-app scenarios (e.g., beyond Venice Itinerary to *planning* and *booking* the flight in conjunction with Airbnb).

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 0/10

### LLM Performance for Code Generation on Noisy Tasks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23598v1)
- **Authors**: Radzim Sendyka, Christian Cabrera, Andrei Paleyes, Diana Robinson, Neil Lawrence
- **Abstract**: This paper investigates the ability of large language models (LLMs) to recognise and solve tasks which have been obfuscated beyond recognition. Focusing on competitive programming and benchmark tasks (LeetCode and MATH), we compare performance across multiple models and obfuscation methods, such as noise and redaction. We demonstrate that all evaluated LLMs can solve tasks obfuscated to a level where the text would be unintelligible to human readers, and does not contain key pieces of instruction or context. We introduce the concept of eager pattern matching to describe this behaviour, which is not observed in tasks published after the models' knowledge cutoff date, indicating strong memorisation or overfitting to training data, rather than legitimate reasoning about the presented problem. We report empirical evidence of distinct performance decay patterns between contaminated and unseen datasets. We discuss the implications for benchmarking and evaluations of model behaviour, arguing for caution when designing experiments using standard datasets. We also propose measuring the decay of performance under obfuscation as a possible strategy for detecting dataset contamination and highlighting potential safety risks and interpretability issues for automated software systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) to solve code generation tasks that have been obfuscated to the point of being unreadable by humans. The authors use LeetCode and MATH datasets and apply various obfuscation techniques like noise injection, redaction, and truncation. The results show that LLMs often solve heavily obfuscated tasks, even when key instructions are missing. The authors introduce the concept of "eager pattern matching" to explain this behavior, suggesting that the models rely on memorization and overfitting of training data rather than genuine reasoning.  They also demonstrate that performance decay under obfuscation varies significantly between contaminated (older) and unseen (newer) datasets. The paper concludes that this over-reliance on pattern matching can lead to unreliable performance, potential safety risks, and challenges in evaluating the true capabilities of LLMs.  The authors propose using performance decay under obfuscation as a method for detecting dataset contamination.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper's novelty lies in its systematic investigation of LLM performance under extreme obfuscation, going beyond previous studies that focused on resilience to minor noise. It's also novel to highlight the performance decay under obfuscation as a proxy for dataset contamination and overfitting.

**Strengths:**

*   **Well-defined experiments:** The paper has a clear methodology, using established datasets and well-defined obfuscation techniques. The experiments are reproducible.
*   **Empirical evidence:** The results convincingly demonstrate the ability of LLMs to solve tasks that are unintelligible to humans, supporting the "eager pattern matching" hypothesis.
*   **Concept of eager pattern matching**: Offers a useful descriptor for the LLM behavior.
*   **Strong analysis:** The paper provides a good analysis of the implications of the findings, discussing potential risks and challenges for LLM evaluation and deployment.
*   **Adversarial example**: Well chosen and effectively illustrates the vulnerability of the systems to incorrect eager pattern matching.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Model Selection:** While a diverse range of LLMs were chosen, some of them are not top of the line when it comes to the ability to solve the tasks in the original datasets. The paper would be more convincing if state-of-the-art models had been tested.
*   **Limited scope of datasets**: While LeetCode and MATH are commonly used, they may not be representative of all code generation or problem-solving tasks.
*   **Human Baseline metric.** The paper correctly states that the human baseline metric cannot be compared directly to the LLM metric and does not attempt to do so. Nevertheless, its inclusion is unnecessary.

**Significance Score:**

The paper makes a significant contribution by highlighting the limitations of current LLM evaluation practices and the potential risks associated with over-reliance on memorization. The proposed method of using performance decay under obfuscation as a contamination detection strategy is valuable, though requires further research and refinement. The work raises important questions about the trustworthiness of LLMs in code generation and calls for more rigorous evaluation methods.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### A Comprehensive Evaluation of Multi-Modal Large Language Models for Endoscopy Analysis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23601v1)
- **Authors**: Shengyuan Liu, Boyun Zheng, Wenting Chen, Zhihao Peng, Zhenfei Yin, Jing Shao, Jiancong Hu, Yixuan Yuan
- **Abstract**: Endoscopic procedures are essential for diagnosing and treating internal diseases, and multi-modal large language models (MLLMs) are increasingly applied to assist in endoscopy analysis. However, current benchmarks are limited, as they typically cover specific endoscopic scenarios and a small set of clinical tasks, failing to capture the real-world diversity of endoscopic scenarios and the full range of skills needed in clinical workflows. To address these issues, we introduce EndoBench, the first comprehensive benchmark specifically designed to assess MLLMs across the full spectrum of endoscopic practice with multi-dimensional capacities. EndoBench encompasses 4 distinct endoscopic scenarios, 12 specialized clinical tasks with 12 secondary subtasks, and 5 levels of visual prompting granularities, resulting in 6,832 rigorously validated VQA pairs from 21 diverse datasets. Our multi-dimensional evaluation framework mirrors the clinical workflow--spanning anatomical recognition, lesion analysis, spatial localization, and surgical operations--to holistically gauge the perceptual and diagnostic abilities of MLLMs in realistic scenarios. We benchmark 23 state-of-the-art models, including general-purpose, medical-specialized, and proprietary MLLMs, and establish human clinician performance as a reference standard. Our extensive experiments reveal: (1) proprietary MLLMs outperform open-source and medical-specialized models overall, but still trail human experts; (2) medical-domain supervised fine-tuning substantially boosts task-specific accuracy; and (3) model performance remains sensitive to prompt format and clinical task complexity. EndoBench establishes a new standard for evaluating and advancing MLLMs in endoscopy, highlighting both progress and persistent gaps between current models and expert clinical reasoning. We publicly release our benchmark and code.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "EndoBench: A Comprehensive Evaluation of Multi-Modal Large Language Models for Endoscopy Analysis":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces EndoBench, a new comprehensive benchmark for evaluating Multi-Modal Large Language Models (MLLMs) in the context of endoscopy analysis. EndoBench aims to address the limitations of existing benchmarks by providing a more realistic and diverse evaluation environment. It encompasses four endoscopic scenarios (Gastroscopy, Colonoscopy, Capsule endoscopy, Surgical endoscopy), twelve specialized clinical tasks with twelve secondary subtasks, and five levels of visual prompting granularities. The authors benchmark 23 state-of-the-art models (general-purpose, medical-specialized, and proprietary) and establish human clinical performance as a reference standard. The experiments reveal that proprietary models outperform open-source and medical-specialized models but still trail human experts, medical-domain fine-tuning improves task-specific accuracy, and model performance remains sensitive to prompt format and task complexity.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Scope:** The paper's main strength is the comprehensive nature of the EndoBench benchmark.  It moves beyond limited, task-specific endoscopic evaluations to encompass the full spectrum of endoscopic practice and needed analytical abilities.
    *   **Clinically Relevant Tasks:** The tasks included in EndoBench mirror the actual workflow of clinical endoscopy, making it a more ecologically valid evaluation tool. The breakdown into categories and secondary tasks is well-structured and clinically sound.
    *   **Rigorous Methodology:** The paper describes a detailed and rigorous methodology for dataset construction, QA standardization, and filtering. This increases the reliability and validity of the benchmark. They use the established models for image representation, DINOv2, and a model for question rephrasing, GPT-4o-mini, improving the framework.
    *   **Extensive Experiments:** The paper presents a thorough evaluation of a wide range of MLLMs, including open-source, medical-specific, and proprietary models. The comparison to human expert performance provides a meaningful baseline.
    *   **Clear Results and Insights:** The results are clearly presented and lead to insightful conclusions about the current capabilities and limitations of MLLMs in endoscopy analysis. The case studies provide illustrative examples of the types of errors MLLMs make.
    *   **Publicly Available Resource:** The paper mentions the intention to publicly release the benchmark and code, which will be a valuable resource for the community and facilitate future research.
    *   **Addresses limitations and bias in current MLLMs:** Paper addresses the various inherent biases involved in current MLLMs, such as Diagnostic Inequality Risks, Technological Exclusion of Underserved Healthcare Systems, Security Vulnerabilities in MLLMs Diagnostics.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Static 2D Images:** The benchmark relies on static 2D images, which limits its ability to assess the models' understanding of spatial-depth relationships and temporal dynamics crucial for endoscopic procedures. This is explicitly acknowledged in the limitations section.
    *   **Reliance on Multiple-Choice:** The closed-set multiple-choice evaluation format, while facilitating objective assessment, may not fully capture the nuanced reasoning and decision-making processes of clinicians in open-ended scenarios.
    *   **Dataset Imbalance:** The dataset shows an unbalanced distribution. This may result in biased learning.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The creation of EndoBench is a significant contribution to the field of medical AI.  Prior benchmarks were too narrow in scope. EndoBench directly addresses this by creating a diverse resource.
    *   The comparative analysis of different MLLM architectures, including proprietary models, is valuable for understanding the strengths and weaknesses of different approaches.
    *   The identification of key limitations of current MLLMs in endoscopy analysis (lack of domain knowledge, perceptual errors, and incomplete responses) provides important directions for future research.
    *   The paper acknowledges and discusses potential negative social impacts, showing awareness of responsible AI development.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   EndoBench has the potential to become a standard benchmark for evaluating MLLMs in endoscopy, driving progress in the field.
    *   The insights from this paper could inform the development of new MLLM architectures and training strategies tailored to the specific challenges of endoscopic image analysis.
    *   The public release of EndoBench could stimulate further research and collaboration within the medical AI community.

**Score:** 8.5

**Rationale:** EndoBench represents a significant advancement in the evaluation of MLLMs for endoscopy. Its comprehensive scope, clinically relevant tasks, rigorous methodology, and extensive experiments make it a valuable contribution to the field. While the reliance on static 2D images and multiple-choice questions limits its ecological validity to some extent, these limitations are clearly acknowledged. EndoBench has the potential to significantly influence future research and development in this area, promoting the creation of more reliable and clinically useful AI tools for endoscopy. The high score reflects its novelty, comprehensiveness, and potential impact.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Muddit: Liberating Generation Beyond Text-to-Image with a Unified Discrete Diffusion Model
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23606v1)
- **Authors**: Qingyu Shi, Jinbin Bai, Zhuoran Zhao, Wenhao Chai, Kaidong Yu, Jianzong Wu, Shuangyong Song, Yunhai Tong, Xiangtai Li, Xuelong Li, Shuicheng Yan
- **Abstract**: Unified generation models aim to handle diverse tasks across modalities -- such as text generation, image generation, and vision-language reasoning -- within a single architecture and decoding paradigm. Autoregressive unified models suffer from slow inference due to sequential decoding, and non-autoregressive unified models suffer from weak generalization due to limited pretrained backbones. We introduce Muddit, a unified discrete diffusion transformer that enables fast and parallel generation across both text and image modalities. Unlike prior unified diffusion models trained from scratch, Muddit integrates strong visual priors from a pretrained text-to-image backbone with a lightweight text decoder, enabling flexible and high-quality multimodal generation under a unified architecture. Empirical results show that Muddit achieves competitive or superior performance compared to significantly larger autoregressive models in both quality and efficiency. The work highlights the potential of purely discrete diffusion, when equipped with strong visual priors, as a scalable and effective backbone for unified generation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper "Muddit: Liberating Generation Beyond Text-to-Image with a Unified Discrete Diffusion Model":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Muddit, a unified discrete diffusion transformer for multimodal generation (text and images).  Unlike previous unified diffusion models trained from scratch, Muddit leverages pre-trained visual priors from a text-to-image backbone and combines it with a lightweight text decoder.  This allows for fast, parallel generation across modalities within a single architecture.  The authors demonstrate Muddit's competitive or superior performance (quality and efficiency) compared to much larger autoregressive models on tasks like text-to-image synthesis, image-to-text synthesis, and visual question answering. The work emphasizes discrete diffusion's potential as a scalable unified generation backbone, particularly when combined with strong visual priors.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using *discrete diffusion* for *unified* multimodal generation isn't entirely novel (UniDisc, Dual Diffusion attempt this). However, Muddit's main novelty lies in **effectively leveraging a pre-trained text-to-image backbone (Meissonic) as a strong visual prior within a discrete diffusion framework.**  This is a crucial difference, addressing a significant weakness of previous discrete diffusion approaches that struggled with generative fidelity due to a lack of robust pre-trained components.  The image-first approach (instead of language-first), is also a differentiating factor.
*   **Significance:** The significance of this work stems from demonstrating that *purely* discrete diffusion, when equipped with strong pre-trained visual priors, *can* be a viable and competitive approach for unified multimodal generation.  Current trends heavily favor autoregressive models (leveraging LLMs), or continuous diffusion for image synthesis. Muddit suggests a potentially more efficient alternative, especially given the parallel nature of discrete diffusion which addresses the sequential decoding bottleneck of autoregressive methods. The observed speedups in inference compared to AR models is noteworthy.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Addresses limitations:** Directly tackles the identified weaknesses of autoregressive (slow inference) and previous discrete diffusion (poor generative fidelity) approaches.
    *   **Strong empirical results:** Provides compelling evidence that Muddit achieves competitive or superior performance on various multimodal tasks. The GenEval score is particularly compelling.
    *   **Unified Architecture:** The paper convincingly argues that the architecture is truly unified, with the same diffusion process and loss function applied to both text and image generation.  This simplicity is a strength.
    *   **Scalability & Efficiency:** The use of discrete diffusion allows for parallel processing, potentially leading to more efficient scaling compared to autoregressive approaches.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on Meissonic:**  Muddit's performance is heavily dependent on the quality of the pre-trained Meissonic backbone. While this is acknowledged, it also raises concerns about the generalizability to other modalities or if the quality of Meissonic doesn't improve. This is a dependency on existing IP from another source.
    *   **Limited Comparison:** Although the paper compares against several baselines, including diffusion methods, it is important to note there are already unified foundation models with more developed multi-modal capabilities, like Gemini, which is not directly compared.
    *   **Text Understanding Limits:** The approach of a visual-first foundation limits its ability for long-form reasoning and text understanding compared to LLM-driven models. The visual prior might be strong but might impose restrictions to the extent to which it can interpret and adhere to textual instructions.
    *   **Image Resolution Limitations**: Discrete diffusion approaches have shown limitations in generating very high-resolution images.

*   **Impact:** Muddit's impact could be significant if it inspires further research and development of discrete diffusion for multimodal generation. The approach could lead to more efficient and scalable multimodal models, especially for applications where visual priors are strong and fast inference is crucial. The work has the potential to shift the research focus toward exploring discrete diffusion as a viable alternative to autoregressive and continuous diffusion methods.

**Justification for Score:**

Muddit presents a compelling and well-executed approach to unified multimodal generation. The work addresses critical limitations in the field, leveraging pre-trained visual priors within a discrete diffusion framework. The empirical results are strong, demonstrating competitive or superior performance compared to significantly larger autoregressive models.

While the novelty is incremental (building upon existing discrete diffusion and pre-trained backbones), the *effective combination* of these elements is significant.  The reliance on the Meissonic backbone and the limitations in long-form text understanding are weaknesses, but the potential for scalability and efficiency makes it a valuable contribution.

Therefore, I would score this paper a **Score: 7**.

**Rigorous Rationale:** The score reflects Muddit's substantial advancements in using discrete diffusion for multimodal tasks by skillfully integrating robust visual priors from a pre-trained model. This addresses critical limitations and showcases promising performance, while the score also acknowledges Muddit's reliance on pre-existing technology, thus diminishing the overall novelty and highlighting potential difficulties in generalizing the approach to other modalities or backbones. Overall, Muddit presents an interesting alternative pathway for multimodal generation but depends heavily on existing methods.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Inference-time Scaling of Diffusion Models through Classical Search
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23614v1)
- **Authors**: Xiangcheng Zhang, Haowei Lin, Haotian Ye, James Zou, Jianzhu Ma, Yitao Liang, Yilun Du
- **Abstract**: Classical search algorithms have long underpinned modern artificial intelligence. In this work, we tackle the challenge of inference-time control in diffusion models -- adapting generated outputs to meet diverse test-time objectives -- using principles from classical search. We propose a general framework that orchestrates local and global search to efficiently navigate the generative space. It employs a theoretically grounded local search via annealed Langevin MCMC and performs compute-efficient global exploration using breadth-first and depth-first tree search. We evaluate our approach on a range of challenging domains, including planning, offline reinforcement learning, and image generation. Across all tasks, we observe significant gains in both performance and efficiency. These results show that classical search provides a principled and practical foundation for inference-time scaling in diffusion models. Project page at diffusion-inference-scaling.github.io.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a framework for improving the inference-time control of diffusion models by incorporating principles from classical search algorithms.  The core idea is to adapt the generated outputs of diffusion models to meet diverse test-time objectives using a combination of local and global search. Local search is implemented via annealed Langevin MCMC, while global exploration leverages breadth-first search (BFS) and depth-first search (DFS) on the generative space. The authors evaluate their approach across various domains, including planning, offline reinforcement learning, and image generation, demonstrating significant gains in both performance and efficiency. They also formalize sample evaluation through a verifier function, enabling strategic navigation within the generative manifold.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** While the individual components (diffusion models, classical search, Langevin MCMC) are not novel, the *combination* and application to inference-time scaling in diffusion models is reasonably novel. The key is the *orchestration* of local (gradient-guided) and global (tree-based) search within the latent space of a diffusion model.  The paper's strength lies in proposing a unified framework and demonstrating how different classical search techniques can be adapted to the diffusion model setting. The theoretical connection of training-free guidance with recurrence to Langevin MCMC contributes to the theoretical novelty. However, similar ideas have been explored in other contexts, such as tree-of-thoughts reasoning in large language models, reducing the overall novelty to a moderate extent.

* **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial challenge in diffusion models: the lack of direct control over the generated outputs during inference.  By introducing search, it becomes possible to steer the generation process towards outputs that satisfy specific criteria, opening up new applications in areas like robotics, planning, and controllable content generation.  The gains in computational efficiency compared to naive "best-of-N" sampling are significant and practical. The results demonstrating improvements in tasks like maze planning and image generation using task-specific verifiers are noteworthy. The paper's significance is further bolstered by its systematic evaluation and clear demonstration of compute efficiency improvements.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Unified Framework:** Provides a general framework for inference-time search in diffusion models.
    * **Principled Approach:** Grounded in classical search and Langevin MCMC.
    * **Strong Empirical Results:** Demonstrates improvements in several challenging domains.
    * **Compute Efficiency:** Shows significant gains in efficiency compared to baselines.
    * **Theoretical Contribution:** Provides a theoretical unification of training-free guidance and Langevin MCMC.
    * **Clarity:** The paper is well-written and easy to follow, making the concepts accessible.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Hyperparameter Tuning:** The method still requires significant hyperparameter tuning, potentially limiting its ease of use in practice. Though addressed with the mention of adaptive hyperparameter tuning for DFS and an analysis of parameters in Section C.5, this doesn't completely mitigate concerns about general usability.
    * **Verifier Dependence:** The performance is highly dependent on the quality of the verifier function, which can be challenging to design and train. The "double-verifier" approach to address adversarial examples partially mitigates this but adds complexity. This isn't necessarily a flaw, but a crucial consideration for adoption.
    * **Limited Theoretical Guarantees:** While the Langevin MCMC component has theoretical convergence guarantees, the behavior of the overall framework with global search is less theoretically understood.
    * **Incremental Contribution:** Although the combination of existing techniques is effective, the individual techniques themselves aren't novel. The theoretical unifications are incremental.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of diffusion models by providing a practical and principled approach to inference-time control.  It could inspire further research on combining classical search techniques with modern generative models and lead to new applications of diffusion models in various domains.

**Overall Score and Justification:**

The paper provides a well-executed and reasonably novel approach to improving inference-time control in diffusion models. It combines existing techniques in a non-trivial way and demonstrates substantial empirical gains. However, the reliance on hyperparameter tuning and verifier functions, along with the somewhat incremental nature of the theoretical contributions, limits its overall impact. Therefore, a score of **7** is appropriate.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Characterizing the Expressivity of Transformer Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23623v1)
- **Authors**: Jiaoda Li, Ryan Cotterell
- **Abstract**: Transformer-based language models (LMs) have achieved widespread empirical success, but their theoretical expressive power remains only partially understood. Prior work often relies on idealized models with assumptions -- such as arbitrary numerical precision and hard attention -- that diverge from real-world transformers. In this work, we provide an exact characterization of fixed-precision transformers with strict future masking and soft attention, an idealization that more closely mirrors practical implementations. We show that these models are precisely as expressive as a specific fragment of linear temporal logic that includes only a single temporal operator: the past operator. We further relate this logic to established classes in formal language theory, automata theory, and algebra, yielding a rich and unified theoretical framework for understanding transformer expressivity. Finally, we present empirical results that align closely with our theory: transformers trained on languages within their theoretical capacity generalize perfectly over lengths, while they consistently fail to generalize on languages beyond it.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper provides a precise characterization of the expressive power of fixed-precision transformers with strict future masking and soft attention. It demonstrates that these models are exactly as expressive as a fragment of linear temporal logic (LTL) containing only the past operator (LTL[◆]). This connection is further related to partially ordered deterministic finite automata (p.o. DFAs), R-trivial monoids, and left-deterministic polynomials, creating a unified theoretical framework. The paper also presents empirical results that support the theory: transformers generalize well on languages within their theoretical capacity but fail on languages beyond it. The study includes transformer language models and discusses their equivalence to transformer recognizers in expressive power. The paper offers a fine-grained landscape that delineates what standard fixed-precision transformers can and cannot recognize.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in providing an exact characterization of fixed-precision transformers with soft attention and strict masking using LTL[◆]. Prior work had established bounds (e.g., C-RASP) or considered different attention mechanisms (UHA). Resolving this open question is a significant step forward. The connection to p.o. DFAs, R-trivial monoids, and left-deterministic polynomials further enriches the framework.

* **Significance:** The significance of this work is multi-faceted. First, it provides a more realistic theoretical understanding of transformers, as it deals with fixed precision, a crucial constraint in practical implementations. The results provide a more accurate picture of transformer capabilities and limitations compared to works that assume arbitrary precision. It accurately mirrors empirical results by showing which languages can be recognized by standard transformers and those they struggle with. Second, the connection to LTL and other formal language classes allows leveraging a rich body of existing theory to better understand transformers.  The work directly challenges claims that transformers can easily recognize languages like the bounded Dyck language without special architectural choices. Third, identifying a limitation may spur the development of modifications that improve a transformer's expressive capacity while maintaining computational tractability.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Precise characterization:** The paper provides an *exact* characterization, not just an upper or lower bound.
    * **Realistic setting:** The focus on fixed precision makes the results highly relevant to practical applications.
    * **Strong theoretical foundation:**  Connecting transformers to established formal language theory and logic provides a solid foundation for understanding their capabilities.
    * **Empirical validation:** The experiments strongly support the theoretical claims.
    * **Completeness:** Includes language modeling, extending the recognizer result to models of practical interest.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited scope:** While the paper focuses on a more realistic setting (fixed precision), it still deals with an idealized model (strict masking and absence of positional encoding, though the latter is addressed).  Real-world implementations might deviate in subtle ways.
    * **Focus on recognizers:** While language models are covered, the focus is predominantly on language recognizers which is a theoretical construct and simplification.

* **Justification of Score:**

The paper provides a substantial contribution to the field by resolving an important open question and providing a tight characterization of transformer expressivity under realistic constraints. The theoretical rigor, combined with empirical validation, makes this a high-quality and influential paper. It is essential reading for researchers working on understanding the theoretical properties of neural networks, particularly those interested in transformers and formal language theory.  The paper offers concrete guidance and constraints for building better and more theoretically grounded transformer architectures. However, it is also a somewhat incremental step from prior work by Yang et al. [47] and Yang and Chiang [46] as it refines existing bounds. There are also limitations in scope of what real-world models this addresses.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### ZeroSep: Separate Anything in Audio with Zero Training
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23625v1)
- **Authors**: Chao Huang, Yuesheng Ma, Junxuan Huang, Susan Liang, Yunlong Tang, Jing Bi, Wenqiang Liu, Nima Mesgarani, Chenliang Xu
- **Abstract**: Audio source separation is fundamental for machines to understand complex acoustic environments and underpins numerous audio applications. Current supervised deep learning approaches, while powerful, are limited by the need for extensive, task-specific labeled data and struggle to generalize to the immense variability and open-set nature of real-world acoustic scenes. Inspired by the success of generative foundation models, we investigate whether pre-trained text-guided audio diffusion models can overcome these limitations. We make a surprising discovery: zero-shot source separation can be achieved purely through a pre-trained text-guided audio diffusion model under the right configuration. Our method, named ZeroSep, works by inverting the mixed audio into the diffusion model's latent space and then using text conditioning to guide the denoising process to recover individual sources. Without any task-specific training or fine-tuning, ZeroSep repurposes the generative diffusion model for a discriminative separation task and inherently supports open-set scenarios through its rich textual priors. ZeroSep is compatible with a variety of pre-trained text-guided audio diffusion backbones and delivers strong separation performance on multiple separation benchmarks, surpassing even supervised methods.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the ZeroSep paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ZeroSep, a novel zero-shot audio source separation framework. It leverages pre-trained text-guided audio diffusion models to separate audio mixtures into their constituent sources *without* any task-specific training or fine-tuning.  ZeroSep works by inverting the mixed audio into the diffusion model's latent space and then using text conditioning to guide the denoising process, recovering individual sources based on text prompts. The authors demonstrate that ZeroSep achieves competitive, and in some cases surpassing, separation performance compared to supervised methods on several benchmarks. This is achieved by carefully balancing unconditional and conditional predictions, allowing for separation rather than pure generation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The central idea of using pre-trained text-to-audio diffusion models for zero-shot source separation is innovative. While previous works have explored language-conditioned separation, they still rely on supervised training.  ZeroSep's training-free approach represents a significant departure from this paradigm. The surprising discovery that a generative model can be repurposed for a discriminative task through careful configuration is a valuable contribution. However, the underlying techniques (diffusion models, DDIM/DDPM inversion, classifier-free guidance) are already well-established; the core novelty lies in the *combination* and *configuration* for this specific task. The open-set capability is also a notable advantage.

*   **Significance:** If the claims hold up to further scrutiny, ZeroSep has the potential to significantly impact the field.  The elimination of task-specific training addresses a major bottleneck in audio source separation, potentially opening doors for separating sources in diverse and complex real-world acoustic scenes where labeled data is scarce or unavailable. The method's versatility across different diffusion backbones adds to its practical value. The finding that better generative models lead to better separation provides a clear direction for future research. The potential to extend ZeroSep into spatial audio separation adds to the significance.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Truly zero-shot: No fine-tuning or task-specific data is required.
    *   Competitive Performance: Achieves results comparable to or better than some supervised methods, especially on the MUSIC dataset.
    *   Open-Set Capabilities: Handles diverse mixtures and queries without retraining.
    *   Modular and Versatile: Works with different pre-trained diffusion backbones.
    *   Clear Ablation Studies: Demonstrates the importance of key components and parameters.
    *   Good qualitative results and supplementary material, including a web demo, add to the impact.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Relies on pre-trained text-to-audio diffusion models: Performance is heavily dependent on the quality and capabilities of these models.  Bias in the training data of the diffusion model could translate into biases in the separation results. The limitations of diffusion inversion methods are also inherited.
    *   Computational cost: Diffusion models are computationally expensive, which might limit the real-time applicability of ZeroSep.
    *   Failure Cases: As acknowledged in the paper, it can struggle with complex mixtures where interfering sources possess significant energy, and the number of diffusion steps are limited.
    *   Potential for Artifacts: Generative models, even when carefully configured, can introduce artifacts or distortions. Although the paper addresses this, it remains a potential concern.
    *   Limited Metrics for Perceptual Quality: While feature-based metrics such as FAD and LPAPS are used, more robust perceptual quality evaluations would strengthen the claims.

*   **Potential Influence:** ZeroSep could inspire a new wave of research in training-free audio source separation and encourage the development of better text-guided audio diffusion models.  It offers a compelling alternative to supervised learning, particularly for scenarios where labeled data is scarce or when dealing with novel acoustic events. It could also impact applications like automated music remixing, audio editing, and noise reduction in hearing aids.

*   **Justification of Score:** The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of audio source separation by demonstrating a training-free approach that leverages pre-trained diffusion models. While the method is dependent on the quality of pre-trained models and has limitations regarding computational costs and failure cases, the potential impact of eliminating the need for supervised training and addressing the open-set scenarios cannot be understated.  The clearly written paper, strong ablation studies, and supplementary materials further enhance the impact.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.SD
- **Score**: 7/10

### AutoSchemaKG: Autonomous Knowledge Graph Construction through Dynamic Schema Induction from Web-Scale Corpora
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23628v1)
- **Authors**: Jiaxin Bai, Wei Fan, Qi Hu, Qing Zong, Chunyang Li, Hong Ting Tsang, Hongyu Luo, Yauwai Yim, Haoyu Huang, Xiao Zhou, Feng Qin, Tianshi Zheng, Xi Peng, Xin Yao, Huiwen Yang, Leijie Wu, Yi Ji, Gong Zhang, Renhai Chen, Yangqiu Song
- **Abstract**: We present AutoSchemaKG, a framework for fully autonomous knowledge graph construction that eliminates the need for predefined schemas. Our system leverages large language models to simultaneously extract knowledge triples and induce comprehensive schemas directly from text, modeling both entities and events while employing conceptualization to organize instances into semantic categories. Processing over 50 million documents, we construct ATLAS (Automated Triple Linking And Schema induction), a family of knowledge graphs with 900+ million nodes and 5.9 billion edges. This approach outperforms state-of-the-art baselines on multi-hop QA tasks and enhances LLM factuality. Notably, our schema induction achieves 95\% semantic alignment with human-crafted schemas with zero manual intervention, demonstrating that billion-scale knowledge graphs with dynamically induced schemas can effectively complement parametric knowledge in large language models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "AutoSchemaKG: Autonomous Knowledge Graph Construction through Dynamic Schema Induction from Web-Scale Corpora":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces AutoSchemaKG, a novel framework for autonomously constructing knowledge graphs (KGs) from unstructured text data. Unlike traditional KG construction methods that rely on predefined schemas crafted by domain experts, AutoSchemaKG dynamically induces schemas directly from the text using large language models (LLMs). The framework simultaneously extracts knowledge triples (entity-entity, entity-event, event-event relationships) and conceptualizes these triples into broader semantic categories, effectively generating a schema on the fly.  The authors construct a large-scale KG, ATLAS, by processing over 50 million documents from diverse sources (Wikipedia, Semantic Scholar, Common Crawl). They demonstrate that ATLAS achieves high semantic alignment with human-crafted schemas and outperforms state-of-the-art baselines in downstream tasks like multi-hop question answering and factuality enhancement in LLMs. A key aspect of AutoSchemaKG is the incorporation of events as first-class citizens in the KG, allowing it to capture temporal relationships and procedural knowledge missed by traditional entity-only KGs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its ability to autonomously construct large-scale KGs *without* predefined schemas. While previous works have explored schema induction, AutoSchemaKG distinguishes itself through its holistic approach of simultaneously extracting triples, inducing schemas, and modelling *both* entities and events at scale. The conceptualization process and its use for schema induction are also a novel contribution. The comparison with retrieval-augmented generation from the pretraining dataset's source is interesting, though perhaps less innovative than the core schema induction process.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from addressing a critical bottleneck in KG construction: the manual schema creation process. By automating schema induction, AutoSchemaKG offers the potential to create more scalable, adaptable, and comprehensive KGs that can evolve with the data. Demonstrating improved performance on multi-hop QA and LLM factuality further highlights its practical impact. The high semantic alignment with human-crafted schemas is a strong indicator of the quality of the induced schemas. The event-centric approach is also significant, as it captures richer relational knowledge than entity-only approaches.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Autonomous Schema Induction:** Eliminating the need for manual schema creation is a major strength.
    *   **Scalability:** The construction of ATLAS demonstrates the framework's ability to handle web-scale data.
    *   **Comprehensive Knowledge Representation:** Incorporating both entities and events provides a richer semantic structure.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** Demonstrating improvements on downstream tasks validates the effectiveness of AutoSchemaKG.
    *   **High Semantic Alignment:** Achieving 95% alignment with human-crafted schemas without manual intervention is impressive.
    *   **Event-Centric Approach:** Captures richer knowledge representation than entity-centric models.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on LLMs:** The performance of AutoSchemaKG is heavily dependent on the capabilities of the underlying LLMs. Biases and limitations of these models could affect the quality of the KG. Also, the very technical and specialized domains struggle.
    *   **Computational Cost:** Constructing billion-scale KGs is computationally expensive, potentially limiting accessibility.
    *   **Potential for Inconsistencies:** While achieving high semantic alignment, the framework could still introduce inconsistencies or contradictions due to errors in extraction or conceptualization. The reliance on a counting-based evaluation method with DeepSeek-V3 as a judge is solid, but still relies on the judgements of another model.
    *   **Limited Downstream Task Variety:** While multi-hop QA and factuality enhancement are valuable, exploring performance on a broader range of KG-driven applications would strengthen the paper.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's findings could significantly influence the KG construction and utilization fields. AutoSchemaKG provides a pathway for building more adaptable and scalable KGs, enabling more advanced reasoning and knowledge-intensive applications. The event-centric approach could also inspire new research directions in knowledge representation. The result is really more of a "schema-aware knowledge graph."

*   **Justification of Score:**

AutoSchemaKG presents a substantial advance in KG construction, tackling a key limitation of existing methods. The autonomous schema induction, large-scale construction, and empirical validation justify a high score. The limitations regarding LLM dependence, computational cost, and downstream task variety are acknowledged. Because of these limitations, I will not give it an exceptional score, but rather a high score.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.CL

### MCP Safety Training: Learning to Refuse Falsely Benign MCP Exploits using Improved Preference Alignment
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23634v1)
- **Authors**: John Halloran
- **Abstract**: The model context protocol (MCP) has been widely adapted as an open standard enabling the seamless integration of generative AI agents. However, recent work has shown the MCP is susceptible to retrieval-based "falsely benign" attacks (FBAs), allowing malicious system access and credential theft, but requiring that users download compromised files directly to their systems. Herein, we show that the threat model of MCP-based attacks is significantly broader than previously thought, i.e., attackers need only post malicious content online to deceive MCP agents into carrying out their attacks on unsuspecting victims' systems. To improve alignment guardrails against such attacks, we introduce a new MCP dataset of FBAs and (truly) benign samples to explore the effectiveness of direct preference optimization (DPO) for the refusal training of large language models (LLMs). While DPO improves model guardrails against such attacks, we show that the efficacy of refusal learning varies drastically depending on the model's original post-training alignment scheme--e.g., GRPO-based LLMs learn to refuse extremely poorly. Thus, to further improve FBA refusals, we introduce Retrieval Augmented Generation for Preference alignment (RAG-Pref), a novel preference alignment strategy based on RAG. We show that RAG-Pref significantly improves the ability of LLMs to refuse FBAs, particularly when combined with DPO alignment, thus drastically improving guardrails against MCP-based attacks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the security risks associated with the Model Context Protocol (MCP), an open standard for integrating generative AI agents. It highlights the susceptibility of MCP to "falsely benign attacks" (FBAs) where attackers need only post malicious content online to deceive MCP agents into carrying out attacks on unsuspecting victims' systems. The authors introduce a new MCP dataset, MCP-FBAs, and explore the effectiveness of direct preference optimization (DPO) for refusal training of large language models (LLMs). They find DPO's effectiveness varies, and introduce Retrieval Augmented Generation for Preference alignment (RAG-Pref), a novel preference alignment strategy based on RAG, to improve FBA refusals, particularly when combined with DPO. The paper also introduces Total Retrieval-Agent Deception (TRADE), a new MCP attack framework and new LLM refusal metrics that reflect practical LLM inference settings and the immediate impact of MCP-enabled attacks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper introduces several novel elements:

*   **TRADE Attack Framework:** Expanding the threat model beyond RADE by showing that attackers don't need to trick users into downloading files, but can exploit publicly available online content. This significantly widens the attack surface.
*   **MCP-FBAs Dataset:** Addressing the lack of dedicated MCP attack data by creating a high-quality dataset of FBAs and truly benign samples. This is a valuable contribution for future research.
*   **Refusal Metrics for MCP:**  Introducing more stringent and context-aware evaluation metrics, especially considering the real-world impact of MCP exploits. The focus on multi-generation performance is a significant advancement.
*   **RAG-Pref Algorithm:** Developing a novel RAG-based preference alignment method that outperforms DPO in many scenarios and is complementary to it.
*   **Demonstration of vulnerabilities in widely used LLMs (including those with safety alignment):** Demonstrating that even state-of-the-art LLMs struggle with FBAs, especially those leveraging reasoning capabilities tuned with GRPO.

**Significance:**

The significance of this work lies in:

*   **Highlighting a critical vulnerability in a widely adopted protocol:**  The MCP's widespread adoption makes this a high-impact problem. Identifying weaknesses early allows for proactive mitigation.
*   **Providing practical solutions:** The RAG-Pref algorithm offers a relatively simple, training-free approach to improve refusal capabilities and mitigate FBAs.
*   **Improving the evaluation of LLMs for safety-critical applications:** By introducing stricter refusal metrics that reflect real-world inference settings, the authors enable more accurate assessments of LLM safety and security.
*   **Understanding how different model types react to FBA attacks.** Showing how GRPO-based models are especially vulnerable provides critical context for future safety research.

**Strengths:**

*   Well-defined problem and clearly articulated goals.
*   Strong empirical evaluation using a new, relevant dataset.
*   Detailed analysis of different alignment techniques and their interactions.
*   Introduction of practical and effective mitigation strategies (RAG-Pref).
*   Emphasis on real-world attack scenarios and more stringent refusal metrics.
*   Addresses important aspects related to agentic security.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited scope:**  The study primarily focuses on the FileSystem server within the MCP. The effectiveness of the proposed solutions may vary for other MCP servers and tools. Future work that explores broader MCP-tooling attacks would improve this analysis.
*   **Specific model architectures:** While the study considers a range of LLMs, it might not be fully representative of all possible architectures and training paradigms. However, this is unavoidable given the rapid evolution of the field.
*   **Complexity of the Experimental Setup:** DPO's implementation and many parameters that require ablation, but lack of space in the manuscript may limit depth in discussing DPO-aligned hyperparameter choices.
*   **The RAG-Pref algorithm improves refusal, but the dataset for determining how to retrieve the 'right' information requires curation.** The real-world use case is limited by the user's ability to identify the right contexts for retrieval and, subsequently, defense.

**Overall:**

This paper makes a significant contribution by identifying a critical vulnerability in the MCP, providing a novel dataset for evaluating MCP security, and developing a practical mitigation strategy.  The analysis of DPO and the introduction of RAG-Pref offer valuable insights into improving LLM security for agentic applications. The emphasis on realistic attack scenarios and stringent metrics strengthens the paper's practical relevance. While the scope is limited to the FileSystem server, the findings have broader implications for MCP security and warrant further investigation. The weakness around RAG-Pref input curation needs to be addressed with follow-on research.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:** The paper presents novel contributions that are highly relevant to the field. The TRADE attack framework is a very important real-world addition to agentic research. The introduction of the MCP-FBAs dataset fills a gap in the security data available for agentic testing, and the RAG-Pref algorithm provides a practical way to mitigate FBAs. The score reflects the overall high quality of the work, with a slight deduction due to the focus on a specific MCP server and the RAG-Pref curation aspect. These limitations indicate opportunities for future research rather than fundamental flaws. These findings have implications across a wider range of agentic application.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### Are Reasoning Models More Prone to Hallucination?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23646v1)
- **Authors**: Zijun Yao, Yantao Liu, Yanxu Chen, Jianhui Chen, Junfeng Fang, Lei Hou, Juanzi Li, Tat-Seng Chua
- **Abstract**: Recently evolved large reasoning models (LRMs) show powerful performance in solving complex tasks with long chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning capability. As these LRMs are mostly developed by post-training on formal reasoning tasks, whether they generalize the reasoning capability to help reduce hallucination in fact-seeking tasks remains unclear and debated. For instance, DeepSeek-R1 reports increased performance on SimpleQA, a fact-seeking benchmark, while OpenAI-o3 observes even severer hallucination. This discrepancy naturally raises the following research question: Are reasoning models more prone to hallucination? This paper addresses the question from three perspectives. (1) We first conduct a holistic evaluation for the hallucination in LRMs. Our analysis reveals that LRMs undergo a full post-training pipeline with cold start supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and verifiable reward RL generally alleviate their hallucination. In contrast, both distillation alone and RL training without cold start fine-tuning introduce more nuanced hallucinations. (2) To explore why different post-training pipelines alters the impact on hallucination in LRMs, we conduct behavior analysis. We characterize two critical cognitive behaviors that directly affect the factuality of a LRM: Flaw Repetition, where the surface-level reasoning attempts repeatedly follow the same underlying flawed logic, and Think-Answer Mismatch, where the final answer fails to faithfully match the previous CoT process. (3) Further, we investigate the mechanism behind the hallucination of LRMs from the perspective of model uncertainty. We find that increased hallucination of LRMs is usually associated with the misalignment between model uncertainty and factual accuracy. Our work provides an initial understanding of the hallucination in LRMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the phenomenon of hallucination in Large Reasoning Models (LRMs), questioning whether their reasoning capabilities, enhanced through post-training, necessarily lead to more factual accuracy.  The study evaluates LRMs trained with different post-training pipelines: (1) SFT+RL (cold start supervised fine-tuning followed by reinforcement learning), (2) RL-only, and (3) SFT-only (distillation). The authors find that LRMs developed with SFT+RL generally exhibit reduced hallucination, while RL-only and SFT-only training can *increase* hallucination. They identify two key cognitive behaviors contributing to hallucination:  Flaw Repetition (repeating flawed logic) and Think-Answer Mismatch (final answer not aligning with the reasoning process). Finally, they explore the mechanism from the perspective of model uncertainty, finding that misalignment between model uncertainty and factual accuracy correlates with increased hallucination.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's central question, "Are Reasoning Models More Prone to Hallucination?" is timely and addresses a critical concern in the development of LLMs.  The systematic evaluation of different post-training pipelines and the identification of Flaw Repetition and Think-Answer Mismatch is also novel. The uncertainty analysis, while not entirely new in the context of LLMs, provides a specific and useful perspective on hallucination within reasoning models. *However*, some individual components, such as the use of LLM-as-a-Judge, are not entirely novel methods. The combination of these methods to address the specific issue of hallucination in *reasoning* models is where the novelty primarily lies.

* **Significance:** The paper's findings are significant because they challenge the common assumption that simply equipping a model with reasoning capabilities automatically reduces hallucination.  The identification of post-training pipelines that *increase* hallucination is particularly important for model developers. The cognitive behavior analysis provides insights that can guide the design of better reasoning processes. Furthermore, the uncertainty perspective opens up avenues for developing methods to detect and mitigate hallucination. *However*, the work has some limitations as indicated by the authors, making it hard to make a strong claim.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper thoroughly evaluates a range of LRMs with varying post-training methods.
    * **Detailed Analysis:** The cognitive behavior analysis and uncertainty probing provide valuable insights into the causes of hallucination.
    * **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly presents the experimental setup, results, and analysis.
    * **Reproducibility:** The code availability enhances the reproducibility and allows for further investigation.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Scope:** The study focuses primarily on short-form QA tasks, and may not generalize to other generation tasks (long-form, multi-turn dialogue).
    * **LLM-as-a-Judge Subjectivity:** While Qwen3-32B is a good choice, LLM-as-a-Judge introduces potential subjectivity into the evaluation process. This is mitigated by using a state-of-the-art reasoning LLM for judging, but alternative judging strategies are not explored.
    * **Limited Control:** The study relies on existing models, limiting the control over training data and hyperparameters.
    * **Parameter Shift Analysis Limitations:** The parameter shift analysis fails to conclusively demonstrate or negate the role of catastrophic forgetting and would benefit from a more detailed exploration.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more reliable LRMs by highlighting the importance of factuality evaluation during model development and by providing insights into the causes and mechanisms of hallucination. It should also push for more exploration with different judgements and more detailed controlled training.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a valuable and timely investigation into hallucination in LRMs. The comprehensive evaluation and detailed analysis of post-training pipelines, cognitive behaviors, and uncertainty mechanisms contribute significantly to our understanding of the problem. *While the study has some limitations in scope and methodology*, the findings are important and have the potential to influence the development of more reliable and trustworthy LRMs. It provides a good start and framework for future research into LRMs.

### Continuous Chain of Thought Enables Parallel Exploration and Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23648v1)
- **Authors**: Halil Alperen Gozeten, M. Emrullah Ildiz, Xuechen Zhang, Hrayr Harutyunyan, Ankit Singh Rawat, Samet Oymak
- **Abstract**: Current language models generate chain-of-thought traces by autoregressively sampling tokens from a finite vocabulary. While this discrete sampling has achieved remarkable success, conducting chain-of-thought with continuously-valued tokens (CoT2) offers a richer and more expressive alternative. Our work examines the benefits of CoT2 through logical reasoning tasks that inherently require search capabilities and provide optimization and exploration methods for CoT2. Theoretically, we show that CoT2 allows the model to track multiple traces in parallel and quantify its benefits for inference efficiency. Notably, one layer transformer equipped with CoT2 can provably solve the combinatorial "subset sum problem" given sufficient embedding dimension. These insights lead to a novel and effective supervision strategy where we match the softmax outputs to the empirical token distributions of a set of target traces. Complementing this, we introduce sampling strategies that unlock policy optimization and self-improvement for CoT2. Our first strategy samples and composes $K$ discrete tokens at each decoding step to control the level of parallelism, and reduces to standard CoT when $K=1$. Our second strategy relies on continuous exploration over the probability simplex. Experiments confirm that policy optimization with CoT2 indeed improves the performance of the model beyond its initial discrete or continuous supervision.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Continuous Chain of Thought (CoT2), an alternative to the standard discrete Chain of Thought (CoT) approach in language models.  Instead of autoregressively sampling discrete tokens from a fixed vocabulary, CoT2 utilizes continuously-valued tokens. The authors argue this offers richer expressiveness and enables parallel exploration of reasoning paths.  They demonstrate theoretically that CoT2 allows tracking multiple traces in parallel, enhancing inference efficiency. They show a single-layer transformer with CoT2 can solve a combinatorial problem (subset sum) under certain conditions.  They introduce a novel supervision strategy (CSFT) matching softmax outputs to empirical token distributions of target traces and propose sampling strategies to unlock policy optimization and self-improvement, including a multi-token sampling method (CoT2-MTS) and continuous exploration over the probability simplex. Experiments on logical reasoning tasks validate the effectiveness of CoT2 and its improvement through policy optimization.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper tackles a significant limitation of current language model architectures: discrete token sampling, which limits information density and exploration. The core idea of CoT2 – using continuous token representations to enable parallel reasoning – is conceptually novel and potentially powerful.

**Strengths:**

*   **Theoretical Foundation:** The paper provides theoretical justification for CoT2, demonstrating its ability to track multiple traces and solve the subset sum problem. This is a strong point, as it provides a more solid footing for the approach compared to purely empirical methods. The theoretical comparison to discrete CoT also helps clarify the potential benefits in terms of sample complexity.
*   **Algorithmic Contributions:** The paper introduces concrete algorithms (Base CoT2, CoT2-MTS) and training strategies (CSFT) to realize the CoT2 concept.  CoT2-MTS provides a flexible way to control the level of parallelism. The addition of the Dirichlet sampling strategy enhances exploration.
*   **Empirical Validation:** The paper presents experimental results on MNNS, ProntoQA, and ProsQA, demonstrating the effectiveness of CoT2 and the benefits of policy optimization. The results consistently show improvements over discrete baselines under suitable conditions (e.g., exceeding a certain embedding dimension threshold).
*   **Novelty:** The approach of using continuous token representations for chain-of-thought reasoning, the explicit multi-trace supervision through CSFT, and the integration of reinforcement learning are significant departures from standard CoT techniques. The idea of using the *softmax output* to deterministically construct continuous tokens is novel.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Scalability Concerns:** The paper acknowledges that CoT2 might require larger embedding dimensions, which could pose challenges for scaling to more complex tasks and larger models. The practical cost of achieving sufficient embedding capacity is not deeply investigated.
*   **Task Dependence:** The experiments focus on logical reasoning tasks with inherent search capabilities. It's not clear how well CoT2 would generalize to other types of reasoning tasks or to open-ended text generation, where the benefits of parallel exploration might be less pronounced.
*   **Interpretability:** While increasing performance, the paper notes that the continuous representation shadows the model’s intermediate decision process and could reduce interpretability. This trade-off needs to be considered, especially if interpretability is a requirement.
*   **Implementation Complexity:** The implementation seems more complex than standard CoT, and some of the techniques (Dirichlet sampling for RL) require tuning extra hyperparameters.
*   **Limited Ablation Studies**: While various methods are presented, the study does not involve detailed ablation studies for each component. This lack of a clear contribution analysis makes it difficult to assess each strategy’s specific contribution and interdependency.

**Significance:**

The paper opens a promising avenue for research in chain-of-thought reasoning. It challenges the dominant paradigm of discrete token sampling and provides a framework for exploring continuous representations. If the scalability concerns can be addressed, CoT2 could lead to more efficient and powerful reasoning systems. The link to policy optimization through RL is also a significant contribution, paving the way for self-improving reasoning models.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper presents a novel idea with theoretical grounding and empirical validation, the scalability and task dependence concerns, as well as the somewhat complex implementation, prevent it from achieving a very high score. The significance is somewhat limited by the focus on specific reasoning tasks and the potential limitations in interpretability.
