### Optimization-Free Diffusion Model -- A Perturbation Theory Approach
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23652v1)
- **Authors**: Yuehaw Khoo, Mathias Oster, Yifan Peng
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models have emerged as a powerful framework in generative modeling, typically relying on optimizing neural networks to estimate the score function via forward SDE simulations. In this work, we propose an alternative method that is both optimization-free and forward SDE-free. By expanding the score function in a sparse set of eigenbasis of the backward Kolmogorov operator associated with the diffusion process, we reformulate score estimation as the solution to a linear system, avoiding iterative optimization and time-dependent sample generation. We analyze the approximation error using perturbation theory and demonstrate the effectiveness of our method on high-dimensional Boltzmann distributions and real-world datasets.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces an optimization-free approach for diffusion models.  Instead of learning the score function through neural network training and forward SDE simulations, it proposes representing the score function as an expansion in eigenfunctions of the backward Kolmogorov operator associated with the diffusion process's base distribution.  This reformulation transforms score estimation into solving a linear system, avoiding iterative optimization and time-dependent sample generation.  The paper analyzes the approximation error using perturbation theory, demonstrating the method's effectiveness on high-dimensional Boltzmann distributions and real-world datasets. It addresses the curse of dimensionality by projecting the score function onto a sparse cluster basis. The overall approach claims to be both optimization-free and forward SDE-free, relying instead on numerical linear algebra techniques.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its *optimization-free* and *forward SDE-free* approach to diffusion models.  The shift from training neural networks to solve a linear system, leveraging the spectral properties of the backward Kolmogorov operator, presents a conceptually different perspective. The idea of using a cluster basis to mitigate the curse of dimensionality is also a relevant, though not entirely new, idea in high-dimensional approximation, but it is a worthwhile adaptation in this context.

*   **Significance:** The potential significance stems from several factors:
    *   **Computational Efficiency:** If the linear systems can be solved efficiently and the required eigenbasis computations are tractable, the method *could* offer computational advantages over traditional diffusion models, particularly in scenarios where neural network training is expensive or unstable. The fast numerical techniques such as randomized SVD help in this regard.
    *   **Theoretical Tractability:** The use of a linear system, coupled with perturbation theory, offers a more amenable framework for theoretical analysis compared to the complexities of neural network optimization. The theoretical results help in understanding and quantifying the score estimation error and the potential limitations of the proposed method.
    *   **Interpretability:** Expressing the score function in terms of eigenfunctions could potentially lead to more interpretable models compared to black-box neural networks, though this is not thoroughly explored in the paper.
    *   **Beyond Neural Networks:** The paper pushes beyond the reliance of neural networks and offers new perspectives on estimating the score function accurately.

*   **Weaknesses and Limitations:**
    *   **Eigenfunction Computation:** The practical applicability hinges on the ability to efficiently compute the eigenfunctions of the backward Kolmogorov operator.  While the paper describes approaches, the computational cost and scalability of this step, particularly for complex base distributions, remain a crucial concern and may limit the overall applicability.
    *   **Base Distribution Choice:** The performance is likely sensitive to the choice of the base distribution.  The paper utilizes mean-field approximations, which may not always be accurate, especially if the underlying data distribution exhibits strong correlations.
    *   **Linearity Assumption:** Representing the score function as a linear combination of eigenfunctions imposes a linearity constraint that *could* limit the model's expressiveness compared to the non-linear representations learned by neural networks. The perturbation theory analysis offers some guarantees when the data density is "close" to the base distribution, but the method's robustness to significant deviations from this assumption needs further investigation.
    *   **Practical Considerations:** The details of implementation could have benefitted from expanded discussion.
    *   **Experimental Validation:** While the paper presents results on Boltzmann distributions and MNIST, more comprehensive benchmarks, particularly on high-resolution image datasets, would be necessary to establish the method's competitiveness with state-of-the-art diffusion models. The experimental section needs more detail on the exact setup and parameter configurations to allow for rigorous verification and comparison.
    *   **Monte Carlo Error:** In the paper error analysis is limited to the score function approximation error. The paper also acknowledged that a small residual approximation error may persist. However, it would strengthen the claims by bounding both the approximation error and Monte Carlo integration error.

*   **Potential Influence:** If the computational and scalability challenges are addressed, this work *could* influence the field by providing a viable alternative to neural network-based diffusion models. It offers a new angle for theoretical analysis and could inspire further research on spectral methods for generative modeling.

**Justification for the Score:**

Despite the weaknesses, the paper presents a novel idea with potential benefits in terms of computational efficiency, theoretical tractability, and interpretability. However, the practicality hinges on factors such as efficient eigenfunction computation and the ability to scale to complex datasets while achieving competitive performance with neural network based diffusion models. There needs to be more substantial evidence of the method's practical benefits relative to existing approaches and its ability to overcome the limitations.

### How does Transformer Learn Implicit Reasoning?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23653v1)
- **Authors**: Jiaran Ye, Zijun Yao, Zhidian Huang, Liangming Pan, Jinxin Liu, Yushi Bai, Amy Xin, Liu Weichuan, Xiaoyin Che, Lei Hou, Juanzi Li
- **Abstract**: Recent work suggests that large language models (LLMs) can perform multi-hop reasoning implicitly -- producing correct answers without explicitly verbalizing intermediate steps -- but the underlying mechanisms remain poorly understood. In this paper, we study how such implicit reasoning emerges by training transformers from scratch in a controlled symbolic environment. Our analysis reveals a three-stage developmental trajectory: early memorization, followed by in-distribution generalization, and eventually cross-distribution generalization. We find that training with atomic triples is not necessary but accelerates learning, and that second-hop generalization relies on query-level exposure to specific compositional structures. To interpret these behaviors, we introduce two diagnostic tools: cross-query semantic patching, which identifies semantically reusable intermediate representations, and a cosine-based representational lens, which reveals that successful reasoning correlates with the cosine-base clustering in hidden space. This clustering phenomenon in turn provides a coherent explanation for the behavioral dynamics observed across training, linking representational structure to reasoning capability. These findings provide new insights into the interpretability of implicit multi-hop reasoning in LLMs, helping to clarify how complex reasoning processes unfold internally and offering pathways to enhance the transparency of such models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "How does Transformer Learn Implicit Reasoning?":

**Summary:**

The paper investigates how Transformers learn implicit multi-hop reasoning, where a model produces correct answers without explicitly verbalizing intermediate steps. The authors train Transformers from scratch on a controlled symbolic environment and analyze the learning trajectory. They identify three developmental stages: memorization, in-distribution generalization, and cross-distribution generalization. The study reveals that training with atomic triples accelerates learning but is not strictly necessary for ID generalization. Crucially, generalization to second-hop queries requires query-level exposure to specific compositional structures. To interpret these behaviors, the authors introduce two novel diagnostic tools: cross-query semantic patching, to identify semantically reusable intermediate representations, and a cosine-based representational lens, to reveal clustering in hidden space correlated with successful reasoning. They link representational structure to reasoning capability, showing successful generalization correlates with cosine-based clustering and that ID triples accelerate learning by constraining the representation space. They also identify that apparent first-hop OOD generalization is actually an artifact of representational alignment induced by ID exposure.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper presents several novel contributions. The identification of the three-stage learning trajectory is interesting, although arguably somewhat intuitive. The introduction of cross-query semantic patching is a good way to localize intermediate entity representations. Furthermore, the use of cosine-based representational lens, and showing that representation cluster is correlated with reasoning capabilities. However, the diagnostic tools are not very complex or computationally demanding.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant question: how do LLMs learn implicit reasoning?  While the symbolic environment is a simplification of real-world language, it provides a level of experimental control that is difficult to achieve with pre-trained models and large datasets. The findings provide valuable insights into the internal mechanisms underlying implicit reasoning, which can inform the development of more transparent and controllable LLMs. The detailed analysis provides a framework for studying similar phenomena in other settings.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Controlled Experimental Setting:** The use of a symbolic environment allows for precise manipulation and observation of the model's learning process.
    *   **Novel Diagnostic Tools:** Cross-query semantic patching and cosine-based representation analysis provide new ways to probe the internal workings of Transformers.
    *   **Comprehensive Analysis:** The paper combines behavioral studies with mechanistic analyses to provide a holistic understanding of implicit reasoning.
    *   **Clear and Well-Written:** The paper is well-structured and easy to follow, making the findings accessible to a broad audience.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Symbolic Environment Limitations:** The symbolic environment is a simplification of real-world language, which may limit the generalizability of the findings.
    *   **Model Size:** The model is of moderate size.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field by:

    *   Providing a better understanding of how LLMs learn implicit reasoning.
    *   Informing the development of more interpretable and controllable LLMs.
    *   Inspiring new research directions in the area of mechanistic interpretability.

*   **Overall:** The paper makes a solid contribution to the growing field of LLM interpretability. The controlled experiments and novel analysis techniques provide new insights into how Transformers learn implicit reasoning.

### ARC: Argument Representation and Coverage Analysis for Zero-Shot Long Document Summarization with Instruction Following LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23654v1)
- **Authors**: Mohamed Elaraby, Diane Litman
- **Abstract**: Integrating structured information has long improved the quality of abstractive summarization, particularly in retaining salient content. In this work, we focus on a specific form of structure: argument roles, which are crucial for summarizing documents in high-stakes domains such as law. We investigate whether instruction-tuned large language models (LLMs) adequately preserve this information. To this end, we introduce Argument Representation Coverage (ARC), a framework for measuring how well LLM-generated summaries capture salient arguments. Using ARC, we analyze summaries produced by three open-weight LLMs in two domains where argument roles are central: long legal opinions and scientific articles. Our results show that while LLMs cover salient argument roles to some extent, critical information is often omitted in generated summaries, particularly when arguments are sparsely distributed throughout the input. Further, we use ARC to uncover behavioral patterns -- specifically, how the positional bias of LLM context windows and role-specific preferences impact the coverage of key arguments in generated summaries, emphasizing the need for more argument-aware summarization strategies.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary**

The paper "ARC: Argument Representation and Coverage Analysis for Zero-Shot Long Document Summarization with Instruction Following LLMs" introduces a new framework, ARC (Argument Representation Coverage), for evaluating how well instruction-tuned large language models (LLMs) preserve salient arguments when summarizing long documents.  ARC measures coverage at three levels of granularity: Argument Set Coverage (overall coverage), Independent Role Coverage (coverage per argument role), and Subatomic Coverage (fine-grained factual unit coverage within roles).  The authors use ARC to analyze summaries produced by open-weight LLMs on long legal opinions and scientific articles, revealing that while LLMs cover salient arguments to some extent, they often omit critical information, especially when arguments are sparsely distributed.  They also uncover positional and role-specific biases affecting argument coverage.

**Critical Evaluation**

*Novelty:*

The paper's novelty lies primarily in the *framework* itself (ARC) and its *application* to analyze LLM summarization of argumentatively structured documents. While existing work has looked at LLM summarization faithfulness and the preservation of salient content, ARC provides a specific lens focused on argumentative roles as a structured form of saliency. The multi-granularity approach is also novel, offering a more nuanced evaluation than simple binary metrics. The analysis of positional and role-specific biases contributing to information loss is also a valuable contribution. The application to legal and scientific domains, while not entirely new, adds to a growing body of research emphasizing the importance of domain-specific evaluation.

*Significance:*

The paper addresses a crucial problem: the reliability of LLMs in high-stakes domains where preserving argumentation structure is vital. The findings—that LLMs often omit critical information and exhibit positional/role-specific biases—highlight significant limitations of current LLMs for applications requiring faithful and complete summaries. ARC provides a valuable tool for researchers and practitioners to evaluate and improve LLM summarization in these domains. By identifying specific biases, the authors point toward potential areas for future research, such as developing more argument-aware summarization strategies. The work highlights that beyond standard summarization metrics like fluency and coherence, evaluation of argument coverage is crucial for reliable summarization, particularly in domains where structured arguments are important.

*Strengths:*

*   **Well-defined framework:** ARC is clearly defined and provides a structured approach to evaluating argument coverage. The multi-granularity levels offer a good balance between overall assessment and detailed analysis.
*   **Insightful analysis:** The paper presents a comprehensive analysis of LLM behavior, uncovering important positional and role-specific biases.
*   **Practical implications:** The findings have practical implications for improving LLM summarization in high-stakes domains and for developing more robust evaluation metrics.
*   **Reproducible methods:** The authors clearly describe their experimental setup, making the work reasonably reproducible.
*   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and the key concepts are explained clearly, with good use of examples.

*Weaknesses:*

*   **Limited datasets:** The evaluation is limited by the availability of suitable datasets with argument role annotations. The size of the DRI dataset is small and could limit generalizability.
*   **LLM-based decomposition and evaluation:** Reliance on LLMs for decomposing arguments into atomic facts and for evaluating coverage introduces potential inaccuracies and biases. While the authors mitigate this by training classifiers and correlating against human judgments, this aspect remains a source of potential concern.
*   **Lack of a direct comparison to baselines:** While the paper builds upon prior work, it doesn't directly compare ARC to other existing summarization evaluation metrics specifically tailored for argument coverage (if any such metrics exist). A comparison would further highlight the advantages of the ARC framework.
*   **Limited model scope:** The evaluation is limited to specific open-weight LLMs due to computational constraints. Evaluating a wider range of models, including larger closed-source models, would strengthen the findings.

*Potential Influence:*

The paper has the potential to influence the field by:

*   **Promoting more rigorous evaluation of LLM summarization.** ARC provides a template for evaluating the preservation of specific structural elements in summaries, beyond simple text matching.
*   **Motivating research on argument-aware summarization techniques.** The identified biases could lead to the development of new summarization methods that explicitly model argument roles.
*   **Highlighting the need for more specialized datasets for evaluating LLM summarization in structured domains.**
*   **Informing the development of LLM-based tools for high-stakes domains,** ensuring that these tools are reliable and don't inadvertently omit or distort critical information.

**Justification for Score**

The paper makes a solid contribution by introducing the ARC framework and applying it to analyze LLM summarization. The analysis is insightful, and the findings have practical implications. However, the limitations related to datasets and reliance on LLM-based decomposition and evaluation slightly temper the impact. The research fills a gap in the current evaluation landscape, prompting researchers to move beyond standard summarization metrics. However, the study needs better generalization.

- **Classification**: cs.CL

### Keyed Chaotic Tensor Transformations for Secure And Attributable Neural Inference
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23655v1)
- **Authors**: Peter David Fagan
- **Abstract**: This work introduces a novel framework for secure and privacy-preserving neural network inference based on keyed chaotic dynamical transformations. The proposed method applies a deterministic, cryptographically seeded chaotic system to tensors, producing non-invertible, user-specific transformations that enable authenticated inference, tensor-level watermarking, and data attribution. This framework offers a scalable and lightweight alternative to conventional cryptographic techniques, and establishes a new direction for tensor-level security in AI systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Keyed Chaotic Tensor Transformations for Secure and Attributable Neural Inference":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel method for secure and privacy-preserving neural network inference using keyed chaotic dynamical transformations. The approach applies a deterministic, cryptographically seeded chaotic system to tensors, creating non-invertible, user-specific transformations. These transformations aim to enable authenticated inference, tensor-level watermarking, and data attribution. The authors claim this framework is a lightweight and scalable alternative to traditional cryptographic techniques for securing AI systems.  The method involves sampling graph topologies based on a cryptographic key, selecting a dynamical update rule, and then characterizing the chaotic behavior of the resulting system. Applications such as key-authenticated neural network inference, watermarking for model & user attribution, data privacy, and secure data contribution to shared models are discussed.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The core idea of using keyed chaotic systems for tensor transformations in a security context is somewhat novel. While chaotic systems have been used in machine learning (e.g., reservoir computing), employing them specifically as a security primitive for privacy-preserving inference is a less explored area. The concept of deterministically generating these systems from cryptographic keys to achieve user-specific transformations and attribution is also a contribution.

**Significance:** The potential significance of this work lies in addressing a practical need for lightweight and scalable security mechanisms in ML inference. Traditional cryptographic approaches like homomorphic encryption can be computationally expensive, limiting their applicability.  A method that offers a reasonable trade-off between security, computational overhead, and compatibility with existing models would be valuable. The potential for watermarking and attributing usage is also a positive aspect that could help in controlling model abuse.

**Strengths:**

*   **Lightweight and Scalable Potential:**  The approach is presented as a potential alternative to heavyweight cryptographic methods, which could make it more practical for real-world applications.  The fact that it's designed to be compatible with existing model architectures is a plus, as it avoids the need for retraining or significant model modifications.
*   **Multiple Security Objectives:** The framework attempts to address multiple security goals (privacy, attribution, watermarking) with a single mechanism.
*   **Clear Conceptual Framework:** The paper outlines a relatively clear procedure for constructing and applying the chaotic transformations.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Lack of Empirical Validation:** The most significant weakness is the absence of experimental results. The authors acknowledge that "empirical validation and formal security analysis are planned for revisions." Without these, it's impossible to assess the practical performance, scalability, or security guarantees of the method. Claims about lightweightness and scalability are purely speculative at this point.
*   **Unclear Security Guarantees:** The paper doesn't provide a rigorous security analysis. While it mentions potential resistance to inversion and chosen-input attacks, it doesn't quantify the security level or provide any proofs. The claim that reverse engineering the transformation is "computationally impractical" requires more concrete justification. The vulnerability to attacks relying on known plaintext (original and transformed tensors) needs to be explored.
*   **Sensitivity to Parameter Selection:** Chaotic systems are known to be sensitive to parameter settings. The paper acknowledges this but doesn't elaborate on how to ensure consistently chaotic behavior or how to prevent trivial or periodic dynamics. If the chaotic system is not properly configured, the security properties could be significantly weakened.
*   **Practical Considerations:** The process of sampling a graph, choosing update rules, and characterizing chaotic behavior based on a key can be resource intensive. How fast/expensive is the key-based transformation? Can this be easily added to an existing pipeline with ease?

**Justification of Score:**

Given the lack of empirical results and formal security analysis, the paper presents an interesting idea with potential but currently unsubstantiated claims. The absence of experimental validation makes it difficult to assess the practical value and security guarantees of the proposed framework. The novelty of the approach warrants recognition, but the lack of concrete evidence limits its current impact.

### VideoREPA: Learning Physics for Video Generation through Relational Alignment with Foundation Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23656v1)
- **Authors**: Xiangdong Zhang, Jiaqi Liao, Shaofeng Zhang, Fanqing Meng, Xiangpeng Wan, Junchi Yan, Yu Cheng
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements in text-to-video (T2V) diffusion models have enabled high-fidelity and realistic video synthesis. However, current T2V models often struggle to generate physically plausible content due to their limited inherent ability to accurately understand physics. We found that while the representations within T2V models possess some capacity for physics understanding, they lag significantly behind those from recent video self-supervised learning methods. To this end, we propose a novel framework called VideoREPA, which distills physics understanding capability from video understanding foundation models into T2V models by aligning token-level relations. This closes the physics understanding gap and enable more physics-plausible generation. Specifically, we introduce the Token Relation Distillation (TRD) loss, leveraging spatio-temporal alignment to provide soft guidance suitable for finetuning powerful pre-trained T2V models, a critical departure from prior representation alignment (REPA) methods. To our knowledge, VideoREPA is the first REPA method designed for finetuning T2V models and specifically for injecting physical knowledge. Empirical evaluations show that VideoREPA substantially enhances the physics commonsense of baseline method, CogVideoX, achieving significant improvement on relevant benchmarks and demonstrating a strong capacity for generating videos consistent with intuitive physics. More video results are available at https://videorepa.github.io/.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces VideoREPA, a novel framework designed to enhance the physics plausibility of videos generated by text-to-video (T2V) diffusion models. The authors observed that current T2V models often struggle to create physically realistic content due to limitations in their inherent physics understanding. VideoREPA addresses this by distilling physics understanding from video foundation models (VFMs) into T2V models through token-level relational alignment.  Specifically, the paper proposes a Token Relation Distillation (TRD) loss that leverages spatio-temporal alignment to guide the fine-tuning of pre-trained T2V models. Experimental results show that VideoREPA improves the physics commonsense of the baseline CogVideoX model and achieves significant improvements on physics-related benchmarks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper's primary novelty lies in the application of representation alignment (REPA) techniques, specifically TRD, to the domain of text-to-video generation, with a focus on improving physics plausibility.  While REPA has been used in image diffusion models, adapting it to video presents significant challenges due to the added complexity of temporal dynamics and the need to maintain stability during fine-tuning. The paper adequately addresses these challenges by introducing a TRD loss tailored for video.
    However, the idea of using self-supervised learned representations from VFMs to improve generation is not entirely new. Prior work has explored using visual encoders to improve generation quality.  The specific focus on *physics* and the *relational* alignment is a valuable contribution, but the core idea builds upon existing representation learning strategies.
    The Token Relation Distillation (TRD) loss is reasonably novel in its design to capture spatio-temporal relationships. It provides a specific approach to soft knowledge transfer, moving beyond direct feature alignment. The paper makes a convincing argument as to why direct feature alignment, as in standard REPA, is unsuitable for fine-tuning T2V models for physics knowledge.

*   **Significance:** The problem of generating physically plausible videos is an important one. Current T2V models often create videos that violate basic physics principles, hindering their adoption in applications that require realistic simulations or interactions. VideoREPA offers a promising approach to address this limitation by injecting physics knowledge into T2V models. The quantitative results on VideoPhy and VideoPhy2 demonstrate a significant improvement in physical commonsense compared to the baseline and other existing models (including WISA which focuses on physics). The qualitative examples also provide convincing evidence of improved physical realism. The paper makes a compelling argument about the importance of physics plausibility in video generation, the shortcomings of current T2V models, and how their method is addressing the issue.
    The impact could be substantial if this approach can be generalized and scaled to more complex video generation scenarios.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-designed method (TRD loss) that addresses the specific challenges of video.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation with quantitative and qualitative results.
    *   Ablation studies that validate the effectiveness of the proposed components.
    *   The analysis of why direct REPA is unsuitable is well articulated and valuable.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   While TRD attempts to improve physical plausibility, it doesn't explicitly model or reason about physics.  It's learning relational representations that correlate with physical laws but lacks explicit physical constraints.
    *   The method relies on the availability of good video foundation models (VFMs). The performance is limited by the quality of the VFM used for knowledge distillation.
    *   The discussion of computational costs associated with processing video inputs for VFMs is somewhat brief.
    *   The paper mentions limitations in validating the approach for pre-training VDMs, but doesn't offer suggestions about future approaches to alleviate computational challenges.
*   **Potential Influence:**  VideoREPA could influence future research in T2V generation by:

    *   Highlighting the importance of physics plausibility as a key evaluation metric.
    *   Encouraging the development of new methods for injecting knowledge from VFMs into T2V models.
    *   Inspiring further research into representation alignment techniques specifically tailored for video.
    *   Showing the effectiveness of token level relational alignment for fine-tuning T2V models.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:**

VideoREPA makes a solid contribution to the field by specifically addressing the issue of physical plausibility in text-to-video generation, and by proposing a token-level relational alignment (TRD) loss. It takes a principled approach by distilling knowledge from VFMs. The experiments are well-designed, and the results demonstrate significant improvements over existing methods. The analysis of why standard REPA does not work well is also valuable.
However, the novelty is incremental, building on existing representation alignment and transfer learning ideas. The technique is also inherently limited by the reliance on the quality and availability of VFMs. The method does not explicitly model or reason about physics, but improves plausibility by learning relevant relational representations. All these aspects contribute to assigning the paper a good, but not outstanding, score.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Active Layer-Contrastive Decoding Reduces Hallucination in Large Language Model Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23657v1)
- **Authors**: Hongxiang Zhang, Hao Chen, Tianyi Zhang, Muhao Chen
- **Abstract**: Recent decoding methods improve the factuality of large language models~(LLMs) by refining how the next token is selected during generation. These methods typically operate at the token level, leveraging internal representations to suppress superficial patterns. Nevertheless, LLMs remain prone to hallucinations, especially over longer contexts. In this paper, we propose Active Layer-Contrastive Decoding (ActLCD), a novel decoding strategy that actively decides when to apply contrasting layers during generation. By casting decoding as a sequential decision-making problem, ActLCD employs a reinforcement learning policy guided by a reward-aware classifier to optimize factuality beyond the token level. Our experiments demonstrate that ActLCD surpasses state-of-the-art methods across five benchmarks, showcasing its effectiveness in mitigating hallucinations in diverse generation scenarios.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Active Layer-Contrastive Decoding Reduces Hallucination in Large Language Model Generation."

**Summary:**

This paper introduces Active Layer-Contrastive Decoding (ActLCD), a novel decoding strategy to reduce hallucinations in large language models (LLMs). Unlike previous methods like DoLa, which apply layer contrasting statically at each token generation step, ActLCD dynamically decides when to apply layer contrasting using a reinforcement learning policy. This policy is trained to maximize cumulative reward, guided by a reward-aware classifier to optimize factuality beyond the token level. The authors demonstrate ActLCD's effectiveness on five benchmarks, including TruthfulQA, LongFact, StrategyQA, GSM8k, and a domain-specific software package hallucination task, showing improvements over state-of-the-art methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty of the paper lies in its dynamic and adaptive approach to layer contrasting. Existing methods apply contrasting in a static or uniform manner, which the authors argue can lead to "overthinking" for simple token predictions and can exacerbate early errors through hallucination snowballing. By framing decoding as a sequential decision-making problem and employing reinforcement learning, ActLCD offers a more nuanced and context-aware approach. However, the application of reinforcement learning to decoding is not entirely new, and the specific contribution hinges on the combination with layer contrasting and the design of the reward function.

*   **Significance:** Hallucination is a major problem in LLMs, limiting their reliability and trustworthiness. The results presented in the paper demonstrate that ActLCD can significantly reduce hallucinations across a diverse set of benchmarks and LLMs, which is a valuable contribution. The method does not require external knowledge sources or parameter updates, making it efficient and practical for real-world applications.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper provides compelling evidence of ActLCD's effectiveness across multiple benchmarks and models.
    *   **Adaptive Approach:** The dynamic nature of ActLCD addresses limitations of static layer contrasting methods.
    *   **Practicality:** ActLCD is efficient and doesn't require external resources or fine-tuning.
    *   **Detailed Analysis:** The authors provide a good analysis of why ActLCD works and how it differs from existing methods.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Complexity:** The approach is more complex than methods like DoLa, requiring a RL agent. This can create more engineering burden.
    *   **Reward Function Sensitivity:** The performance of ActLCD is likely sensitive to the design of the reward function, which requires careful tuning.
    *   **Offline RL Limitations:** The reliance on offline RL means the policy is limited by the data used to train it, potentially hindering exploration and adaptation to unseen scenarios.
    *   **Lack of Theoretical Justification:** While the paper presents empirical results, it lacks a deep theoretical justification for why the proposed approach is effective. This makes it harder to generalize from the reported experiments.

**Overall Assessment:**

While the paper has some limitations, the novelty and significance of the approach, coupled with the strong empirical results, warrant a positive evaluation. The idea of dynamically controlling layer contrasting offers a promising direction for reducing hallucinations. However, the complexity, potential sensitivity to reward function design, and the reliance on offline RL should be taken into account. Future research should focus on addressing these limitations, providing deeper theoretical insights, and exploring alternative methods for optimizing the trade-off between factuality and decoding efficiency.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### D-AR: Diffusion via Autoregressive Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23660v1)
- **Authors**: Ziteng Gao, Mike Zheng Shou
- **Abstract**: This paper presents Diffusion via Autoregressive models (D-AR), a new paradigm recasting the image diffusion process as a vanilla autoregressive procedure in the standard next-token-prediction fashion. We start by designing the tokenizer that converts images into sequences of discrete tokens, where tokens in different positions can be decoded into different diffusion denoising steps in the pixel space. Thanks to the diffusion properties, these tokens naturally follow a coarse-to-fine order, which directly lends itself to autoregressive modeling. Therefore, we apply standard next-token prediction on these tokens, without modifying any underlying designs (either causal masks or training/inference strategies), and such sequential autoregressive token generation directly mirrors the diffusion procedure in image space. That is, once the autoregressive model generates an increment of tokens, we can directly decode these tokens into the corresponding diffusion denoising step in the streaming manner. Our pipeline naturally reveals several intriguing properties, for example, it supports consistent previews when generating only a subset of tokens and enables zero-shot layout-controlled synthesis. On the standard ImageNet benchmark, our method achieves 2.09 FID using a 775M Llama backbone with 256 discrete tokens. We hope our work can inspire future research on unified autoregressive architectures of visual synthesis, especially with large language models. Code and models will be available at https://github.com/showlab/D-AR
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the D-AR paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces D-AR (Diffusion via Autoregressive Models), a novel approach to image generation that bridges diffusion models and autoregressive models. D-AR casts the image diffusion process as a standard next-token prediction task using a specially designed "sequential diffusion tokenizer." This tokenizer converts images into sequences of discrete tokens, where each token corresponds to a diffusion denoising step.  The key idea is that these tokens inherently follow a coarse-to-fine order suitable for autoregressive modeling.  A standard next-token prediction autoregressive model (like Llama) is then applied to these tokens, mirroring the image diffusion process. The paper highlights several advantages of D-AR, including fast inference through KV caching, consistent previews during partial token generation, and the potential for zero-shot layout control. Experiments on ImageNet show competitive FID scores compared to existing methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of reframing diffusion as an autoregressive task is moderately novel. While previous works have attempted to combine diffusion and autoregressive models, D-AR's approach of using a sequential tokenizer to directly map diffusion steps to discrete tokens and leveraging standard next-token prediction is a distinguishing factor. The "sequential diffusion tokenizer" is perhaps the most original contribution. The novelty is also in showing how to apply an existing LLM like Llama to image generation *without* modifying its inherent structure. This differs from other approaches that require specific architectures for vision.

*   **Significance:**  The significance lies in its potential to unify image generation with the increasingly powerful capabilities of large language models (LLMs). By framing image generation as a sequence prediction problem, D-AR allows LLMs to be directly applied to visual tasks. It also provides a way to unify multimodal tasks within a single architecture. The identified benefits – fast inference, consistent previews, zero-shot layout control – are all practically relevant and valuable in real-world applications.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Simplicity:** The approach is conceptually relatively straightforward, building upon existing components (LLMs and diffusion models). This simplicity allows for easier implementation and experimentation.
    *   **Leveraging LLMs:**  The method directly leverages the advancements in LLMs, potentially benefiting from future scaling and improvements in LLM architectures.
    *   **Efficiency:** Claimed advantages of KV cache-friendly inference and potential for streaming decoding are significant.
    *   **Competitive results:**  The ImageNet results, although not state-of-the-art overall, are competitive, demonstrating the viability of the approach, particularly given the moderate model size.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Tokenizer Complexity:** The sequential diffusion tokenizer is a critical component and adds complexity to the pipeline. The results are dependent on the quality of this tokenizer. It also adds additional training requirements.
    *   **Dependency on Diffusion:** The method's success relies on the inherent properties of diffusion models. While it bridges the gap, it is fundamentally tied to the diffusion process.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Layout Control:** The paper mentions zero-shot layout control, but a more detailed investigation of this capability is warranted.
    *   **Scalability:** While the method theoretically benefits from LLM scaling, the paper doesn't demonstrate results at very large model scales. The potential bottlenecks and limitations of this approach when applied to models with billions of parameters are unexplored.
    *   **FID results:** The reliance of FID score on ImageNet does not capture other aspects of generated image quality, such as photorealism. It would be interesting to see user preference testing (e.g., AB testing against other generative methods).

*   **Potential Influence:** D-AR could influence future research in unified autoregressive architectures for visual and multimodal synthesis. It provides a pathway for leveraging LLMs for image generation and could inspire new tokenization strategies for visual data.

**Rationale for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and potentially significant approach to image generation. The strengths lie in its simplicity, efficient streaming inference, the fact that it leverages existing LLMs, and competitive quantitative results. However, the reliance on a specific tokenizer and limited exploration of the method's potential at very large scales are weaknesses. It is still an early-stage research and it remains to be seen how the scalability issues are resolved, and whether or not D-AR would achieve SoTA performance with increased compute and model size.

### OpenUni: A Simple Baseline for Unified Multimodal Understanding and Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23661v1)
- **Authors**: Size Wu, Zhonghua Wu, Zerui Gong, Qingyi Tao, Sheng Jin, Qinyue Li, Wei Li, Chen Change Loy
- **Abstract**: In this report, we present OpenUni, a simple, lightweight, and fully open-source baseline for unifying multimodal understanding and generation. Inspired by prevailing practices in unified model learning, we adopt an efficient training strategy that minimizes the training complexity and overhead by bridging the off-the-shelf multimodal large language models (LLMs) and diffusion models through a set of learnable queries and a light-weight transformer-based connector. With a minimalist choice of architecture, we demonstrate that OpenUni can: 1) generate high-quality and instruction-aligned images, and 2) achieve exceptional performance on standard benchmarks such as GenEval, DPG- Bench, and WISE, with only 1.1B and 3.1B activated parameters. To support open research and community advancement, we release all model weights, training code, and our curated training datasets (including 23M image-text pairs) at https://github.com/wusize/OpenUni.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the OpenUni paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces OpenUni, a simple, lightweight, and open-source baseline for unifying multimodal understanding and generation. OpenUni bridges pre-trained multimodal large language models (MLLMs) and diffusion models using learnable queries and a lightweight transformer-based connector.  The authors demonstrate that OpenUni can generate high-quality, instruction-aligned images and achieves strong performance on benchmarks like GenEval, DPG-Bench, and WISE with a relatively small number of activated parameters (1.1B-3.1B). The authors release model weights, training code, and curated datasets, including 23M image-text pairs, to promote open research.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Simplicity and Openness:** The paper's primary strength lies in its emphasis on simplicity and accessibility.  By providing a clear, minimalist architecture and fully open-source resources, OpenUni lowers the barrier to entry for researchers interested in unified multimodal models.  This is a valuable contribution to the field.
*   **Efficiency:** OpenUni demonstrates strong performance with a relatively small number of parameters. This efficiency is crucial for wider adoption and deployment, particularly for researchers with limited computational resources. The comparison to BLIP3-0 and other models is compelling in this regard.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper evaluates OpenUni across a diverse set of tasks and benchmarks, including image generation (GenEval, DPG-Bench, WISE) and multimodal understanding (MMBench, MMMU, MME-P).  This provides a comprehensive picture of OpenUni's capabilities and limitations.
*   **Reproducibility:**  The release of model weights, code, and datasets is a significant contribution that enhances reproducibility and allows other researchers to build upon OpenUni.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Incremental Novelty:** The architecture of OpenUni is not groundbreaking. It largely follows the design choices presented in MetaQuery [31], leveraging learnable queries and a lightweight connector between MLLMs and diffusion models.  The novelty is primarily in demonstrating that a minimalist approach can achieve strong performance and in providing an open-source implementation.  The core architectural components have been explored in prior work.
*   **Reliance on Pre-trained Models:** The performance of OpenUni is heavily dependent on the pre-trained MLLM (InternVL3). While leveraging pre-trained models is a common and practical approach, it also means that OpenUni's capabilities are limited by the strengths and weaknesses of the underlying MLLM.  It's more of an adapter than a fundamentally new architecture.
*   **Limited Novelty in Training Strategy:** The two-stage training recipe (pre-training on 23M image-text pairs followed by fine-tuning on 60k high-quality images) is a relatively standard practice in multimodal learning. There is no in-depth innovation in the approach taken here.
*   **Text Rendering limitations:** The paper acknowledges the model's struggle to render texts in generated images. This is a notable limitation for practical applications requiring the generation of images with legible text.

**Significance:**

OpenUni's significance stems from its practical utility and the accessibility it provides to the research community. While the architectural novelty may be limited, the open-source release and competitive performance make it a valuable baseline for future research on unified multimodal models. It provides a readily available platform for experimentation and comparison. It democratizes research in this field.

**Justification for Score:**

While OpenUni is a well-executed and practically useful contribution, its incremental novelty limits its overall score. The reliance on existing architectural components and training strategies, albeit refined and combined effectively, prevents it from being a groundbreaking innovation. It doesn't fundamentally change the direction of the field. However, its emphasis on simplicity, efficiency, and open-source availability makes it a significant enabler for future research.

### ToolHaystack: Stress-Testing Tool-Augmented Language Models in Realistic Long-Term Interactions
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23662v1)
- **Authors**: Beong-woo Kwak, Minju Kim, Dongha Lim, Hyungjoo Chae, Dongjin Kang, Sunghwan Kim, Dongil Yang, Jinyoung Yeo
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated strong capabilities in using external tools to address user inquiries. However, most existing evaluations assume tool use in short contexts, offering limited insight into model behavior during realistic long-term interactions. To fill this gap, we introduce ToolHaystack, a benchmark for testing the tool use capabilities in long-term interactions. Each test instance in ToolHaystack includes multiple tasks execution contexts and realistic noise within a continuous conversation, enabling assessment of how well models maintain context and handle various disruptions. By applying this benchmark to 14 state-of-the-art LLMs, we find that while current models perform well in standard multi-turn settings, they often significantly struggle in ToolHaystack, highlighting critical gaps in their long-term robustness not revealed by previous tool benchmarks.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary, critical evaluation, and score for the TOOLHAYSTACK paper.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TOOLHAYSTACK, a novel benchmark designed to rigorously evaluate the long-term interaction capabilities of tool-augmented language models (TALMs). Unlike existing benchmarks that focus on short, single-turn interactions, TOOLHAYSTACK simulates realistic, multi-session conversations featuring complexities like evolving user goals, semantic noise, and fragmented context. The benchmark is structured around three core challenges: context recall, information shift, and missing context, each with two difficulty levels. The authors evaluate 14 state-of-the-art LLMs on TOOLHAYSTACK and find that while these models perform well in standard multi-turn settings, they often struggle significantly in the proposed benchmark, highlighting key deficiencies in their long-term robustness. Controlled ablation studies are conducted to further analyze failure modes and the impact of factors like distractor tasks. The paper emphasizes the limitations of existing multi-turn benchmarks in accurately reflecting the complexity of real-world, long-term tool use.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the explicit focus on long-term interaction robustness. While multi-turn benchmarks exist, TOOLHAYSTACK stands out for its structured approach to simulate complexities found in real-world conversations, specifically the inclusion of evolving goals, semantic noise, and fragmented contexts. The "needle-in-a-haystack" approach to contextual noise is also a valuable contribution. This moves beyond the simple concatenation of turns and API responses.
*   **Significance:** The paper's findings are significant because they reveal a critical gap in the capabilities of current TALMs. The demonstrated performance degradation of even highly capable models on TOOLHAYSTACK suggests that existing benchmarks may overestimate their real-world readiness. This is particularly important as LLMs become increasingly integrated into applications requiring sustained interaction and context awareness, like personal assistants.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Well-Defined Benchmark:** TOOLHAYSTACK is clearly defined, with well-motivated scenarios and a controllable difficulty structure.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The evaluation of 14 models, including both open-source and proprietary ones, provides a broad perspective on the current state of the field.
    *   **Detailed Analysis:** The ablation studies and error analysis offer valuable insights into the specific failure modes of TALMs in long-term interactions.
    *   **Emphasis on Realistic Scenarios:** The focus on realistic scenarios with natural language interaction patterns provides the benchmark with high ecological validity.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **API Selection:** While the paper emphasizes the use of realistic APIs, it acknowledges the challenge of manually constructing a large number of high-quality APIs. The findings might be limited by the specifics of the chosen API set. This is an area of potential improvement and could affect the generalizability of the findings.
    *   **Limited Number of Sessions:** The paper admits limitations regarding the number of sessions in each interaction. While multi-turn, these sessions could be extended further to truly capture how these systems function after a longer term use.
    *   **CoT limitations:** While the experiments testing chain-of-thought reasoning are well-performed, they demonstrate that its beneficial qualities aren't universal. This could limit the findings as, while informative, they aren't widely applicable due to the specific conditions which allow it to shine.
    *   **Reliance on LLMs for Data Generation and Validation:** The reliance on LLMs (GPT-40) for data generation, scenario creation, and some validation steps introduces a potential bias. Although human review is included, a more rigorous, human-centric validation process could strengthen the benchmark. LLMs have a tendency to create data biased by their own training.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence future research in the field by highlighting the importance of long-term interaction robustness. It can encourage the development of new evaluation methods and model architectures that address the identified limitations. This benchmark will likely become a standard in the future for long-term evaluations.

*   **Rigorous Rationale:** The scoring is based on: a) its novel emphasis in a field that is rapidly expanding; b) the significance of it's results, which point to key limitations in the current state-of-the-art technology; c) the detailed methodology that has the potential to spur further research in evaluation methodologies; and d) the relatively small weaknesses that mostly have to do with the nature of the experiments (difficulty in constructing new APIs, or the number of turns, both of which can be expanded upon in the future). This leaves room for improvement to the experiment in the future that should allow the paper to score even higher.

### LoLA: Low-Rank Linear Attention With Sparse Caching
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23666v1)
- **Authors**: Luke McDermott, Robert W. Heath Jr., Rahul Parhi
- **Abstract**: Transformer-based large language models suffer from quadratic complexity at inference on long sequences. Linear attention methods are efficient alternatives, however, they fail to provide an accurate approximation of softmax attention. By additionally incorporating sliding window attention into each linear attention head, this gap can be closed for short context-length tasks. Unfortunately, these approaches cannot recall important information from long contexts due to "memory collisions". In this paper , we propose LoLA: Low-rank Linear Attention with sparse caching. LoLA separately stores additional key-value pairs that would otherwise interfere with past associative memories. Moreover, LoLA further closes the gap between linear attention models and transformers by distributing past key-value pairs into three forms of memory: (i) recent pairs in a local sliding window; (ii) difficult-to-memorize pairs in a sparse, global cache; and (iii) generic pairs in the recurrent hidden state of linear attention. As an inference-only strategy, LoLA enables pass-key retrieval on up to 8K context lengths on needle-in-a-haystack tasks from RULER. It boosts the accuracy of the base subquadratic model from 0.6% to 97.4% at 4K context lengths, with a 4.6x smaller cache than that of Llama-3.1 8B. LoLA demonstrates strong performance on zero-shot commonsense reasoning tasks among 1B and 8B parameter subquadratic models. Finally, LoLA is an extremely lightweight approach: Nearly all of our results can be reproduced on a single consumer GPU.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "LoLA: Low-Rank Linear Attention with Sparse Caching," an inference-only strategy designed to improve the performance of linear attention models on long sequences.  LoLA addresses the limitations of linear attention (which suffers from memory collisions, hindering accurate associative recall) by distributing historical tokens into three forms of memory: a sliding window for recent tokens, a sparse global cache for difficult-to-memorize tokens, and a recurrent hidden state for generic tokens. LoLA employs a self-recall check to determine which key-value (KV) pairs should be cached, effectively mitigating memory collisions with a constant-size cache. The paper demonstrates that LoLA significantly improves associative recall, enhances language modeling performance on zero-shot commonsense reasoning tasks, and can be efficiently implemented on consumer GPUs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The combination of linear attention with sparse caching is a significant step forward, addressing a well-known problem of information retention in linear attention models. Prior work like LoLCATs used sliding window attention, but LoLA's sparse caching mechanism is a novel and more effective way to deal with long-range dependencies. The self-recall check to determine which KV pairs to cache is also a clever and practical innovation. The idea of memory collision mitigation through a caching layer is not completely new, but the application to the specific context of linear attention and the proposed solution are novel.

*   **Significance:** The paper provides compelling evidence that LoLA significantly boosts the performance of linear attention models on long-context tasks like pass-key retrieval. The fact that LoLA can achieve a 97.4% accuracy on the S-NIAH task at 4K context lengths, with a substantially smaller cache than Llama-3.1 8B, is a major achievement. Furthermore, LoLA's improvements on zero-shot commonsense reasoning tasks indicate its potential to enhance language modeling capabilities beyond specific recall tasks. The lightweight nature of LoLA, reproducible on a single consumer GPU, significantly enhances its accessibility and usability for a broad range of researchers and practitioners.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Strong empirical results on both synthetic recall tasks (RULER benchmark) and real-world language modeling tasks (LM Evaluation Harness).
    *   Clear and well-motivated problem statement.
    *   Well-explained methodology and design choices.
    *   Demonstration of efficient implementation on consumer GPUs.
    *   Ablation studies providing insights into the effectiveness of the different components of LoLA.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The ablation study primarily focuses on the scoring function and caching mechanisms. Further analysis on the relationship of various parameters, specifically chunk size and cache size (with a more in-depth exploration on memory collisions for high-complexity tasks), could be valuable.
    *   The evaluation is largely focused on inference-only, implying that LoLA requires pre-trained LoLCATs weights. More insight on how to train from scratch and if training LoLA from scratch produces the same performance as adding it onto LoLCATs would provide additional understanding on the efficiency of LoLA overall.

*   **Impact:** LoLA has the potential to significantly impact the field of efficient language modeling. It offers a practical solution for improving the performance of linear attention models on long sequences without requiring extensive retraining or specialized hardware. LoLA can potentially serve as a foundational framework that enables future research focused on improved sparse caching mechanisms and integration of low-rank linear approximations. However, LoLA does rely on pre-trained LoLCATs weights to achieve its strong results, meaning that it is less of a direct plug-and-play strategy and more of an additive strategy that requires existing models.

**Justification:**

The contribution is significant due to the substantial empirical improvements demonstrated, the clarity of the approach, and the practical implications of the method. The relatively lightweight nature and efficient deployment adds to its appeal.  The design is well-motivated and the paper thoroughly investigates the core principles. However, as it currently stands, the impact may be somewhat limited due to LoLA's need for the model to be distilled through LoLCATs. There is room for further development to extend LoLA's capabilities (potentially through more efficient models) and alleviate this limitation.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Fortune: Formula-Driven Reinforcement Learning for Symbolic Table Reasoning in Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23667v1)
- **Authors**: Lang Cao, Jingxian Xu, Hanbing Liu, Jinyu Wang, Mengyu Zhou, Haoyu Dong, Shi Han, Dongmei Zhang
- **Abstract**: Tables are a fundamental structure for organizing and analyzing data, making effective table understanding a critical capability for intelligent systems. While large language models (LMs) demonstrate strong general reasoning abilities, they continue to struggle with accurate numerical or symbolic reasoning over tabular data, especially in complex scenarios. Spreadsheet formulas provide a powerful and expressive medium for representing executable symbolic operations, encoding rich reasoning patterns that remain largely underutilized. In this paper, we propose Formula Tuning (Fortune), a reinforcement learning (RL) framework that trains LMs to generate executable spreadsheet formulas for question answering over general tabular data. Formula Tuning reduces the reliance on supervised formula annotations by using binary answer correctness as a reward signal, guiding the model to learn formula derivation through reasoning. We provide a theoretical analysis of its advantages and demonstrate its effectiveness through extensive experiments on seven table reasoning benchmarks. Formula Tuning substantially enhances LM performance, particularly on multi-step numerical and symbolic reasoning tasks, enabling a 7B model to outperform O1 on table understanding. This highlights the potential of formula-driven RL to advance symbolic table reasoning in LMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "FORTUNE: Formula-Driven Reinforcement Learning for Symbolic Table Reasoning in Language Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces FORTUNE, a reinforcement learning (RL) framework designed to train language models (LMs) to perform symbolic table reasoning by generating executable spreadsheet formulas. The key idea is to use the correctness of the formula's execution result as a reward signal, rather than relying on supervised formula annotations. This enables the LM to learn formula derivation through reasoning.  The authors theoretically analyze the advantages of this approach compared to textual reasoning and supervised fine-tuning (SFT), and empirically demonstrate its effectiveness on seven table reasoning benchmarks. The results show that FORTUNE significantly improves LM performance, especially on multi-step numerical and symbolic reasoning tasks. The authors also show that initializing RL with SFT enhances performance, and that combining textual and symbolic reasoning components leads to state-of-the-art results.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the application of RL, specifically a reward structure based on formula execution correctness, to train LMs for generating spreadsheet formulas for table reasoning. While program synthesis and RL for LMs are not entirely new, the specific combination targeted at spreadsheet formula generation and the focus on table reasoning contributes incremental novelty. The theoretical analysis comparing symbolic and textual reasoning, and SFT versus RL provides a solid foundation for the method.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its potential to improve the accuracy and robustness of LMs in handling numerical and symbolic reasoning over tabular data, which has been a persistent challenge. Spreadsheet formulas offer a powerful and flexible medium for representing executable symbolic operations. By enabling LMs to leverage them, FORTUNE opens up possibilities for more reliable and transparent decision-making based on tabular data. The empirical results are convincing and demonstrate substantial performance gains, enabling relatively smaller models to rival much larger ones. The improved performance on OOD benchmarks suggests better generalization capabilities.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The extensive experiments on seven diverse benchmarks provide robust evidence for the effectiveness of FORTUNE.
    *   **Theoretical Justification:** The theoretical analysis provides a clear understanding of the advantages of the proposed approach.
    *   **Clear Methodology:** The paper clearly describes the methodology and implementation details, making it easy to reproduce.
    *   **Practical Relevance:** The problem addressed is practically relevant, and the proposed solution has the potential to be applied in real-world applications.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Datasets and Experimental Coverage:** Although the experiments are extensive, the experiments focused only a subset of the all the model and dataset.
    *   **Exploration Challenges:** RL from scratch can be highly unstable. Curating independent and high-quality cold-start corpora and identifying optimal initialization checkpoints for reinforcement learning remain open challenges.
    *   **Reward Shaping Could Be Improved:**. The current reward signal is based solely on binary execution accuracy. a more fine-grained reward shaping improves both learning stability and the quality of generated formulas

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field of table reasoning and LM training by demonstrating the effectiveness of formula-driven RL. It could inspire further research on developing more sophisticated RL algorithms for symbolic reasoning and on exploring the use of spreadsheet formulas as a general-purpose representation for executable knowledge. The work also highlights the importance of combining textual and symbolic reasoning components in LMs.

*   **Rigorous Rationale:**  The paper provides a well-defined problem space, strong theoretical motivation, a clear methodology, and comprehensive experimental validation. The limitations are also discussed openly, providing direction for future research. The performance improvements shown on multiple benchmarks, particularly the out-of-distribution generalization, justify a relatively high score.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:**  The paper presents a novel and effective approach to symbolic table reasoning using formula-driven RL. The empirical results are strong, and the theoretical analysis provides a solid foundation for the method. The paper's limitations are acknowledged, and the potential influence on the field is significant. The innovation is not entirely groundbreaking, keeping it from scoring much higher but the contribution is clearly valuable and well-executed.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### ImmunoDiff: A Diffusion Model for Immunotherapy Response Prediction in Lung Cancer
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23675v1)
- **Authors**: Moinak Bhattacharya, Judy Huang, Amna F. Sher, Gagandeep Singh, Chao Chen, Prateek Prasanna
- **Abstract**: Accurately predicting immunotherapy response in Non-Small Cell Lung Cancer (NSCLC) remains a critical unmet need. Existing radiomics and deep learning-based predictive models rely primarily on pre-treatment imaging to predict categorical response outcomes, limiting their ability to capture the complex morphological and textural transformations induced by immunotherapy. This study introduces ImmunoDiff, an anatomy-aware diffusion model designed to synthesize post-treatment CT scans from baseline imaging while incorporating clinically relevant constraints. The proposed framework integrates anatomical priors, specifically lobar and vascular structures, to enhance fidelity in CT synthesis. Additionally, we introduce a novel cbi-Adapter, a conditioning module that ensures pairwise-consistent multimodal integration of imaging and clinical data embeddings, to refine the generative process. Additionally, a clinical variable conditioning mechanism is introduced, leveraging demographic data, blood-based biomarkers, and PD-L1 expression to refine the generative process. Evaluations on an in-house NSCLC cohort treated with immune checkpoint inhibitors demonstrate a 21.24% improvement in balanced accuracy for response prediction and a 0.03 increase in c-index for survival prediction. Code will be released soon.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "ImmunoDiff: A Diffusion Model for Immunotherapy Response Prediction in Lung Cancer":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ImmunoDiff, a novel diffusion model aimed at predicting immunotherapy response in Non-Small Cell Lung Cancer (NSCLC).  ImmunoDiff synthesizes post-treatment CT scans from baseline imaging by incorporating clinically relevant constraints, anatomical priors (lobar and vascular structures), and a cbi-Adapter. The cbi-Adapter ensures consistent multimodal integration of imaging and clinical data (demographics, biomarkers, PD-L1 expression).  The model is trained in two stages: anatomy-guided training on public datasets and clinical variable-guided response prediction on an in-house NSCLC cohort. The authors claim improved balanced accuracy in response prediction and c-index in survival prediction compared to existing methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a relatively novel approach by employing a diffusion model specifically tailored to immunotherapy response prediction in NSCLC. The integration of anatomical priors via lobe and vascular segmentation and the use of a cbi-Adapter for multimodal data fusion represents a unique contribution. Previous studies have used diffusion models in medical imaging, but not quite with this specific focus, architectural choices, and clinical integration scheme for this particular clinical task. The clinical variable conditioning mechanism, using a range of factors is also interesting.

*   **Significance:** Accurate prediction of immunotherapy response is a critical unmet need in NSCLC management. If ImmunoDiff consistently demonstrates the claimed improvements in accuracy and survival prediction, it could have a significant impact on clinical decision-making. However, the reliance on an in-house dataset is a major limiting factor (see below).

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Addresses an important clinical problem.
    *   Presents a well-structured and technically sound approach based on diffusion models.
    *   The use of anatomical priors and the cbi-Adapter appears promising for enhancing the realism and clinical relevance of the synthesized CT scans.
    *   Reports quantitative improvements over baseline methods.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Dataset:** The evaluation is primarily based on a relatively small, in-house NSCLC cohort (n=74 for response prediction and survival). This raises serious concerns about the generalizability of the findings. A single-center study may not accurately reflect the heterogeneity of patient populations and imaging protocols across different institutions. External validation on independent datasets is crucial.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** While ablation studies are performed, more detail regarding the individual impact of each clinical variable on the overall performance would strengthen the claims. For instance, how did accuracy change when age/sex was not used in variable guidance?
    *   **Baseline Comparison:** While the authors compare to various baseline methods, including conventional CNNs and Transformers, it would be more compelling to compare against other state-of-the-art radiomics approaches that specifically focus on longitudinal data (e.g., methods that explicitly model changes in tumor characteristics over time). This would provide a more relevant benchmark for assessing the added value of the diffusion-based synthesis approach.
    *   **Clinical Impact:** The potential clinical impact is contingent on the generalizability of the model, which needs to be demonstrated. The paper only alludes to this potential impact without concrete evidence.
    *   **Figure quality issues:** Some figures in the submission are of very low quality.

*   **Potential Influence:** If validated on larger, multi-center datasets, ImmunoDiff could influence the development of more accurate and personalized predictive models for immunotherapy response in NSCLC. It could also provide a framework for integrating imaging and clinical data in other medical domains.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the weaknesses described above, the score cannot be higher than a 6. While the approach is novel and potentially significant, the limited and single-center validation dataset casts a substantial shadow over the reliability of the reported improvements. Furthermore, more direct comparison against state-of-the-art longitudinal radiomics approaches is warranted to truly demonstrate the value of the proposed diffusion-based synthesis. The figure quality issues also detract from the overall presentation. The paper needs substantial improvements in validation and broader comparison with existing techniques to warrant a higher score.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: eess.IV
- **Score**: 6/10

### Learning Compositional Functions with Transformers from Easy-to-Hard Data
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23683v1)
- **Authors**: Zixuan Wang, Eshaan Nichani, Alberto Bietti, Alex Damian, Daniel Hsu, Jason D. Lee, Denny Wu
- **Abstract**: Transformer-based language models have demonstrated impressive capabilities across a range of complex reasoning tasks. Prior theoretical work exploring the expressive power of transformers has shown that they can efficiently perform multi-step reasoning tasks involving parallelizable computations. However, the learnability of such constructions, particularly the conditions on the data distribution that enable efficient learning via gradient-based optimization, remains an open question. Towards answering this question, in this work we study the learnability of the $k$-fold composition task, which requires computing an interleaved composition of $k$ input permutations and $k$ hidden permutations, and can be expressed by a transformer with $O(\log k)$ layers. On the negative front, we prove a Statistical Query (SQ) lower bound showing that any SQ learner that makes only polynomially-many queries to an SQ oracle for the $k$-fold composition task distribution must have sample size exponential in $k$, thus establishing a statistical-computational gap. On the other hand, we show that this function class can be efficiently learned, with runtime and sample complexity polynomial in $k$, by gradient descent on an $O(\log k)$-depth transformer via two different curriculum learning strategies: one in which data consists of $k'$-fold composition functions with $k' \le k$ presented in increasing difficulty, and another in which all such data is presented simultaneously. Our work sheds light on the necessity and sufficiency of having both easy and hard examples in the data distribution for transformers to learn complex compositional tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper studies the learnability of compositional functions by transformers, focusing on the k-fold composition task. This task requires learning an interleaved composition of k permutations and can be expressed by a transformer with O(log k) layers. The authors demonstrate a statistical-computational gap by proving an exponential lower bound in k for Statistical Query (SQ) learners. Conversely, they show that gradient descent on an O(log k)-depth transformer can efficiently learn the task, with polynomial runtime and sample complexity in k, using curriculum learning strategies (increasing difficulty or simultaneous presentation of data). This highlights the importance of easy-to-hard data for training transformers on complex compositional tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its theoretical analysis of the learnability of compositional functions in transformers *under specific data distributions* and *task structures*. Previous work has focused on the expressivity of transformers or empirical demonstrations, but this paper offers a formal analysis of the statistical and computational trade-offs involved in *learning* such functions. The exploration of curriculum learning as a remedy to statistical-computational gaps in transformer learning is valuable. The k-fold composition task seems to be new, or at least formalized in a novel way in this work.

*   **Significance:**
    *   **Strength:** The results shed light on why transformers, despite their expressive power, sometimes struggle with compositional reasoning.
